Welcome — How to Use This JavaScript Guide

How this guide is organisedThe right mindsetTools you will use all course long

This guide is a self-contained, offline course that takes you from JavaScript’s very first value up through async, classes, and error handling. Work through the chapters in order, and type every example yourself to build muscle memory. No internet is required, since all reference material is included in the guide itself.

This is a complete, self-paced path through modern JavaScript and ES6+, from primitives to fetch, classes, and functional patterns. It all lives in a single HTML file — no internet required, progress is saved in your browser.

How this guide is organised

Shows how the guide is organized into three parts spanning twelve chapters plus exercises. Every chapter ends with copy-ready code that you should type out by hand rather than pasting. This structure moves you from core values and syntax toward async, classes, and debugging.

  • Part I (Ch. 1–5) — foundations: values, functions, objects, the DOM, and modern syntax.
  • Part II (Ch. 6–8) — moving data: modules, promises, and fetch.
  • Part III (Ch. 9–12) — advanced: classes, collections, functional patterns, and debugging.
  • Every chapter ends with exercises. Code is copy-ready; type it rather than pasting to build muscle memory.

The right mindset

Explains how to think in JavaScript by putting values first, then learning the handful of core ideas that everything else builds on. When you master the core concepts once, advanced topics become pattern matching. Practice recognizing which value a piece of code produces at each step.

JavaScript rewards a small core of deep ideas: values and types, functions, and the object model. Almost every advanced topic is a combination of these. Learn them properly and the framework code of tomorrow stops surprising you.

Keep the browser console open while you study. This guide explains the why and the sequence; the console is your playground and laboratory.

Tools you will use all course long

Lists the tools you will use throughout the course, including Node.js, browser developer tools, and the console. Install them once and they carry you through every chapter. Run scripts with node, and log values with console.log to inspect what your code produces.

ToolPurpose
Node.jsRun JavaScript outside the browser (Ch. 6–8)
Browser devtoolsElements, console, and network panel (Ch. 4, 7, 8)
console.log()Your primary debugging instrument
npmInstall packages and run scripts (Ch. 6)

1 · Values, Types & Operators

1.1 Primitives and object types1.2 typeof and coercion1.3 Equality and falsy values1.4 Logical and nullish operators1.5 Template literals

Establishes the value system JavaScript is built on: primitives, objects, coercion, and the operators that combine them. Everything later in the guide leans on this chapter. In practice you ask typeof for a value’s kind, observe how operators coerce mismatched types, and compare values to control flow.

A JavaScript program is just a stream of values and the operations you perform on them. Get comfortable with the types and the two equality operators here, and the hardest bugs of your future disappear.

1.1 Primitives and object types

JavaScript has seven primitive types and one non-primitive. Every variable holds one or a reference to one, and the distinction between the primitives and objects explains a large share of the language's behaviour — including why a string is not an array and why an array is not an object.

The seven, and what each holds

TypetypeofHoldsFalsy
undefined"undefined"Nothing. The absence of a valueYes
null"object"Explicitly nothing. The absence of an objectYes
boolean"boolean"true or falsefalse
number"number"Every numeric value. One type for integers and floats0, -0, NaN
bigint"bigint"Arbitrary-precision integers0n
string"string"UTF-16 code units. Immutable""
symbol"symbol"A unique reference, used as a property keyNo
object"object"A reference to a value in the heapAll objects are truthy
typeof null is "object". This is a bug from the first JavaScript engine in 1995, kept for backwards compatibility because so much existing code depended on it. It has been proposed for removal and never will be, because the web would break. Use value === null to test for it, and never typeof value === "object" alone.

The primitives that look like objects

Strings and numbers let you call methods on them, which appears to contradict "primitives are not objects". What happens is that the property access boxes the value into a wrapper object, calls the method, and throws the wrapper away. "abc".length is new String("abc").length, minus the allocation.

// boxing happens on every property access, invisibly
"abc".length;          // 3
(42).toFixed(2);       // "42.00"
true.toString();       // "true"

// which is why these are all ReferenceErrors, not type errors
// there is no autoboxing for properties you ASSIGN to a primitive
str.length = 10;       // TypeError in strict mode, ignored in sloppy
num.foo = 1;           // same

// so primitives are immutable, and you cannot add properties to them
"use strict";
str.length = 10;       // TypeError: Cannot assign to read only property
// the wrapper objects exist, but you never need them
new String("abc") === "abc";   // false! different objects
new Number(42) === 42;         // false
Object("abc") == "abc";        // true, via coercion - but === is false

// which is the canonical demonstration that objects are compared
// by identity and primitives by value
const a = { x: 1 };
const b = { x: 1 };
a === b;              // false - two references to two objects
a.x === b.x;          // true  - 1 === 1

const s1 = "abc", s2 = "abc";
s1 === s2;            // true  - primitives compare by value
const o1 = {}, o2 = {};
o1 === o2;            // false - objects compare by reference

Numbers, and the three that surprise

ValueWhat it isGotcha
0.1 + 0.20.30000000000000004Binary floating point. Not a bug in the arithmetic
Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER9007199254740991Above it, adding 1 changes nothing
NaNNot a numberNaN === NaN is false
InfinityOverflowFrom 1/0, or from an unrepresentable result
-0Negative zero-0 === 0 is true, but Object.is(-0, 0) is false
// money is the classic casualty of binary floating point
0.1 + 0.2 === 0.3;              // false
0.1 + 0.2;                      // 0.30000000000000004

// three fixes, in order of preference
(0.1 + 0.2).toFixed(2);         // "0.30" - display only
Math.abs(a - b) < 1e-9;          // compare with a tolerance
1234.50;                         // integers in cents - the real fix

// NaN needs Object.is, because === is defined to be false
NaN === NaN;                     // false
Object.is(NaN, NaN);             // true
Number.isNaN(NaN);               // true
Number.isNaN("abc");             // false - unlike the global isNaN
isNaN("abc");                    // true  - it coerces first, and lies

// and -0 needs Object.is too
Object.is(-0, 0);                // false
Object.is(-0, -0);               // true
Use Number.isNaN(), never the global isNaN(). The global one coerces its argument first, so isNaN("abc") is true — the string was coerced to a number, which then was not a number. The gap is that the answer is about the coercion, not about your value.

Seven primitives plus objects; typeof null is a permanent lie, and strings box invisibly on every property access. Objects compare by reference and primitives by value, which is the distinction behind most equality surprises. For money, store integers in the smallest unit.

1.2 typeof and coercion

typeof is the only operator in JavaScript that does not coerce its operand to a number, and that exception is why it is useful and why it is also wrong about two cases. Every other operator converts its operands, so typeof is the only type test that does not have the answer already changed before it looks.

What it returns, and the two that are wrong

OperandtypeofCorrect?
undefined"undefined"Yes
null"object"No. A 1995 bug, kept forever
true"boolean"Yes
42"number"Yes
10n"bigint"Yes
"s""string"Yes
Symbol()"symbol"Yes
() => {}"function"Yes, and functions are objects
[], {}, new Date()"object"Technically true, and nearly useless
new Number(1)"object"Wrong in spirit — it is a number
document.all"undefined"A deliberate second bug, for legacy compatibility
// typeof does NOT coerce, which is its whole purpose
typeof "42";            // "string"  - not coerced to a number first
typeof (42 + "");       // "string"  - and the addition DID coerce

typeof;                 // "undefined" - the identifier was never declared
typeof undeclaredVar;   // "undefined" - no ReferenceError. This is why
                        // typeof is safe on undeclared identifiers

42 + "";                // "42"  - the + is what coerced, not typeof
typeof 42 + "";         // "number"

// but arithmetic DOES coerce
"10" + 5;               // "105"  - string wins with +
"10" - 5;               // 5     - number wins with -
"10" * "5";             // 50    - * coerces both to numbers
"abc" * 2;              // NaN

Coercion, and where it happens

OperationConverts operands toWhy
+Strings, if either operand is a string; otherwise numbersThe one exception, and the source of most surprises
- * / % **NumbersThere is no string arithmetic, so all coerce to number
< > <= >=NumbersRelational comparison is numeric
== !=Depends on both types — see the next sectionThe Abstract Equality Comparison algorithm
=== !==NothingType and value, with no conversion
The + operator on a stringStrings"1" + 1 is "11"
if, while, &&, ||To booleanTruthiness, not coercion to a number
// ToNumber, on the strings that matter
Number("");        // 0
Number("  42  ");  // 42  - leading and trailing whitespace is ignored
Number("42px");    // NaN
Number("0x1f");    // 31  - hex, octal and binary literals are accepted
Number("1e3");     // 1000
Number("Infinity") // Infinity
Number(null);      // 0
Number(undefined); // NaN
Number([]);        // 0   - "" converts to 0
Number([5]);       // 5   - "5" converts to 5
Number([1,2]);     // NaN - "1,2" is not a number
Number({});        // NaN
Number(true);      // 1

// ToString, on the values that matter
String(null);      // "null"    NOT "undefined"
String(undefined); // "undefined"
String([1,2]);     // "1,2"     Array.prototype.toString joins with commas
String({});        // "[object Object]"

// which is why [] + {} is "[object Object]", and a famous
// interview question with an entirely explainable answer
[] + {};           // "[object Object]"  - "" + "[object Object]"
[] + [];           // ""               - "" + "" then... no, see below
({}) + [];         // "[object Object]"
Array-to-string conversion joins with commas and produces no brackets, which is why [] + [] is "" and [1] + [2] is "12". It is a string, not the number 12, and it is the reason so much code writes Number(a) + Number(b) where it meant addition.

The tools for the cases typeof cannot answer

QuestionAnswerCost
Is it undefined?x === undefinedFree
Is it nullish?x == null, or x ?? dFree
Is it a plain object?x !== null && typeof x === "object" && !Array.isArray(x)Free
Is it an array?Array.isArray(x)Free, and correct across realms
Is it a function?typeof x === "function"Free
Is it a Date?x instanceof DateFails across realms; use Object.prototype.toString
Is it a Promise?x instanceof PromiseFails across realms
Anything, exactly?Object.prototype.toString.call(x)A string allocation. Not for hot paths
// instanceof fails across realms, which is a real and common case:
// an iframe's Array is a different constructor from this one's
const other = iframe.contentWindow;
other.Array === Array;                              // false
[] instanceof other.Array;                          // false - wrong answer
other.Array.isArray([]);                            // true  - correct

// so Array.isArray is the right test, and it has been since ES5
Array.isArray([]);          // true
Array.isArray(new Proxy([], {}));   // true

// Object.prototype.toString for the cases with no dedicated check
Object.prototype.toString.call(new Date());    // "[object Date]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(null);          // "[object Null]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(undefined);     // "[object Undefined]"
Object.prototype.toString.call([]);            // "[object Array]"
Object.prototype.toString.call(function(){});   // "[object Function]"
// and Symbol.toStringTag can override it, which is a feature:
class MyThing { get [Symbol.toStringTag](){ return "MyThing"; } }
Object.prototype.toString.call(new MyThing());  // "[object MyThing]"

// the pattern that reads well in application code
function isPlainObject(v){
  if (v === null || typeof v !== "object") return false;
  const proto = Object.getPrototypeOf(v);
  return proto === Object.prototype || proto === null;
}
A useful asymmetry: typeof is guaranteed not to throw, even for an undeclared identifier, so it is the one type check that is safe before you know a binding exists. Every other test either throws a ReferenceError or requires the value to already be a value.

typeof is wrong about null and unhelpful about arrays and class instances. Use Array.isArray rather than instanceof so it survives an iframe, and Object.prototype.toString.call for the rest. typeof on an undeclared identifier is the one type test that cannot throw.

One consequence worth stating separately: because typeof is the only non-coercing operator, it is also the only type test that cannot throw. A function parameter you have not yet received a value for is undefined and typeof reports it safely; a bare read of the identifier would be a ReferenceError only if the binding itself does not exist, which is a different failure with a different fix.

typeof is the only non-coercing operator, and it is wrong about null by a 1995 bug. + is the only operator that prefers strings; every other arithmetic operator converts to number. Know ToNumber and ToString for null, [] and {} and most coercion surprises become predictable.

1.3 Equality and falsy values

JavaScript has two equality operators: one that compares values and one that compares values and types. The strict one is the correct one, and the loose one exists for legacy coercion. The loose one has a documented algorithm, and knowing it is the difference between guessing and predicting.

The Abstract Equality Comparison algorithm

When the two operands are of the same type, == behaves exactly like ===. Everything interesting happens when they differ.

  1. If both are null, or both are undefined, they are equal.
  2. If one is null and the other undefined, they are not equal.
  3. If one is a number and one a string, the string is converted to a number, then compared.
  4. If one is a boolean, it is converted to a number, then compared.
  5. If one is an object and one a primitive, the object is converted to a primitive, then compared.
  6. If both are objects, they are equal only if they are the same object.
// the consequences of those six steps
null == undefined;      // true  - step 1
null == 0;              // false - step 2
undefined == 0;         // false - step 2

0 == "";                // true  - step 3, "" -> 0
0 == "0";               // true  - step 3
0 == false;             // true  - step 4, false -> 0
"" == false;            // true
null == false;          // false - null never converts
undefined == false;     // false

[] == "";               // true  - step 5, [] -> "" then step 3
[] == 0;                // true  - [] -> "" -> 0
[0] == false;           // true  - [0] -> "0" -> 0
[1] == true;            // true  - [1] -> "1" -> 1

{} == "[object Object]";// true  - step 5
{} == 0;                // false - "[object Object]" -> NaN
NaN == NaN;             // false - step 6, and NaN !== NaN
({}) == {};             // false - two different objects
({ a: 1 }) == ({ a: 1 }); // false - and no coercion, because both are
                         // objects, so step 5 never runs
WrittenResultWhy
== nullTrue for null and undefinedSteps 1 and 2. This is the one legitimate use of loose equality
== ""Also true for 0 and falseSteps 3 and 4. Almost never intended
== undefinedIdentical to === undefinedNo case where null equals it
=== nullOnly nullCorrect
=== undefinedOnly undefinedCorrect

Falsy values, in full

// there are exactly eight falsy values
false;      // boolean
0;          // number
-0;         // number
0n;         // bigint
"";         // string, including "" and no other string
null;       // object, by accident
undefined;  // undefined
NaN;        // number, and NaN is the only number that is falsy

// everything else is truthy, including the ones that look falsy
Boolean({});            // true
Boolean([]);            // true
Boolean("false");       // true  - a non-empty string
Boolean("0");           // true
Boolean(document.all);  // false - the one deliberate exception

// and 0n is falsy while 0n == 0 is true, because === does not
// coerce across bigint and number
0n == 0;                 // true
0n === 0;                // false - different types
A non-empty string is always truthy, including "false" and "0". This is why if (input.value) is wrong for a form field — the string "0" passes the check. Test the value, not the truthiness of a string that came from a form.
// the tests that are actually right
value == null;                  // null or undefined, deliberately
value === null;                 // only null
value === undefined;            // only undefined
typeof value === "undefined";   // works even for undeclared identifiers

// and for numbers, test the number
if (Number.isFinite(count) && count > 0){ }   // rejects NaN and Infinity
if (count){ }                    // rejects 0 - which may be valid
if (typeof count === "number"){ } // does NOT reject NaN

// and for strings, check the length
if (name){ }                     // "" is falsy, so this works
if (name.length){ }              // equivalent, and clearer about why

// Object.is for the two cases === gets wrong
Object.is(NaN, NaN);      // true
Object.is(-0, 0);          // false

Object comparison, which has no coercion at all

// two objects are equal only if they are the same object.
// There is no structural comparison in the language.
({ a: 1 }) === ({ a: 1 });     // false
[1,2] === [1,2];               // false
new String("x") === "x";       // false

// which is why these do not work as intended
if (list.includes({ id: 1 })){ }        // always false
if (config === { retries: 3 }){ }        // always false
if (arr.indexOf({ a: 1 }) !== -1){ }     // always -1

// the three correct approaches
const found = list.some(item => item.id === 1);        // some()
const found2 = list.find(item => item.id === 1);       // and the item
const idx = list.findIndex(item => item.id === 1);     // and the index
const has = new Set(list.map(x => x.id)).has(1);        // for many lookups

// deep equality needs a function, because the language has none
function deepEqual(a, b){
  if (Object.is(a, b)) return true;
  if (typeof a !== "object" || typeof b !== "object"
      || a === null || b === null) return false;
  const ka = Object.keys(a), kb = Object.keys(b);
  if (ka.length !== kb.length) return false;
  return ka.every(k => deepEqual(a[k], b[k]));
}
// and note it is recursive, so it is not safe on a cyclic structure
// and it does not know about Map, Set, Date or a class's prototype

// which is what structuredClone plus comparison is for, and why
// the testing libraries ship their own
expect(built).toEqual(expected);   // structural, with asymmetric matchers
expect(a).toBe(b);                // reference identity, same as ===
// the traps in the eight falsy values, in the wild
function bad1(x){ return x || "default"; }        // 0 -> "default"
function bad2(x){ return x && "ok"; }             // 0 -> 0, not "ok"
function bad3(x){ return x ?? "default"; }         // correct
function bad4(x){ return x ? "yes" : "no"; }       // "" -> "no"

// for a numeric option, ?? is right and || is a bug
const page = Number(input.value) || 1;    // typing 0 gives page 1
const page2 = Number(input.value) ?? 1;   // 0 stays 0

// for a checkbox, the DOM gives a boolean, so neither is needed
const checked = checkbox.checked;          // already boolean

// for a string from a form, truthiness works but is easy to misread
if (name){ }                 // "" is falsy, so this is correct
if (name.length){ }          // clearer about why

// and the one that needs explicit handling
if (0){ }                   // never runs - and 0 may be a valid count
if (count !== 0 && count != null){ }   // says what it means

Objects compare by identity only — [1,2] === [1,2] is false and there is no structural equality in the language, so use some, find or a Set for lookups by value. Use === everywhere except a deliberate x == null, and remember a non-empty string is always truthy.

Use === everywhere except for the one case where value == null is genuinely what you mean — testing for either nullish. There are exactly eight falsy values, a non-empty string is always truthy, and NaN and -0 are the two cases where === and Object.is disagree.

1.4 Logical and nullish operators

Three operators that do not return booleans, and therefore can be chained and can carry values. This is not an oddity to work around — it is how you write a default without an if, and it is the reason && can guard a whole expression.

Logical AND, and what it returns

a && bReturnsReads as
Both truthyb"both"
a falsya"a, so stop"
a truthy, b falsyb"a, then b — and b is the problem"
// it returns a VALUE, not a boolean
0 && "x";            // 0
1 && "x";            // "x"
"" && "x";           // ""
null && "x";         // null

// which makes it a default:
const timeout = options.timeout || 5000;   // no - 0 becomes 5000
const timeout = options.timeout ?? 5000;   // yes - only nullish falls back

// and a guard, which is the most common use:
el && el.focus();          // no error if el is null
el?.focus();               // same, and shorter

// three && in sequence: stop at the first falsy, return it
user && user.address && user.address.city
// null    undefined              "London"
// readable, but the result type is a union of three

// better, when there is more than one step
user?.address?.city ?? "Unknown"

Logical OR, and the default-value trap

// || returns the first truthy value
0 || "default";        // "default"  <-- but 0 was intentional!
"" || "default";      // "default"  <-- same for an empty string
false || "default";   // "default"

"value" || "default" // "value"
null || "default"    // "default"

// ?? returns the first NON-NULLISH value, and only that
0 ?? "default"        // 0        <-- the whole point
"" ?? "default"       // ""
false ?? "default"    // false
null ?? "default"     // "default"
undefined ?? "d"      // "d"

// || on a number is almost always a bug
page = userInput || 1;          // 0 becomes 1
page = userInput ?? 1;          // 0 stays 0

// and for a default that allows any falsy value
const retries = config.retries || 3;    // config.retries = 0 -> 3
const retries = config.retries ?? 3;    // config.retries = 0 -> 0
The one place && and || may not be mixed without parentheses is a real rule, not style. a && b || c is a syntax error: the grammar forbids mixing the two without explicit grouping, precisely because a ?? b || c is almost never what anyone means. Write (a && b) || c and (a ?? b) || c and be explicit about the intent.

Nullish coalescing, and optional chaining

// ?. short-circuits the whole chain, not one link
user?.profile?.address?.city
// undefined if ANY link is null or undefined

// and it also short-circuits the CALL, which matters
user?.notify();          // does not throw if user is null
getUser()?.notify?.();   // same for a possibly-missing method

// ?? is the default that only fires for nullish
const items = opts.items ?? [];        // opts.items = null -> a new array
const items = opts.items || [];        // opts.items = 0 -> a new array

// assignment forms, which are newer than the operators themselves
a ??= b;                // b only evaluated if a is nullish
obj?.prop ??= 1;        // combined with optional chaining

// mixing ?? with && or || needs parentheses - this is a syntax error
// a ?? b || c
// a || b ?? c
(a ?? b) || c           // correct
(a || b) ?? c           // correct
NeedWriteNot
Default when nullisha ?? ba || b — loses 0, "", false
Default when falsya || b—
Guard a whole chaina?.b?.ca && a.b && a.b.c
Optional call or indexa?.b?.(), a?.[0]A manual check
Botha?.b ?? cValid, and the common case

Short-circuit evaluation, which is the real point

Beyond returning values, && and || avoid evaluating the right-hand side. That is what makes them a control-flow tool as much as an operator.

// the right side is not evaluated at all
let calls = 0;
const expensive = () => { calls++; return "value"; };

false && expensive();   // nothing runs
true  || expensive();   // nothing runs
true  && expensive();   // calls === 1
false || expensive();   // calls === 2

// which is the guard, written declaratively
el && el.classList.add("ready");
// if el is null, classList is never touched

// and the reason a guard beats an if statement here
if (el) el.classList.add("ready");     // the same, in two lines
// the && form cannot be broken by a missing semicolon the way
// this classic can:
if (el) el.classList.add("ready")
;[1,2].forEach(() => {})   // ASI hazard: now el.classList.add(...) returns undefined

// optional chaining is the null-safe version and always wins when
// the value can legitimately be nullish
el?.classList?.add("ready");   // no ASI hazard, no throw

Mixing them, and the syntax error that prevents a bug

// this does not parse:
a ?? b || c
a || b ?? c
a && b ?? c

// and it is a syntax error rather than a warning because each
// combination is almost certainly a mistake:
//   a ?? b || c   - almost always meant (a ?? b) || c
//   a || b ?? c   - the ?? would be unreachable given || semantics

// the two that DO work together
(a ?? b) || c
(a || b) ?? c

// a practical example, and the bug it prevents
// wrong: the ?? is unreachable, so a 0 silently becomes c
const limit = settings.limit || defaults.limit ?? 10;
//        ^ 0 falls through, and then ?? never fires

// right: two explicit steps
const limit = settings.limit ?? defaults.limit;
const effective = limit || 10;
// now 0 becoming 10 is visible in the code
// the four operators side by side, on the same inputs
// value:      0    ""   false  "x"   null  undefined
a && b:       0    ""   false  b      null  undefined
a || b:       b    b    b      "x"    b     b
a ?? b:       0    ""   false  "x"    b     b
a ? b : c:    b    b    b      b      c     c

// so the only row where they differ is the nullish one, and
// that is exactly the row ?? exists for

// a real chain, and what each operator contributes
const label = user?.profile?.name ?? (isAnon ? "Guest" : "Unknown");
// ?.^            handles a nullish chain without throwing
// ??            falls back only for null or undefined
// ?:           falls back for every falsy value, including ""

All four operators, and the only one that distinguishes nullish from falsy is ??. Use it for every default, and pair it with ?. to guard a chain. Mixing ?? with && or || without parentheses is a syntax error, because the combination is almost always a mistake.

There is a fourth operator in this family, and it is the one with the newest semantics. The exponentiation operator is right-associative, which no other arithmetic operator is, so the parentheses that would be required elsewhere are forbidden: 2 ** 3 ** 2 is 512, not 64.

2 ** 3 ** 2;        // 512 - right-associative
Math.pow(2, 3 ** 2);  // 64  - the exponent is evaluated first
-(2 ** 2);          // SyntaxError: unary minus with ** needs parens
(-2) ** 2;          // 4

All three return values rather than booleans, which is what makes defaults and guards possible. Use ?? for a default — it fires only for null and undefined, so a legitimate 0 or "" survives. Use ?. to guard a chain, and remember that mixing ?? with && or || requires explicit parentheses.

1.5 Template literals

A template literal is string interpolation with backticks, but it changes four things at once: how quotes and newlines are written, how whitespace is treated, how nested markup is escaped, and how a function can receive the un-interpolated parts. The fourth is what makes small templating and styling libraries possible.

Interpolation, and what it accepts

const name = "Ada";
const greeting = `Hello, ${name}.`;
greeting;   // "Hello, Ada."

// anything is stringified, and nothing throws
`${42}`;          // "42"
`${[1,2]}`;       // "1,2"
`${null}`;       // "null"
`${undefined}`;  // "undefined"
`${{a:1}}`;       // "[object Object]"

// nested expressions, and nesting inside the expression
const row = `<tr><td>${user.name}</td><td>${user.age}</td></tr>`;
const list = `<ul>
  ${items.map(i => `<li>${i}</li>`).join("\n")}
</ul>`;
// note the inner template is escaped too, and that its own
// interpolations must be escaped in THIS file's source, which is
// why a long nested template is easier to build with a helper

Quotes and newlines, which is the real convenience

// no escaping needed for either
const s = `It's "quoted", and
spans two lines`;

// the same text in a single-quoted string
const s2 = 'It\'s "quoted", and\nspans two lines';

// a template preserves whitespace EXACTLY
const a = `line1
line2`;
const b = "line1\nline2";
a === b;      // true

// so the newline after the opening backtick is real, and it is
// the one thing that bites - see the key example below
<!-- a multi-line template inside an HTML sample, with every
     angle bracket escaped and every interpolation escaped -->
<script>
  const tpl = `
    <li class="item">
      <span>${label}</span>
    </li>
  `;
  // the value has a leading newline, two spaces of indent on
  // each line, and a trailing newline
</script>
The leading and trailing newline is the one thing that catches people, because it is invisible in the editor and permanent at runtime. A template literal starting on the line after the backtick produces a string beginning with , and if that string becomes a cache key, a CSS class name or an id, the newline is part of it for ever. Call .trim() when the value is compared rather than displayed.

Escaping, and the trap templates create

// a template literal is NOT markup-aware
const el = document.createElement("p");
el.textContent = `Hello, ${userInput}`;   // safe: a text node
el.innerHTML  = `Hello, ${userInput}`;   // XSS if the input is markup

// so the rule is: textContent, or escape deliberately
const ENT = { "&": "amp", "&lt;": "lt", "&gt;": "gt",
              '"': "quot", "'": "#39" };
const esc = (s) => String(s)
  .replace(/[&<>"']/g, (c) => "&" + ENT[c] + ";");

el.innerHTML = `Hello, ${esc(userInput)}`;

// or build with DOM methods, which cannot be tricked at all
const p = document.createElement("p");
p.append("Hello, ", userInput);   // append makes a text node
Template literals make injection easier, not harder, because they read as string concatenation rather than as markup construction. A template that emits HTML and interpolates anything a reader supplied is a vulnerability, and the syntax gives no hint of it.
// the empty-result and the one-line cases, which need no
// escaping because they emit no markup
const key = `user:${id}`.trim();          // trim for a value
const cls = `btn btn--${variant}`;        // no markup, so no escaping
const hash = `item-${id}`;               // safe as written

Tagged templates, and raw access

// a tag function receives the literal parts and the values
// SEPARATELY - it is not concatenation, and it cannot be
function tag(strings, ...values){
  strings.raw;   // the source text, with escapes unprocessed
  strings[0];    // the cooked text, escapes processed
  values;        // the interpolated expressions, already evaluated
  return "";
}

tag`a${1}b`;
// strings = ["a", "b"], values = [1]

// so a sanitising tag can leave the literals alone and escape
// only the values - a guarantee concatenation cannot give
const SAFE = Symbol("safeHtml");
const trust = (s) => ({ [SAFE]: true, toString: () => String(s) });

function html(strings, ...values){
  return strings.reduce((out, s, i) => {
    const v = values[i];
    let r;
    if (Array.isArray(v)){
      // a NESTED TAG: its own parts were already escaped
      r = v.reduce((a, str, j) => a + str + (v.values[j] ?? ""), "");
    } else if (v && v[SAFE]){
      r = String(v);                 // explicitly trusted
    } else if (v == null){
      r = "";
    } else {
      r = esc(v);                    // everything else
    }
    return out + r + s;
  }, "");
}

html`<p>${userInput}</p>`;        // escaped
html`<p>${trust(bio)}</p>`;         // deliberately not
A nested tag arrives in values as an array, not a string. A sanitising tag that calls String(v) on one will emit the parts joined by commas — visible output, but not the markup. Check Array.isArray(v) before stringifying, and reduce over the array's own values property.
// strings.raw is what a parser or a styler needs
function css(strings, ...values){
  return strings.raw.reduce((acc, s, i) =>
    acc + s + (i < values.length ? values[i] : ""), "");
}
css`color: ${"red"}`;
// strings[0] and strings.raw[0] are both "color: " here, but for
// a \n or a unicode escape they differ, which is why a styler
// wants raw and an escaper wants cooked

A template literal preserves whitespace exactly, so a leading newline is real — call .trim() when the value is compared rather than displayed. It removes all quote and newline escaping, which makes it read as concatenation rather than as markup, so innerHTML with interpolated input is a vulnerability. A tag function receives the literal parts and the values separately, and that separation is what lets it escape one and trust the other.

2 · Functions, Scope & Closures

2.1 Declarations, expressions and arrow functions2.2 Parameters, defaults and rest2.3 The scope chain and hoisting2.4 Closures and IIFEs2.5 Pure functions

Teaches functions as values, the scope rules that decide what they can see, and closures that let them remember variables. Functions are the building block everything else composes. You write them with declarations, expressions, or arrows and pass them around like any other value.

Functions are the heart of JavaScript. This chapter covers how to write them, how scope decides what they can read, and the closure mechanic behind event handlers and callbacks.

2.1 Declarations, expressions and arrow functions

Four ways to write a function, and they differ in three ways that matter: when they are initialised, what this is inside them, and whether they can be used before their definition appears. Those three differences account for most of the bugs where a function "is not defined" or "losed its context".

The four forms

FormInitialisedthisHoisted?New-able?
Function declarationBefore any code runsDynamic — the call site decidesFullyYes
Function expressionWhen the statement runsDynamicNoYes
Arrow functionWhen the statement runsLexical — never its ownNoNo
Method shorthandWhen the object is createdThe objectNoNo
Class methodWhen the class is definedThe instanceNo—
Class field arrowWhen the instance is madeLexicalNo—
// a declaration is initialised before ANY code runs, so this works
say("hi");                    // fine
function say(m){ return m; }

// an expression is initialised in place, so this throws
say("hi");                    // ReferenceError
const say = m => m;

// which is why hoisting works for declarations and not expressions
console.log(typeof later);    // "function"
console.log(later);           // the function itself
function later(){}

// and this is the TDZ, which is a real error rather than undefined
console.log(tdz);             // ReferenceError, not undefined
const tdz = 1;
// let and const are initialised at the top of their block, and
// touching them before that is an error - which is safer than
// var's silent undefined
// this: the decisive difference
const obj = {
  name: "obj",
  regular(){ return this?.name; },        // "obj" when called as obj.regular()
  arrow: () => this?.name,                // whatever this was where the
                                         // object literal was created
};
const detached = obj.regular;
detached();     // undefined - this is undefined in a plain call
obj.regular();  // "obj"

// call(), apply() and bind() change this explicitly
obj.regular.call({ name: "other" });   // "other"
obj.regular.apply({ name: "other" });  // "other"
const bound = obj.regular.bind({ name: "b" });
bound();                               // "b"

// an arrow has no bind, because it has no own this at all
const boundArrow = obj.arrow.bind({});   // does nothing useful
An arrow function has no this of its own, no arguments, and cannot be used with new. That makes it right for a short callback and wrong for a method, an event handler that needs the element, or anything that must be constructible. If you find yourself writing function inside an arrow inside a method, the arrows are probably wrong.
// which one to reach for
function process(list, fn){ }       // needs this, needs new, or is hoisted
const process = (list, fn) => {}; // a callback: no this, no arguments
const obj = { process(){ } };       // a method: needs this
class X { method(){} }              // a class method
class X { field = () => {} }      // a bound field: this is the instance
                                       // and it survives being passed around

// arguments does not exist in an arrow
function f(){
  return Array.from(arguments);   // works
}
const g = () => {
  return arguments;                // ReferenceError, or the outer one
};
// and for the common case, rest parameters are clearer anyway
const f2 = (...args) => args;

Arrows, precisely

PropertyfunctionArrow
thisDynamic, from the callLexical, from where it was written
argumentsYesNo
newYesNo
Prototype propertyHas .prototypeNone
HoistingDeclaration: yes. Expression: noExpression only
Return of an object literalNeeds parenthesesImplicit
Named in a stack traceYesNo — inferred
// the object-literal difference, which bites on line one
function f(){ return { a: 1 }; }     // returns the object
const g = () => ({ a: 1 });       // needs the parentheses

// or use a block body
const h = () => { return { a: 1 }; };

// named, for stack traces and recursion
const fact = function fact(n){ return n <= 1 ? 1 : n * fact(n - 1); };
const fact2 = (n) => n <= 1 ? 1 : n * fact2(n - 1);  // infinite - TDZ
// the arrow version cannot self-reference

// arguments replaced by rest
function f(){ return Array.from(arguments); }
const g2 = (...args) => args;

// a method needs this; an arrow never does
class Counter {
  #n = 0;
  increment = () => { this.#n++; return this.#n; };
  // a class FIELD holding an arrow, so it is bound per instance
  // and survives being passed as a callback:
  const run = this.increment;
  run();          // 1 - this is still the instance

  incrementMethod(){ this.#n++; }
  // a prototype METHOD, unbound: passing it loses the receiver
}
// the decision, in order
// 1. does it need its own this?            -> function
// 2. does it need to be new-able?          -> function
// 3. does it need arguments or a prototype?-> function
// 4. is it a callback with no this?        -> arrow

// the mistake in both directions
const handlers = items.map(item => function(){ this.remove(item); });
// this inside is undefined or the window, depending on strict mode

const handlers2 = items.map(item => () => this.remove(item));
// wrong: every arrow captured the SAME this, from the enclosing scope

const handlers3 = items.map(function(item){
  return () => this.remove(item);       // each function has its own this
});
// correct: an outer function to give each arrow its own receiver

An arrow has lexical this, no arguments, no prototype and cannot be constructed, and returning an object literal needs parentheses. Use it for callbacks; use a function for anything that needs its own receiver, and a class field holding an arrow when a method must survive being passed as a callback.

Choosing, in one list

The whole question reduces to four tests, in order: does it need its own this, does it need new, does it need arguments or a prototype, and is it a callback? The first three yes means a function; otherwise an arrow. The overwhelmingly common case in application code is a callback that needs no receiver, and an arrow is right for it.

An arrow has lexical this, no arguments, no prototype and cannot be constructed, and returning an object literal needs parentheses. Use it for callbacks; use a function for anything needing its own receiver, and a class field holding an arrow when a method must survive being passed as a callback.

Function declarations hoist fully and are usable before their definition; const arrow functions do not, and touching one before its block initialises throws a TDZ error rather than giving undefined. An arrow has lexical this, no arguments, and cannot be used with new — so it suits a callback and not a method.

2.2 Parameters, defaults and rest

Parameters can be given defaults, collected into an array, collected into an object, or none of that. Three rules govern them, and each one has an exception worth knowing: defaults apply on undefined only, rest must be last, and duplicate parameter names are allowed in sloppy mode.

Defaults

function greet(name = "there"){ return Hello, ${name}; }
greet();          // "Hello, there"
greet("Ada");     // "Hello, Ada"
greet(undefined); // "Hello, there" - the ONLY value that triggers it
greet(null);       // "Hello, null"  - null is a value, not an absence
greet(0);         // "Hello, 0"
greet("");        // "Hello, "

// defaults can reference earlier parameters, which is unusual and useful
function padded(str, len = str.length){ return str.padEnd(len); }
padded("ab");     // "ab"
padded("ab", 5);  // "ab   "

// and they evaluate at call time, not at definition time
const DEFAULT_SIZE = 100;
function sized(size = DEFAULT_SIZE){ return size; }
DEFAULT_SIZE = 200;
sized();          // 200 - read when called
A default triggers only on undefined. That is the whole rule, and it is what makes ?? and = different in a function signature — function f(x = d) falls back for undefined and function f(x = d ?? e) adds a second fallback for null. If you want null to trigger the default, either write the ?? or normalise with x ?? d in the body.
// destructured parameters, with defaults in the pattern
function draw({
  x = 0, y = 0,
  width = 100,
  height = width / 2,        // later bindings can use earlier ones
  fill = "#000",
} = {}){
  // the = {} is needed only if the WHOLE argument may be omitted;
  // without it, draw() throws on destructuring undefined
  return { x, y, width, height, fill };
}
draw();                                  // all defaults
draw({ width: 200 });                     // height becomes 100
draw({ width: 200, height: 300 });         // both set

Rest parameters

PropertyDetail
Must be lastNothing can follow it in the parameter list
Always an arrayEven for one argument: f(1) gives [1]
Distinct from argumentsA real array, and it does not see the parameters individually
Does not destructure(...rest) collects; ({ a, ...rest }) destructures
Only in the parameter listNot a declaration — you cannot let ...rest
// collecting arguments
const sum = (...nums) => nums.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0);
sum();          // 0    - not an error, unlike Math.sum()
sum(1, 2, 3);   // 6
sum(1);         // 1

// mixing declared parameters with rest
function log(level, ...messages){
  return [${level}] ${messages.join(" ")};
}
log("info", "a", "b");   // "[info] a b"
log("info");             // "[info] "   - an empty array, no error

// rest on the left-hand side of a destructuring assignment
const [first, ...others] = [1, 2, 3, 4];
// first = 1, others = [2, 3, 4]

const { a: { b }, ...c } = { a: { b: 1 }, d: 2, e: 3 };
// b = 1, c = { d: 2, e: 3 }

// the same syntax in a function signature does the reverse
function pick({ a, ...rest }){ return [a, rest]; }
// and the spread side, which is the reverse operation
const merged = { ...defaults, ...overrides };   // later wins
const combined = [first, ...rest, last];      // spread into an array literal
Math.max(...numbers);                          // spread into a call

// the one place it does NOT work
const s = new Set(spreadable);   // TypeError: not iterable
// Set and Map take an iterable, not a spread argument -
// this is a real mistake that reads as correct

Parameter scope and the TDZ

// parameters live in their own scope, before the body
function f(a, b){
  return a + b;
}
// a and b exist only inside. They are not properties of the
// function, and they do not leak into the enclosing scope.

// and a later parameter can be referenced by an earlier default,
// but not the reverse
function ok(a = 1, b = a + 1){ }    // fine
function bad(a = b + 1, b = 2){ }    // ReferenceError: b is in the TDZ

// destructured parameters get the same treatment
function g({ a, b = a * 2 } = {}){ return a + b; }
g({ a: 3 });    // 9  - b defaults to 6
g();            // NaN - a is undefined, so a + b is NaN

// arguments and parameters coexist, and the mapping is subtle
function h(a){
  arguments[0] = 99;
  return a;      // 99 - they are linked in sloppy mode
}
function strict(a){
  "use strict";
  arguments[0] = 99;
  return a;      // 1 - unlinked in strict mode
}
// ES modules and classes are always strict, so there you get 1
// always. Do not rely on the linked behaviour.
// arguments is not an array, and this matters
function count(){
  return arguments.length;             // works
  // return arguments.map(f);          // TypeError: not a function
  // arguments.forEach(f);             // TypeError
  // [...arguments];                   // works - rest is cleaner
}

// and prefer rest everywhere, because it is real syntax
function count2(...args){ return args.length; }   // args IS an array

// the one case arguments still has: a function that must accept
// a variable number of arguments without naming them, and that
// is not a candidate for rest
function log(){
  const level = arguments[0] || "info";
  return Array.from(arguments).slice(1);
}
Passing an object of named options beats a long positional parameter list past about three parameters, because the call site becomes self-describing and the parameters can be reordered without touching every caller. It also makes the optional ones genuinely optional without a long run of undefined placeholders.
// four positional parameters: the call site tells you nothing
createUser("Ada", "ada@test", "admin", true, "London");

// an options object: the call site is readable
createUser({
  name: "Ada",
  email: "ada@test",
  role: "admin",
  active: true,
  city: "London",     // ...and the 5th is added later without
});                    // touching the first four callers

// with defaults applied in one place
function createUser({
  name,
  email,
  role = "user",
  active = true,
  city = null,
} = {}){
  return { name, email, role, active, city };
}

Defaults fire only on undefined; a later parameter can use an earlier default but not the reverse. arguments is not an array and is unlinked in strict mode — use rest parameters, which are real syntax. Past three parameters, take an options object so the call site is readable and reordering is safe.

Defaults fire on undefined only — never on null, 0 or "". Rest must be last and always produces an array, so f() with only rest gives []. ...spread in a new Set() is an error: the constructor takes an iterable, not a spread argument.

2.3 The scope chain and hoisting

Scope decides where a name can be seen, and it is decided lexically — by where the code is written — not by where a function is called from. Understanding that sentence explains hoisting, closures, this, and most of the "why can it see that" questions in the language.

The chain

ScopeCreated byVisible to
Globalvar and function declarations at the top levelEverything
FunctionA function body, an arrow bodyEverything nested inside it
Block{} — any block, not only functionsEverything in that block
ModuleAn ES module's top levelThat module only
ClosureA function that outlives its enclosing callWhatever it captured
Catchcatch (e)The catch block only
Class bodyA class declaration's { }The class body only
Function parameterThe parameter listThe function body
// lexical, not dynamic: decided by the source text
const x = "global";
function outer(){
  const x = "outer";
  function inner(){
    // it finds the NEAREST x in the SOURCE, which is outer's
    return x;
  }
  return inner();
}
outer();            // "outer"

const call = inner;  // moving the function does not move its scope
call();              // still "outer" - it captured the binding

// shadowing, deliberately
function f(){
  const x = "inner";
  return x;         // "inner"
}
f();                // "inner"

// and the classic gotcha: var in a loop
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));   // 3, 3, 3
}
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));   // 0, 1, 2
}
// var creates ONE binding, shared. let creates a fresh binding per
// iteration. This is the single most useful difference between them.
// the TDZ, which is not "undefined" but an error
{
  console.log(a);   // ReferenceError, not undefined
  let a = 1;
}
{
  console.log(b);   // undefined, not an error
  var b = 1;
}
// let and const are initialised at the top of their block but
// inaccessible until then. var is initialised to undefined at the
// top of its FUNCTION, immediately.

// which is why this is a real difference, not a style preference
let x = "outer";
{
  // let x = "inner";   // SyntaxError: redeclaration
  let y = 1;
}
// and the reverse - the outer name is not visible before the
// shadowing declaration
let z = 1;
{
  console.log(z);     // ReferenceError - z is in the TDZ here,
                      // because the inner z shadows it
  let z = 2;
}
// the module scope, which is the reason modules are safe
// a.mjs
const secret = "not exported";
export const shared = "exported";
// b.mjs
import { shared } from "./a.mjs";
// shared is fine; secret does not exist here at all

// and every module has its own top-level scope, so two modules can
// both declare const state without colliding
// which is why you cannot accidentally create a global by
// forgetting a declaration - unlike a classic script
var is function-scoped and let/const are block-scoped, and the practical consequences are large: var inside an if is visible after it, let inside a for body is not, var shares one binding across loop iterations, and var in a classic script becomes a property of globalThis — which is how libraries have historically polluted the global namespace.

Shadowing, and when it is a bug

// shadowing: an inner name hides an outer one
const config = { port: 3000 };
function handler(config){   // shadows the global config
  return config.port;       // 3000 only if the parameter was passed
}
handler(config);            // 3000
handler({ port: 8080 });    // 8080
handler();                  // TypeError, not a default

// the dangerous version is shadowing by accident
let id = "global";
function load(itemId){
  return itemId ?? id;     // id is unreachable here
}

// and the version that hides an error
let total = 0;
function addToTotal(total){  // now total inside is a number,
  total += 1;               // and the outer one never changes
}

// the check that catches it
function bad(x){
  if (x) { return x; }
  { const x = 0; }          // block-scoped, harmless
  return x;                 // fine
}
function worse(x){
  if (x) { const x = 2; }   // a redeclaration in the same block
  return x;                 // SyntaxError: redeclaration
// module scope is the reason a missing declaration is safe
// classic script:
var leaked = 1;             // window.leaked === 1
function f(){}              // window.f
// so a typo creates a global instead of an error

// module:
const safe = 1;             // no window.safe
function g(){}              // no window.g
undeclared = 1;             // ReferenceError in strict mode
// which is one of the best reasons to use modules even without
// importing anything

// and the globalThis check for what is actually global
Object.keys(globalThis).filter(k => !k.startsWith("_")).length;
// browsers expose a lot; a leak shows up here
// a linter rule that catches most of this
// no-shadow, no-undef, no-redeclare, no-use-before-define
//
// and the TDZ, which is stricter than var on purpose
function f(){
  console.log(x);
  var x = 1;      // undefined - no error
}
function g(){
  console.log(y);
  let y = 1;      // ReferenceError - no silent pass
}
function h(){
  if (true){
    console.log(z);
    var z = 1;    // undefined
    console.log(z);   // 1 - hoisted to the function top
  }
  console.log(z);    // 1 - visible OUTSIDE the block
}
// var leaks out of every block; let does not. That is the
// difference in one example.

Scope is lexical and shadowing is silent, so an inner name can make an outer one unreachable without any error. var is function-scoped and leaks out of blocks; let is block-scoped and throws a TDZ error if touched before its declaration. Module scope gives you strict mode and no accidental globals for free.

One more scope exists that is easy to forget: the scope of a class body. Names declared directly in a class { } — as opposed to inside a method — exist only within that body, and are not properties of the class or of its instances.

class Widget {
  version = "1.0";            // an instance field - per object
  static count = 0;          // a static field - per class
  #secret = 42;              // private - per object, not reachable outside

  method(){
    const local = 1;         // method scope
    // classCount is not visible here unless declared in the class body
  }
}
Widget.count;                 // 1
w.version;                   // "1.0"
w.count;                     // undefined - static is on the constructor
w.#secret;                   // SyntaxError, not undefined

Scope is lexical: a function sees the variables in the source text around it, not the caller's variables. var is function-scoped and shares one binding across loop iterations; let and const are block-scoped and get a fresh binding per iteration, and touching one before its declaration throws rather than giving undefined.

2.4 Closures and IIFEs

A closure is a function together with the variables it captured from the scope it was written in. It is not a feature to be used carefully — every function in the language is a closure, including ones that capture nothing. It is a description of what a function always is.

What is captured, and when

// the scope is captured, not the value
function counter(){
  let n = 0;                    // one binding, created on call
  return {
    inc(){ return ++n; },
    get(){ return n; }
  };
}
const c1 = counter();
const c2 = counter();           // a SEPARATE call, a separate n
c1.inc(); c1.inc();             // c1's n is 2
c1.get();                       // 2
c2.get();                       // 0  - not shared

// the classic loop bug, and its two correct fixes
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));    // 3, 3, 3
}
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));    // 0, 1, 2 - let handles it
}
for (const i of [0, 1, 2]){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));    // 0, 1, 2
}
// the var version needs an explicit capture:
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  (function(j){
    setTimeout(() => console.log(j));
  })(i);
}                                  // 0, 1, 2
ConstructPer-iteration binding?
var in forNo. One binding, shared
let in forYes. A fresh copy each pass
const in for…ofYes, and it cannot be reassigned
for (let i …; ; )Yes, plus a per-iteration copy of anything the body declares with let

IIFEs, and why they mostly are not needed any more

// an IIFE creates a scope and discards it
(function(){
  const private = "hidden";
  init(private);
})();
// classic script: one global scope. IIFE: a private one.

/* the historical reasons:
   1. avoid polluting the global scope        -> block scope now
   2. private variables                       -> block or closure now
   3. async patterns (jQuery's ready)         -> promises, async/await
   4. UMD module wrappers                     -> ES modules now
   5. avoiding the cost of creating a named
      function in an old engine               -> irrelevant */

// the modern replacements
{ const private = "hidden"; }        // block scope, no function
for (const x of items){ }            // per-iteration binding
document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", init);
await import("./thing.mjs");
import { x } from "./thing.mjs";

// the ONE remaining legitimate use
const module = (function(){
  const internal = 1;
  return { get internal(){ return internal; } };
})();
// genuinely private state, with no other way to express it
// without a class or a closure factory
A closure holding a large object keeps it alive for as long as the closure does, which is a genuine memory leak in a long-lived page. An event listener that captures a large node and is never removed keeps that node and everything it references alive. If a handler is registered on something global — window, document, a store — it is a leak until it is removed.
// a memoised function, the pattern closures exist for
function memoize(fn){
  const cache = new Map();
  return function memoized(...args){
    const key = JSON.stringify(args);
    if (cache.has(key)) return cache.get(key);
    const value = fn.apply(this, args);   // this is forwarded
    cache.set(key, value);
    return value;
  };
}
const slow = (n) => n * n;
const fast = memoize(slow);
fast(4);   // computes
fast(4);   // cached
cache.size; // 1, visible in a debugger

// and the leak, made explicit
const handler = () => { hugeThing.doWork(); };
window.addEventListener("resize", handler);
// ...later
window.removeEventListener("resize", handler);   // required
// or, once
window.addEventListener("resize", handler, { once: true });

Closures as the only privacy mechanism

// a closure is the only way to have a truly private variable
function makeCounter(start = 0){
  let n = start;              // not reachable from outside
  return {
    inc: (by = 1) => { n += by; return n; },
    dec: (by = 1) => { n -= by; return n; },
    get value(){ return n; },  // a getter, read-only from outside
  };
}
const c = makeCounter(10);
c.inc();        // 11
c.value;        // 11
c.n;             // undefined - the name does not exist outside
c.value = 99;   // ignored: a getter with no setter
// and it is not on the prototype, so Object.keys(c) shows the
// methods but there is no way to reach n from outside

// the equivalent with a class and a private field
class Counter {
  #n = 0;                      // truly private, checked by the engine
  inc(by = 1){ this.#n += by; return this.#n; }
  get value(){ return this.#n; }
}
// #n is enforced: no way to reach it, at all

// and with a WeakMap, for the older approach and for objects
const privates = new WeakMap();
function priv(obj, key, value){
  if (!privates.has(obj)) privates.set(obj, new Map());
  privates.get(obj).set(key, value);
}
// the memoisation pattern, done properly
function memoize(fn, keyFn = (...a) => JSON.stringify(a)){
  const cache = new Map();
  const wrapped = function(...args){
    const key = keyFn(...args);
    if (cache.has(key)) return cache.get(key);
    const value = fn.apply(this, args);
    cache.set(key, value);
    return value;
  };
  wrapped.cache = cache;        // so it can be cleared in a test
  return wrapped;
}

const parse = memoize(raw => JSON.parse(raw), raw => raw);
// a string key is cheaper than JSON.stringify on an array

// and the bound-function variant, for methods
class Store {
  #state;
  constructor(state){ this.#state = state; }
  get(key){ return this.#state[key]; }
}
const get = Store.prototype.get.bind(store);
// or, better: arrow class fields, which are already bound
class Store2 {
  #state = {};
  get = (key) => this.#state[key];
}
// and this one survives being passed as a callback
Every function is a closure, including ones that capture nothing — the description is accurate for all of them. The practical consequence is the memory one: a closure keeps every variable it references alive, so a listener on a long-lived object that captures a large node is a leak until it is removed. once: true, or an AbortController signal, is the reliable way to bound that.

A closure captures the scope it was written in, not the values at call time, which is why let in a loop fixes the capture bug and var does not. Use a closure for genuinely private state, and remember every function is one — so a closure over a large object keeps that object alive.

Every function is a closure — it captures the scope it was written in, not the values in it at call time. let in a loop creates a fresh binding per iteration while var shares one, which is the whole loop-capture bug. IIFEs are no longer needed for scoping or async patterns; the remaining use is genuinely private state. A closure over a large object keeps it alive, so remove global listeners.

2.5 Pure functions

A pure function does the same thing for the same input and changes nothing outside itself. That is a stricter definition than "returns the same value", and the second half — no external effects — is the part that makes a function testable and the part that is usually violated first.

The two properties

PropertyMeans
DeterministicSame input, same output, always. No Date.now(), no Math.random(), no reading a global
No side effectsNothing outside is changed: no DOM, no network, no mutation of arguments, no console
// impure: reads the world
function total(items){ return items.length * 1.2; }        // a hardcoded rate
function isNew(date){ return Date.now() - date < 864e5; } // depends on now
function pick(list){ return list[Math.floor(Math.random()*list.length)]; }

// pure: everything it needs is an argument
const VAT = 1.2;
function total(items, rate = VAT){ return items.length * rate; }
function isNew(date, now = Date.now()){ return now - date < 864e5; }
function pick(list, index = 0){ return list[index % list.length]; }
// injecting the non-determinism as a parameter with a default
// keeps the production call site unchanged AND makes it testable

// impure: mutates what it was given
function sortNames(users){
  users.sort((a, b) => a.name.localeCompare(b.name));  // mutates
  return users;
}
function sortNames(users){
  return [...users].sort((a, b) => a.name.localeCompare(b.name));
}

// impure: reaches outside
function save(user){ database.write(user); return user; }
function save(user, db = database){ db.write(user); return user; }
A function that mutates its arguments is impure even when the mutation is "only" on a fresh object it created. items.push(x) inside a function changes the caller's array, and a caller that passes the same array twice gets different behaviour. Copy first — [...items], items.slice(), structuredClone(x) for a deep copy.
// the impure-for-testing trap, and the fix
// hard to test: the price is in a module-scoped constant
const RATE = 1.2;
const total = (n) => n * RATE;

// easy to test: the price is a parameter
const total = (n, rate = RATE) => n * rate;
expect(total(10, 1.2)).toBe(12);
expect(total(10, 0)).toBe(0);          // possible only because rate
                                      // is injectable

// the same applies to time, randomness and identity
const now = () => Date.now();
const uid = () => crypto.randomUUID();

// so tests are deterministic and do not depend on when they run
// or on the order they run in

When impurity is correct

FunctionPure?Why
A React or view render functionMust be pureIt may run any number of times, in any order, including twice in development
An event handlerUsually notChanging the DOM is the point
fetch wrapperNot, but keep the pure part pureValidate and build the request purely; do the network separately
A formatterPure, if the locale is a parametertoLocaleDateString without a locale depends on the environment
A reducerMust be pureSame reason as a render function
A getter for derived statePureIt should read and return, never compute and store
// the pattern: a pure core and an impure shell
// pure: everything that decides
function reducer(state, action){
  switch (action.type){
    case "add":    return { ...state, items: [...state.items, action.item] };
    case "remove": return { ...state, items: state.items.filter(i => i.id !== action.id) };
    default:       return state;    // and unknown actions change nothing
  }
}

// impure: everything that touches the world
function dispatch(action){
  store.dispatch(action);            // the effect
  save(store.getState());            // the persistence
}

// which means the reducer is testable with three lines and no
// mocks, and the untestable part is three lines of glue

Testability, which is the practical payoff

// impure: a test needs the module registry, a fake clock and a
// deterministic random source, or it is a flaky test
function greet(){
  return `Hello, ${user.name}, it is ${new Date().toLocaleTimeString()}`;
}

// pure: a test needs nothing
function greet(user, clock = new Date()){
  return `Hello, ${user.name}, it is ${clock.toLocaleTimeString()}`;
}
expect(greet({ name: "Ada" }, new Date("2026-01-01T09:00:00")))
  .toBe("Hello, Ada, it is 09:00:00");

// and for randomness
const pick = (list, r = Math.random) => list[Math.floor(r() * list.length)];
const ctl = () => 0;      // always picks index 0
expect(pick(["a","b","c"], ctl)).toBe("a");
// the test controls the randomness completely
// the impure shell, and keeping it thin
// pure: all the decisions
function priceFor(cart, rules, now = Date.now()){
  const subtotal = cart.reduce((s, i) => s + i.price * i.qty, 0);
  const discount = rules.discountFor(subtotal, now);
  return { subtotal, discount, total: subtotal - discount };
}

// impure: all the effects
async function checkout(cartId){
  const cart    = await api.getCart(cartId);      // effect
  const rules   = await api.getRules();           // effect
  const priced  = priceFor(cart, rules);          // pure
  await api.post("/checkout", priced);            // effect
  render(priced);                                 // effect
  return priced;
}
// three effectful lines and one pure line that does all the
// thinking, which is the part worth testing
Purity breachFix
Reads Date.now()Take now as a parameter
Calls Math.random()Take a random function, or an index
Reads a module-level constantTake it as a parameter with the constant as default
Mutates an argumentCopy first: [...a], {...o}, structuredClone
Touches the DOM, storage or the networkReturn a description; let the caller perform it
LogsReturn the string; let the caller log it
Depends on locale or timezonePass the locale option and an explicit UTC date
Purity is not about testability alone. A pure render function can be run twice, out of order, or concurrently without the second run seeing the first — which is the property that makes concurrent rendering possible at all. That is why a framework that caches on the identity of a function needs it to be pure.

Inject time, randomness, rates and identities as parameters with production defaults, copy before mutating an argument, and keep every decision in a pure function with the effects in a thin shell. That gives deterministic tests and makes concurrent rendering safe.

Pure means the same input always gives the same output and nothing outside changes. Inject time, randomness and rates as parameters with production defaults, copy before mutating an argument, and keep the deciding logic pure with the side effects in a thin shell around it.

3 · Objects & Arrays

3.1 Object literals and property access3.2 Destructuring, spread and rest3.3 map, filter, reduce and find3.4 some, every and sorting3.5 Copying, references and JSON

Covers the two workhorse structures of JavaScript data: objects for named fields and arrays for ordered lists. Destructuring, spread, and the array methods live here. In practice you read and update these structures with property access, destructuring assignments, and transformation methods like map and filter.

Objects and arrays carry almost all of your data. This chapter teaches how to build, read, copy, and transform them — and the array methods you will use daily.

3.1 Object literals and property access

An object is a keyed collection of properties, and JavaScript gives you four ways to read and write them. Two of them are equivalent and slow, one is the one to use, and one is a trap that behaves differently in a way worth understanding rather than avoiding.

The four access forms

const user = { name: "Ada", age: 36 };

// 1. dot notation - static keys only
user.name;                // "Ada"

// 2. bracket - required for anything dynamic
user["name"];            // "Ada"
const key = "name";
user[key];                // "Ada"

// 3. optional chaining - no throw on a nullish link
user?.address?.city;     // undefined
user.address?.city;      // TypeError: user.address is undefined

// 4. computed - the key IS the expression
const [first, second] = ["n", "name"];
user[first];             // undefined - the KEY is "n", not the value
user[second];            // "Ada"
Bracket notation is not slower than dot notation, despite the old advice. Both compile to the same property access. What bracket notation requires is that the key be known at runtime — and that is a documentation decision far more than a performance one. Write user["name"] or user.name; both are identical in cost.

Keys, and what they can be

Key typeBecomesUse
StringStays a string{ "a-b": 1 } — a key with a dash needs brackets
NumberString{ 1: "a" } and { "1": "a" } are the same property
SymbolStays a SymbolThe only key that does not collide, and is invisible to enumeration
ObjectString, via toStringA trap. Two objects with the same string become one key
const obj = {};
obj[{ toString: () => "same" }] = 1;
obj[{ toString: () => "same" }] = 2;
obj.same;      // 2 - both objects became the key "same"

// symbols cannot collide
const ID = Symbol("id");
const a = { [ID]: 1 }, b = { [ID]: 2 };
a[ID];                       // 1
b[ID];                       // 2
Object.keys(a);             // [] - invisible to enumeration, JSON and
Object.getOwnPropertySymbols(a);  // [Symbol(id)] is where you find it

// a symbol registry, for a private-ish key shared across modules
const KEY = Symbol.for("app.key");
localStorage.setItem(KEY, "x");   // same key everywhere

// which is why library keys should be symbols
lib.observable[key]              // collision-free, and invisible in devtools
Object.keys(obj)                 // does not show your library's internals

Methods, and this

const counter = {
  count: 0,
  increment(){ return ++this.count; },
  arrow: () => typeof this,          // lexical this - not the object
};
counter.increment();     // 1
const f = counter.increment;
f();                     // TypeError: this is undefined (module code is strict)

// so methods need binding, or an arrow field
class Counter {
  #n = 0;
  increment = () => { return ++this.#n; };   // bound per instance
  decrement = () => { return --this.#n; };
}
const c = new Counter();
const g = c.increment;
g();                     // 1 - this is still the instance

// bind() does the same for a prototype method
const h = counter.increment.bind(counter);

// and in a DOM handler, this is the element
document.querySelector("#btn").addEventListener("click", function(){
  this.disabled = true;   // this is the button
});
// an arrow here would capture the module's this, not the button
FormOwn property?Shared across instances?
name(){} in an object literalYesNo — one per object
name(){} in a classNo — on the prototypeYes
name = () => {} as a class fieldYesNo — bound per instance
name: fn in a literalYesNo

Freezing, and when a value is genuinely immutable

There are three levels of protection, and they answer different questions. Two of them are shallow and one is deep, and the difference is exactly the shallow-versus-deep distinction from section 3.5.

CallDepthThrows on a later write?
Object.freeze(obj)ShallowYes, in strict mode. Silently ignored in sloppy mode
Object.seal(obj)ShallowYes for adding; existing properties still writable
Object.preventExtensions(obj)ShallowOnly for adding; the existing ones are fully mutable
A frozen array's elements—NOT protected. Freeze is shallow, so frozen.push() still works
const o = Object.freeze({ a: 1, nested: { b: 2 } });
o.a = 2;          // no effect in strict mode, TypeError caught silently
o.nested.b = 99;  // WORKS - the nested object is not frozen
o.c = 3;          // no effect

// which is why freeze is not "make it immutable"
const arr = Object.freeze([1, 2, 3]);
arr.push(4);      // works. Only the array's own properties are frozen.

// deep freeze, which costs a walk and is still not a security boundary
function deepFreeze(obj, seen = new WeakSet()){
  if (obj === null || typeof obj !== "object" || seen.has(obj)) return obj;
  seen.add(obj);                         // handles a cyclic graph
  Object.freeze(obj);
  for (const v of Object.values(obj)) deepFreeze(v, seen);
  return obj;
}

// and the property-level version, which is what a library needs
const state = { count: 0 };
Object.defineProperty(state, "count", {
  get(){ return backing; },
  set(v){ if (v < 0) throw new RangeError("count"); backing = v; },
  enumerable: true,
  configurable: false,
});
A getter/setter pair with a hidden backing variable is the only genuinely encapsulated property in JavaScript, and it is what makes a read-only value possible: a getter with no setter, or one that silently ignores assignments. Object.freeze prevents a property being replaced but does not stop a setter being invoked.

Dot and bracket access cost the same; bracket is for keys only known at runtime. Every object key becomes a string except a Symbol, so use symbols where collisions matter. A method needs its this bound before it is passed on — a class field holding an arrow does that. freeze is shallow, so it protects one level only.

Dot and bracket access are identical in cost; bracket is for keys you only know at runtime. Every object key becomes a string except a Symbol, so use symbols for anything that must not collide. A method shorthand needs this bound before it is passed anywhere — a class field holding an arrow does that for you.

3.2 Destructuring, spread and rest

Destructuring reads a pattern out of an array or an object in one expression, and binding it to names. Rest collects what the pattern did not match. Together they replace most of the index-and-property code that makes up ordinary application work.

Array patterns

const [first, second, third] = ["a", "b", "c"];
// first = "a", second = "b", third = "c"

const [x, , z] = [1, 2, 3];          // skip the second: x=1, z=3
const [x = 10, y = 20] = [];        // defaults: 10, 20
const [only] = [1];                 // only=1 - no error for missing

// swapping needs no temporary - the classic use
let a = 1, b = 2;
[a, b] = [b, a];                    // a=2, b=1

// nested
const [[x1], [y1]] = [[10], [20]];

// rest collects the remainder - and it must be last
const [head, ...tail] = [1, 2, 3, 4];
// head = 1, tail = [2, 3, 4]

// on the left-hand side of an assignment, not just a declaration
let first2, rest2;
[first2, ...rest2] = "abc";         // spreading a string works
// first2 = "a", rest2 = ["b", "c"]

// and the swap works on object properties too
let obj = { p: 1, q: 2 };
({ p: obj.p, q: obj.q } = { p: 3, q: 4 });   // NOTE the parentheses
// without them the { starts a block, and this is a syntax error
FormDoes
[a, b]Positional. Order is the contract
{ a, b }By name. Order and extra keys are irrelevant
[a, ...rest]All remaining elements
{ a, ...rest }All remaining properties
{ a: x }Rename: a bound to x
{ a = 1 }Default when the value is undefined
{ a: alias }Rename and default together

Object patterns

const { name, age } = { name: "Ada", age: 36, city: "London" };
// name, age bound. `city` is simply not read.

// renaming
const { name: userName } = profile;   // profile.name -> userName

// renaming with a default
const { name: n = "Anonymous" } = profile;

// a default alone uses the same name
const { country = "UK" } = profile;

// nested, which is where it earns its keep
const { data: { user: { name } } } = response;

// and with a guard, so one path covers absent and empty
const { data: { user: { name = "Anonymous" } = {} } = {} } = response;

// rest on an object collects every key NOT named - the inverse
const { id, ...withoutId } = record;
// withoutId has everything except id, and is a NEW object

// rest is shallow: nested objects are still shared references
const { nested } = original;
nested === original.nested;      // true - not cloned

// computed keys, from a variable
const key = "age";
const { [key]: age } = profile;  // profile.age -> age
Defaulting to = {} at every level of a nested pattern is not defensive noise — it is what makes a partial response safe to destructure. const { data: { user } = {} } = res will not throw when data is absent, and the guard is one = {} per level rather than a chain of optional chaining.

In parameters, and where it changes a signature

// as parameters - which is why options objects read well
function connect({ host, port = 5432, ssl = false, retries = 3 } = {}){
  return { host, port, ssl, retries };
}
connect({ host: "db.test" });   // the rest take their defaults

// the `= {}` is required for the whole-argument-omitted case
function f({ a } = {}){ return a; }
f({});      // undefined
f();        // {} thanks to the default

// as arguments at the call site - also common
const first = arr[0], second = arr[1];
send({ to: first, body: second });

// destructuring a function's return value, which removes indexing
const { data: users, total } = await api.list();
const { name, email } = users[0];

// and array destructuring on the return, for a tuple-like result
const [min, max] = bounds;   // if bounds() returns [min, max]

// renaming matters when the key is unclear in the new context
const { length: charCount } = str;
const { id: userId, createdAt } = row;

Destructuring in parameters, and the trap

// the default applies per-property, not to the object
function draw({ x = 0, y = 0, fill = "black" }){
  return `${fill} at ${x},${y}`;
}
draw({});                 // "black at 0,0" - every default applies
draw();                   // TypeError: cannot destructure undefined
draw({ x: 10 });          // "black at 10,0"

// so the whole-object default is needed for the omitted case, and
// it costs nothing
function draw2({ x = 0, y = 0, fill = "black" } = {}){ ... }
draw2();                  // works

// the trap: a default on the WRONG level
function bad({ opts } = { opts: {} }){ return opts.timeout; }
bad();                    // undefined.timeout - TypeError. The outer
                          // default made opts = {}, not { opts: {} }

// the fix: default each level
function good({ opts = {} } = {}){ return opts.timeout; }
