good();                   // undefined - which is probably what you want
good({ opts: { timeout: 5 } });   // 5
// destructuring does not require the object to be an object literal
const [x, y] = "ab";          // "a", "b" - strings are iterable
const { length } = "hello";   // 5
const [first] = new Set([1]); // 1 - Set and Map are iterable
const [a2, b2] = "xy".match(/x(y)/) ?? [];   // "y" from the index 1 group

// renaming on an API with an unhelpful key name
const { "content-type": contentType } = response.headers;

// and destructuring a function's arguments is the clearest form
function requireAll({ host, port = 80, secure = false }){
  return `${secure ? "https" : "http"}://${host}:${port}`;
}
requireAll({ host: "x.test" });   // http://x.test:80

// the value that destructuring cannot help with: a positional
// tuple with three unnamed entries
const [a, b, c] = compute();     // what are these?
// an object, or a comment, is worth more here

Array patterns are positional; object patterns are by name and ignore extras. A default applies per property, so you need = {} on the whole argument as well as per property. Object rest collects the keys you did not name, shallowly — so nested values inside it are still shared.

Array patterns are positional and order-dependent; object patterns are by name and ignore extras. Rest collects what did not match and is shallow, so a nested object inside a rest is still a shared reference. In a parameter position, add = {} so an omitted argument does not throw.

3.3 map, filter, reduce and find

Four array methods that take a function and return a new array, plus one that returns a single value. They are the backbone of data transformation in JavaScript, and choosing between them is mostly a question of what happens when nothing matches.

map, filter, and the empty case

MethodReturnsOn an empty arrayLength
mapA new array, one transformed item per input[]Same as input
filterA new array of the items that passed[]0 to n
findThe first matching itemundefined—
findIndexThe index of the first match-1—
findLastThe last matching itemundefined—
reduceWhatever you accumulateThe initial value, untouched—
someA booleanfalse—
everyA booleantrue — vacuously—
const orders = [
  { id: 1, total: 30, paid: true },
  { id: 2, total: 70, paid: false },
  { id: 3, total: 20, paid: true },
];

// map: same length, always
orders.map(o => o.total * 1.2);      // [36, 84, 24]

// filter: shorter, and the predicate should be a NAME
const unpaid = orders.filter(o => !o.paid);

// find: the first match, and it STOPS - unlike filter
orders.find(o => o.total > 25);    // { id: 2, ... }
orders.filter(o => o.total > 25);   // [{id:2}, {id:1}]  - also finds id 1

// findIndex returns -1, which is falsy but is not 0
const i = orders.findIndex(o => o.id === 2);   // 1
if (i){ }        // WRONG if the match was at index 0!
if (i !== -1){ } // correct
findIndex returns -1 for no match, and -1 is truthy while 0 is falsy — so if (i) is wrong in two different directions at once. Always test i !== -1, or i >= 0, or use at() which returns undefined and behaves consistently.

reduce, which has two shapes

// shape 1: reduce to a single value
const total = orders.reduce((sum, o) => sum + o.total, 0);
const byCustomer = orders.reduce((acc, o) => {
  acc[o.customerId] = (acc[o.customerId] ?? 0) + o.total;
  return acc;
}, {});                                    // the initial value is REQUIRED

// an omitted initial value uses the FIRST ELEMENT as both accumulator
// and current - which throws on an empty array and misbehaves otherwise
orders.reduce((a, b) => a + b);   // string concat on an array of objects

// shape 2: reduce to a new array - which is usually map or filter
const ids = orders.reduce((acc, o) => { acc.push(o.id); return acc; }, []);
// ...but reduce CAN express what map cannot:
const grouped = orders.reduce((acc, o) => {
  (acc[o.paid ? "paid" : "unpaid"] ??= []).push(o);
  return acc;
}, {});
const result = Object.values(grouped).flat().length;

// flatMap, which does both in one pass
const tags = posts.flatMap(p => p.tags);   // one array, not nested
const pairs = orders.flatMap(o => o.items.map(i => [o.id, i.id]));
Always pass the initial value to reduce. Without it the first element is used as the accumulator, which throws on an empty array and silently produces a wrong answer on a one-element array. The initial value is also what fixes the accumulator's type, so [] and {} are how you state "I am building a collection" rather than adding up numbers.
// the chain, and when it stops being readable
const top = orders
  .filter(o => o.paid)
  .sort((a, b) => b.total - a.total)
  .slice(0, 5)
  .map(o => o.id);

// four passes, four arrays allocated. For a large list, one pass:
const top2 = [];
for (const o of orders){
  if (!o.paid) continue;
  top2.push(o);
  top2.sort((a, b) => b.total - a.total);
  if (top2.length === 5) top2.pop();    // wrong: sort must happen before trim
}

// the correct single-pass version
const top3 = [...orders]
  .sort((a, b) => b.total - a.total)   // sort FIRST
  .filter(o => o.paid)
  .slice(0, 5)
  .map(o => o.id);
// note the order changed: filtering before sorting means sorting
// fewer items, which is the whole optimisation

What each returns when nothing matches

Call on an empty arrayReturnsTruthy?Why it matters
[].map(fn)[]YesA render that assumes one item per input gets none
[].filter(fn)[]YesSame
[].find(fn)undefinedNoUse a default or an explicit check
[].findIndex(fn)-1YesThe trap. Test !== -1
[].reduce(fn)The initial valueDependsWithout an initial value it throws
[].some(fn)falseNoCorrect for "is any bad?"
[].every(fn)trueYesVacuously true. A validation that passes nothing
[].flat()[]YesSafe
// the two that catch people, with the fixes

// findIndex: -1 is truthy and 0 is falsy
const idx = list.findIndex(x => x.id === 1);
if (idx){ }              // wrong twice: -1 passes, 0 fails
if (idx !== -1){ }       // correct
if (idx >= 0){ }       // correct
const item = list.at(idx);   // undefined for -1, so this composes

// every: vacuously true on empty
const ok = items.every(isValid);
items.length > 0 && items.every(isValid);   // correct

// flatMap with a callback that can return an array or nothing
const ids = orders.flatMap(o => o.paid ? [o.id] : []);
// note the [] - returning nothing gives you a nested undefined
// if you return a non-array it is inserted as a single element

// and a filter that changes the count
const result = list
  .map(normalise)      // same length - safe before a filter
  .filter(Boolean);    // only correct if normalise can return falsy

// which is why filter(Boolean) is a common and slightly
// dangerous idiom: it silently removes every falsy value from
// the result, including a legitimate 0 or ""

filter(Boolean) deserves the warning. It is a convenient way to drop undefined from a map that may not have matched, and it will equally drop a legitimate 0, "" or false. Write the filter with an explicit predicate when the values can legitimately be falsy, or use flatMap with an explicit empty array.

findIndex returns -1, which is truthy while 0 is falsy — never test it for truthiness. [].every(fn) is true, so a validation needs a length check. Always pass reduce an initial value, and use flatMap instead of filter(Boolean) where a legitimate 0 could be lost.

map and filter return new arrays; find returns the first match and stops; findIndex returns -1, so never test it for truthiness. Always give reduce an initial value — it fixes the accumulator type and makes the empty array safe. Use flatMap rather than map followed by flat.

3.4 some, every and sorting

some and every return booleans and both short-circuit, which makes them O(n) in the best case rather than always. sort mutates in place and depends on a comparator whose contract is remembered wrong by almost everyone.

some, every, and the empty-array trap

const forms = [
  { name: "a", valid: true },
  { name: "b", valid: false },
];

forms.some(f => !f.valid);     // true  - stops at the first failure
forms.every(f => f.valid);     // false - stops at the first failure

// the empty-array answers, and they are not the same
[].some(f => f.valid);        // false - "is there a bad one?" no
[].every(f => f.valid);       // TRUE  - "are all valid?" vacuously yes

// so every() on an empty list passes a validation check that
// should have failed. Always check length first:
const valid = forms.length > 0 && forms.every(f => f.valid);

// include() is the same as some() with a simpler callback
forms.some(f => f.name === "a");   // true
forms.find(f => f.name === "a");   // the item

// at() reads an index, including from the end, safely
const last = forms.at(-1);           // { name: "b", ... }
const oob  = forms.at(99);           // undefined, not undefined property
// unlike arr[99], which is also undefined but a hole
[].every(fn) is true. Any validation built as items.every(isValid) passes an empty list — which is exactly the case where a form with no fields, a permissions query with no rows, or a batch job with no work silently reports success.

sort, and the comparator contract

const items = [10, 9, 1, 100];

// the default comparator converts to STRING and compares lexicographically
items.sort();            // [1, 10, 100, 9]   - not what anyone expects
[10,9,1,100].sort((a,b) => a - b);   // [1, 9, 10, 100]

// it MUTATES, and returns the same array
const orig = [3, 1, 2];
const same = orig.sort();
same === orig;            // true - no new array
const copy = [...orig].sort((a,b) => a - b);   // copy first

// returning a boolean is the bug: it coerces to 0 or 1
items.sort((a, b) => a > b);      // wrong - 0 or 1, so almost nothing moves
items.sort((a, b) => a > b ? 1 : -1);   // correct
items.sort((a, b) => a - b);                // correct, and simplest

// strings: localeCompare, not >
["a","B","c"].sort();                      // uppercase first - wrong
["a","B","c"].sort((a,b) => a.localeCompare(b));   // a, B, c
GoalComparator
Ascending numbers(a, b) => a - b
Descending numbers(a, b) => b - a
Strings, human order(a, b) => a.localeCompare(b)
By a property(a, b) => a.total - b.total
By a property, descending(a, b) => b.total - a.total
Stable multi-key(a, b) => b.date - a.date || a.name.localeCompare(b.name)

Sort is stable in every current engine, which means equal elements keep their relative order. That is what makes a multi-key comparator work: sort by the secondary key, then by the primary, and the second sort preserves the first sort's result for ties. It is also why toSorted exists — a non-mutating version that returns a new array.

// toSorted: no mutation, and it is a copy
const sorted = items.toSorted((a, b) => a - b);
items;      // unchanged
sorted;     // a new array

// toReversed, toSpliced, with, and findLast are the rest
// structuredClone is for a deep copy - and it fails on functions,
// DOM nodes and class prototypes
const deep = structuredClone({ a: 1, b: [2, 3] });   // works
structuredClone({ fn(){} });   // DataCloneError

// which is why a shallow copy is usually what you want
const shallow = { ...original };            // new object, shared nested refs
const deep2 = structuredClone(original);    // fully independent

Sorting, and what it actually costs

The sort specification has required a stable sort since ES2019, and every current engine provides one. That single fact makes multi-key sorting work by sorting the less significant key first.

// multi-key, in the right order: least significant FIRST
records.sort((a, b) =>
  a.lastName.localeCompare(b.lastName) ||   // primary
  a.firstName.localeCompare(b.firstName) || // secondary
  a.birthDate - b.birthDate                  // tertiary
);
// stability means the second sort does not disturb the first sort's
// result for equal last names

// the Intl-aware way, which is genuinely better for names
const collator = new Intl.Collator(undefined, {
  sensitivity: "base",   // a/A and e/É compare equal
  numeric: true,         // "10" sorts after "9", not after "1"
});
records.sort((a, b) => collator.compare(a.name, b.name));
// build the collator ONCE: constructing one per comparison is the
// single most common way to make a sort slow

// performance, in practice
const big = Array.from({ length: 100000 }, (_, i) => i);

// worst case: a comparator that is not a total order
big.sort(() => 0);              // unpredictable, sometimes O(n^2)

// best: a cheap comparator over already-near-sorted data
big.sort((a, b) => a - b);

// and the array methods that are often faster than a chain
BigInt64Array.prototype.sort   // sorts numerically, with no comparator
const typed = new Float64Array(big).sort();    // fast, but loses precision
                                      // beyond Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER
Build an Intl.Collator once and reuse it. A comparator that constructs one per call allocates on every comparison, which for a ten-thousand-element sort is a hundred thousand allocations — and sorting becomes the bottleneck in a function that looked harmless.
// the checks, and their early-exit behaviour
const anyInvalid = items.some(x => !x.ok);   // stops at the first
const allValid   = items.every(x => x.ok);    // stops at the first
// so a validation with most items valid is fast, and one with
// most items INVALID is fast too - whichever the answer is

// count, without reduce
const count = items.filter(x => x.ok).length;
const count2 = items.reduce((n, x) => n + (x.ok ? 1 : 0), 0);

// and a note on includes with NaN
[NaN].includes(NaN);          // true  - SameValueZero
[NaN].indexOf(NaN);          // -1    - strict equality
// a genuine difference, and the reason Object.is and includes
// disagree about NaN while indexOf does not

[].every(fn) is true, so guard a validation with a length check. sort mutates in place, needs a numeric comparator rather than one returning a boolean, and is stable — so sort by the least significant key first. Build an Intl.Collator once rather than per comparison. Use toSorted when you must not mutate.

[].every(fn) is true, so guard a validation with a length check. sort mutates in place, defaults to lexicographic string order, and needs a numeric comparator (a,b) => a-b — never one returning a boolean. Sort is stable, so sort by the secondary key first, and use toSorted when you must not mutate.

3.5 Copying, references and JSON

Assignment copies a reference; it does not copy an object. Every "my array changed and I don't know why" report is that sentence, and the fix is knowing which of the six copy operations you need.

What assignment actually does

const a = { n: { x: 1 } };
const b = a;
b.n.x = 2;
a.n.x;          // 2 - same object, not a copy
b === a;        // true

// so a function that takes an object and mutates it mutates the caller's
function addTag(o, tag){ o.tags.push(tag); }
const post = { tags: [] };
addTag(post, "js");
post.tags;      // ["js"] - changed without the caller asking
OperationDepthCostKeeps prototype?
b = aReferenceFreeYes
{ ...a }ShallowCheapNo
Object.assign({}, a)ShallowCheapNo
structuredClone(a)DeepExpensiveNo
[...arr]Shallow arrayCheapn/a
arr.slice()Shallow arrayCheapn/a
arr.map(x => x)Shallow arrayMediumn/a
const original = { a: 1, nested: { b: 2 }, list: [1, 2] };

// shallow: new top level, SHARED nested values
const shallow = { ...original };
shallow.nested.b = 99;
original.nested.b;     // 99 - shared
shallow.list.push(3);
original.list;         // [1,2,3] - shared

// deep: nothing shared
const deep = structuredClone(original);
deep.nested.b = 99;
original.nested.b;     // 2

// and what spread does to the prototype
class Point { constructor(){ this.x = 1; } get mag(){ return 1; } }
const p = new Point();
const copy = { ...p };
copy.mag;              // undefined - methods are not own properties
copy instanceof Point; // false

// which is why the idiom is explicit about it
const clone = Object.assign(Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(p)), p);
clone instanceof Point;  // true
Almost every time a shallow copy "failed", the actual requirement was either "a new top level so I can add or remove keys" — which is what spread is for — or "fully independent", which is structuredClone. Naming which one you want in a variable (shallow, copy, draft) makes the intent obvious at the call site.

JSON, and what it is for

const state = {
  n: 1,
  s: "a",
  b: true,
  nil: null,
  undef: undefined,      // DROPPED
  fn(){},                // DROPPED
  date: new Date(0),     // becomes a STRING
  map: new Map(),        // becomes {}
  set: new Set([1,2]),   // becomes {}
  err: new Error("x"),   // becomes {}
  nan: NaN,              // becomes null
  inf: Infinity,         // becomes null
  big: 10n,              // THROWS
  cyc: null,             // set below -> THROWS
};
state.cyc = state;

JSON.stringify(state);   // throws: BigInt, then circular

// what actually survives
JSON.stringify({ n:1, s:"a", b:true, nil:null });
// '{"n":1,"s":"a","b":true,"nil":null}'

// and undefined, NaN and Infinity inside an object are silently
// dropped or nulled - which is data loss with no warning
JSON.stringify({ a: undefined, b: NaN, c: Infinity });
// '{}'

// Date becomes an ISO string, and loses the Date identity
const d = new Date();
JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(d)).getTime;   // TypeError: not a function
// the correct fix: a replacer that handles what JSON cannot
function jsonReplacer(key, value){
  if (typeof value === "bigint") return value.toString();
  if (value instanceof Date)   return { __type: "Date", v: value.toISOString() };
  if (value instanceof Map)    return { __type: "Map", v: [...value] };
  if (value instanceof Set)    return { __type: "Set", v: [...value] };
  if (typeof value === "number" && !Number.isFinite(value)) return null;
  if (value instanceof Error)  return { __type: "Error", name: value.name,
                                        message: value.message };
  return value;
}

function jsonReviver(key, value){
  if (value && value.__type === "Date")  return new Date(value.v);
  if (value && value.__type === "Map")   return new Map(value.v);
  if (value && value.__type === "Set")   return new Set(value.v);
  if (value && value.__type === "Error")
    return Object.assign(new Error(value.message), { name: value.name });
  return value;
}

JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(state, jsonReplacer), jsonReviver);
A replacer that only handles the top level does nothing for nested values. And a reviver that cannot tell a real {"__type":"Date"} object from one you created is a deserialisation vulnerability — a payload from an untrusted source can become a Map, a Date or an Error. Only run a reviver on data you produced yourself.

Updating immutably, which is where this pays

Once you stop mutating, three things become possible: a state object can be compared by reference, a re-render can be skipped when nothing changed, and history becomes a list of references rather than a stack of diffs.

// mutating: the reference never changes, so nothing can tell
const state = { items: [] };
state.items.push("a");
state === state;               // true, before and after - useless for skipping

// immutable: a new reference whenever anything changed
let state = { items: [] };
state = { ...state, items: [...state.items, "a"] };
// a new object every time, so prev !== next is a valid change test

// updating one field of one of ten
const next = { ...state, user: { ...state.user, name: "Ada" } };
// both levels need spreading, and the cost is one object per level

// and why that cost is usually worth it
function shouldRender(prev, next){
  return prev !== next;        // a reference check, correct for
}                              // immutable state and wrong for
                               // anything mutated in place

// remove and update, without index arithmetic
const without = items.filter(x => x.id !== id);
const updated = items.map(x => x.id === id ? { ...x, done: true } : x);
// filter for removal, map for update - never splice, which mutates
// and changes length, so every memoised child re-renders
// the shallow trap, which is the one that bites
const state = { a: { count: 0 }, b: { count: 0 } };
const next = { ...state, a: { ...state.a, count: 1 } };
next.b === state.b;            // TRUE - b is still the same object
// so next.b.count is 0, and any memo on b will be reused correctly,
// but any code that compared b by identity across the change will
// see it as unchanged even if it did change elsewhere

// structuredClone is the deep answer, and it has a cost
const deep = structuredClone(state);   // walks the whole graph
// fine for a few hundred nodes, wrong for a large list

// and the map/set caveat: shallow spread does not clone them
const s = { tags: new Set(["a"]) };
const s2 = { ...s };             // s2.tags IS s.tags
s2.tags.add("b");                // s.tags is now {a, b}
// so a Set or Map inside a state object needs explicit handling
const s3 = { ...s, tags: new Set(s.tags) };
// or it needs to be treated as immutable and never mutated

Immutable updates are not automatically faster — each one allocates. They are worth it when something downstream can use the reference to decide whether to do work, because that is a comparison you cannot make against a mutated object at any price.

Assignment copies a reference; spread and slice are shallow and lose the prototype; structuredClone is deep but cannot clone functions or DOM nodes. JSON is transport only — it drops undefined, turns Date into a string and throws on BigInt. Update immutably with spread and map/filter rather than splice, and note a Map or Set inside state is still shared by a shallow copy.

Assignment copies a reference. Spread and slice are shallow and lose the prototype; structuredClone is deep and cannot clone functions or DOM nodes. JSON is for transport only — it drops undefined and functions, turns Date into a string, nulls NaN, and throws on BigInt. Use a replacer and a reviver for anything round-tripping.

4 · The DOM & Events

4.1 Selecting elements4.2 Creating and inserting nodes4.3 Classes and styles4.4 Events, bubbling and delegation4.5 Forms, inputs and preventDefault

Makes pages respond to users by finding elements, building nodes, handling events, and reading forms. This is the browser chapter where code touches the page. You select elements, attach listeners, and update the DOM, then the user’s actions trigger your handlers through the event system.

The Document Object Model is the browser’s view of your HTML. This chapter shows how to select, create, and style elements, and how events flow through them.

4.1 Selecting elements

Five ways to get an element, and the choice between them is mostly about how often you will need to look it up again and how many elements match. Getting it wrong costs either a query on every keystroke or a stale reference that no longer exists.

The five, compared

MethodReturnsLive?Cost
getElementByIdThe element or nulln/aFastest. O(1)
querySelectorThe first match or nulln/aFull selector engine
querySelectorAllA static NodeListNoFull engine, all matches
getElementsByClassNameA live HTMLCollectionYesFaster than the equivalent query
getElementsByTagNameA live HTMLCollectionYesFastest for many matches
closestThe element or nearest ancestor matchingn/aWalks up from a given node
<ul id="list">
  <li class="item" data-id="1">One</li>
  <li class="item" data-id="2">Two</li>
</ul>

// by id - the only one that takes no selector syntax
document.getElementById("list");

// CSS selectors, and the full syntax is available
document.querySelector("#list .item[data-id='2']");
document.querySelectorAll("li.item");         // NodeList - static
document.querySelector(":scope > li");        // :scope for "children of this"

// live collections reflect DOM changes; NodeLists do not
const live = document.getElementsByClassName("item");
live.length;                                  // 2
list.append(newItem);
live.length;                                  // 3 - updated automatically

const stat = document.querySelectorAll(".item");
stat.length;                                  // 2 - and still 2 after the append
// so a static NodeList must be re-queried to see new elements

// closest walks UP, which is what a delegated handler needs
document.addEventListener("click", (e) => {
  const row = e.target.closest("tr[data-id]");
  if (!row) return;                 // clicked outside a row
  handle(row.dataset.id);
});
A live HTMLCollection is both faster and mutated by the DOM while you iterate it. Removing items during a for loop over a live collection skips elements, because indices shift underneath the loop. Iterating a static NodeList has the same problem if you remove nodes; snapshot it with [...collection] first.
// snapshot before mutating while iterating
const items = document.getElementsByClassName("item");
for (const el of [...items]) el.remove();     // safe: [... ] snapshots

for (let i = items.length - 1; i >= 0; i--){  // iterate backwards instead
  items[i].remove();
}

// and the selector shortcuts, which are the same engine
document.querySelector(":hover");             // whatever is hovered - changes
document.querySelectorAll("li:nth-child(odd)");
document.querySelectorAll("input:not([type=hidden])");
document.querySelectorAll("[data-state='open']");
document.querySelectorAll(":has(> img)");     // parent selectors work

// scoped to a subtree - pass a root, not the document
const root = document.querySelector("#panel");
root.querySelectorAll("button");               // only buttons inside #panel

// matches, closest, and contains
el.matches(".item.active");      // boolean, no traversal
el.contains(otherEl);            // is otherEl inside el
el.compareDocumentPosition(b);   // the full ordering

Scoping, and the selectors that cannot be scoped

A selector is matched against a subtree when you pass a root. That handles "inside this component", and two things still cannot be expressed: "not inside anything like this", and "the parent of".

// scoped to a root - the root itself is NOT included by querySelector
root.querySelector(".x");        // descendants only
root.matches(".x");              // does the root itself match?

// the parent - no parent selector exists, but :has works backwards
.card:has(> .badge){ }          // a card with a direct badge child
tr:has(> td:first-child a){ }    // the usual "row has a link" pattern
:has(+ .next){ }                // a sibling AFTER, but never before
// which is the workaround for the missing parent combinator
li:not(:has(+ li:not(:has(+ li)))){ }   // a last-of-type heuristic

// and scoping by exclusion, which :has() finally made possible
.modal:not(:has(iframe)){ }      // a modal with no embedded content
input:not([type=hidden]):not(:disabled):invalid{ border-color: red; }

// querySelectorAll with a comma needs no scoping help
form.querySelectorAll("input[name], select[name]");
// and the CSS.escape for a dynamic id, which is the one place
// a selector string is genuinely unsafe
const sel = "#" + CSS.escape(userSuppliedId);
Interpolating a value into a selector string is an injection point: querySelector("#" + id) throws on an id containing a space or a bracket, and matches something unintended on an id containing a comma. Use getElementById for an id, CSS.escape() where a selector is genuinely required, and dataset plus closest for attributes.
// a live collection shifts while you read it
const rows = document.querySelectorAll("tbody tr");   // static - safe
const rows2 = document.getElementsByTagName("tr");   // live

rows2[0].remove();
rows2.length;      // already one fewer - you skipped an element

// iterating a live collection while mutating it skips items
for (const r of document.getElementsByTagName("li")){
  if (shouldRemove(r)) r.remove();     // wrong: indices shift
}
for (const r of [...document.getElementsByTagName("li")]){   // snapshot
  if (shouldRemove(r)) r.remove();     // correct
}
for (let i = rows.length - 1; i >= 0; i--){                // or backwards
  if (shouldRemove(rows[i])) rows[i].remove();
}

// and the NodeList has the useful methods now
document.querySelectorAll("li").forEach(el => {});   // forEach exists
Array.from(document.querySelectorAll("li"));            // for map/filter

// performance: cache the query when it is in a loop or a hot path
const buttons = document.querySelectorAll("button");
for (const b of buttons){
  b.addEventListener("click", () => { buttons.indexOf(b); });
}
// re-querying inside the handler means a full selector pass per click

Use getElementById for an id, querySelector for one and querySelectorAll for several — and remember the latter is a static NodeList while getElementsBy* are live and shift as the DOM changes, which silently skips elements when you remove during iteration. Use closest for delegated handlers and CSS.escape rather than interpolating into a selector.

Use getElementById for an id, querySelector for one element and querySelectorAll for several — and remember the latter returns a static NodeList while getElementsBy* return live collections that shift under you as you mutate. Use closest for delegated handlers.

4.2 Creating and inserting nodes

Six methods for putting elements into the document, and they differ in what they insert around. Choosing wrongly is why a listener stops firing or a node lands in the wrong parent after a framework has already rendered.

Inside a parent

MethodInsertsParses HTML?Returns
parent.append(child)As the last childYes, if given a stringundefined
parent.prepend(child)As the first childYesundefined
parent.before(node)As the previous siblingYesundefined
parent.after(node)As the next siblingYesundefined
parent.insertBefore(n, ref)Before a reference nodeNoThe inserted node
parent.replaceChildren(...)Replaces everythingYesundefined
el.replaceWith(node)Replaces the element itselfYesundefined
el.remove()Removes the elementn/aundefined
<ul id="list"><li>A</li></ul>
const list = document.querySelector("#list");

// multiple nodes at once, and plain strings, which become text nodes
list.append(el1, el2);
list.append("plain text");          // a Text node - safe, no HTML parsing
list.prepend(li);

// multiple strings DO get parsed as HTML
list.append("<li>one</li>", "<li>two</li>");   // two <li> elements
// which is the difference, and the reason user input must never
// reach an append() that is given a string

// insertBefore: the one method that returns the node
const ref = list.firstElementChild;
const inserted = list.insertBefore(newLi, ref);

// replaceChildren is the common "re-render this region" call
list.replaceChildren(...items);

// and remove, which is on the element, not the parent
el.remove();
// ChildNode.remove() exists on any node type, including text
Every insert method except insertBefore returns undefined, so chaining on the result silently does nothing. And passing an HTML string to append parses it — which is fine for markup you wrote and an injection vector for markup a reader supplied. Use textContent for text, always.

Building elements, and the safe route

// createElement + textContent: no parsing, so no injection
const li = document.createElement("li");
li.className = "item";
li.textContent = userInput;          // a Text node, always safe
li.dataset.id = String(id);
list.append(li);

// a document fragment for many nodes: one reflow instead of many
const frag = document.createDocumentFragment();
for (const item of items){
  const li = document.createElement("li");
  li.textContent = item.name;
  frag.append(li);
}
list.append(frag);                    // a single insertion point

// cloneNode: shallow by default
const dup = li.cloneNode();           // no children, no event listeners
const full = li.cloneNode(true);      // children too, still no listeners
// listeners never clone, because they are not part of the node

// and the template element, which is parsed but never rendered
const tpl = document.querySelector("#row-template");
const row = tpl.content.cloneNode(true);
row.querySelector(".name").textContent = name;
// innerHTML: the tool to be careful with
el.innerHTML = "<p>Hello</p>";        // parses, renders, and
                                                  // DROPS every listener
                                                  // inside, and does not
                                                  // run <script>
el.textContent = "<p>Hello</p>";      // displays it literally

// so innerHTML is fine for markup you wrote, and wrong for:
//   - anything containing user input
//   - re-rendering a region that has event listeners on children
//   - a controlled <textarea> or <input> - it destroys the
//     current value, the selection and the undo history

// and the parsing differences that bite
el.innerHTML = "<li>x</li>";           // the <li> is DROPPED -
                                                  // a bare <li> is not
                                                  // valid outside a <ul>
el.innerHTML = "<tr><td>x</td></tr>";  // same for <tr>
el.innerHTML = "<td>x</td>";              // survives

// so building table cells by innerHTML is a classic silent bug

Fragments, and the one reflow instead of many

Every insertion into the live document can force the engine to recalculate layout. Adding a thousand nodes one at a time in a loop is a thousand opportunities to do that, and a fragment reduces it to one.

<ul id="list"></ul>
const list = document.querySelector("#list");

// 1000 insertions, possibly 1000 layouts
for (const item of items){
  const li = document.createElement("li");
  li.textContent = item.name;
  list.append(li);
}

// one insertion, at most one layout
const frag = document.createDocumentFragment();
for (const item of items){
  const li = document.createElement("li");
  li.textContent = item.name;
  frag.append(li);
}
list.append(frag);        // a fragment's children are MOVED, not copied

// the measure: how many layouts the browser actually did
const observer = new PerformanceObserver(list => {
  for (const e of list.getEntries())
    console.log(e.value, "layout shift");
});
observer.observe({ type: "layout-shift", buffered: true });
// a large batch with many shifts is the signature of this problem

// replaceChildren for a full re-render, which is one operation
list.replaceChildren(...newItems.map(toLi));
Batch writes and keep them together. Reading a layout property — offsetHeight, getBoundingClientRect(), scrollTop — forces layout immediately. Alternating a read and a write in a loop forces a layout per iteration, which is the single most common cause of a page that stutters on scroll or while dragging.
// interleaved read/write - a layout per iteration
for (const el of items){
  el.style.height = el.scrollHeight * 2 + "px";   // write
  console.log(el.offsetHeight);                     // read - forces layout
}

// batched - one layout at the end
const heights = items.map(el => el.scrollHeight * 2);   // all reads
items.forEach((el, i) => { el.style.height = heights[i] + "px"; });

// and with rAF, so it is once per frame rather than per iteration
requestAnimationFrame(() => {
  items.forEach((el, i) => { el.style.height = heights[i] + "px"; });
});

// innerHTML parses, and the difference between append and prepend
// of strings is the whole reason one is safe and one is not
list.append("text");                  // a Text node - never parsed as HTML
list.append("<b>text</b>");   // an element - parsed
list.textContent = "a < b";         // always a Text node

Only insertBefore returns the node; the rest return undefined, so do not chain on them. Append a DocumentFragment for many nodes and you get one insertion instead of many. Build with createElement plus textContent for anything containing input — innerHTML drops listeners, parses user input, and destroys the value, selection and undo history of a controlled field.

Only insertBefore returns the node; the rest return undefined, so do not chain on them. Append a DocumentFragment for many nodes and you get one insertion instead of many. Build with createElement plus textContent for anything containing input — innerHTML drops listeners and parses user input.

4.3 Classes and styles

Three APIs for styling, and the class one is worth preferring over the style one almost every time. The reason is not preference: reading a computed style forces a layout, and doing that in a loop over many elements is measurably slow.

The three APIs

APISetsReturnsNotes
el.classList.add/remove/toggleWhole tokensvoidHandles spaces, duplicates and the SVG case
el.classNameThe whole stringStringOverwrites. Also fails on SVG in older engines
el.classList.toggle(c, force)One token, optionally forcedboolean — whether it is now onThe only one that reports the new state
el.style.prop = vOne inline propertyvoidInline, so it beats every stylesheet rule
el.style.setProperty(k, v)One property, with a priority argumentvoidNeeded for custom properties and !important
el.style.cssTextEverythingStringRead or write the whole block. Parses on every write
const el = document.querySelector("#msg");

// classList is the right default
el.classList.add("is-visible");
el.classList.remove("is-hidden");
el.classList.toggle("is-open");
const isOpen = el.classList.toggle("is-open");    // returns the NEW state
el.classList.replace("old", "new");
el.classList.contains("is-open");                  // boolean, no traversal
el.classList.replaceAll("is-a", "is-b");           // replaces every token

// className overwrites - and this is the bug
el.className = "btn primary";
// ...later, elsewhere:
el.className = "btn";            // `primary` is gone. It was a whole-string
                                 // assignment, not a token edit

// and classList handles the whitespace you forget
el.setAttribute("class", "  a   b  ");
el.classList.contains("a");     // true - classList splits on whitespace
el.className.split(" ")[0];     // "" - the leading spaces break it
// the inline-style trap: it outranks every stylesheet rule
.panel{ display: none; }
el.style.display = "block";        // works, and nothing can override it
// ...and to undo it you must REMOVE the inline value, not set a new one
el.style.display = "";             // correct - clears it
// el.style.display = "none" then deleting the class would not help,
// because the inline style still says block

// custom properties need setProperty
el.style.setProperty("--progress", "0.4");
el.style.getPropertyValue("--progress");          // " 0.4" - note the space
el.style.removeProperty("--progress");

// and !important only through setProperty
el.style.setProperty("color", "red", "important");

// reading a computed style forces a layout - never in a loop
for (const e of items){
  if (getComputedStyle(e).display !== "none"){ ... }   // one layout EACH
}
for (const e of items){
  if (e.offsetParent !== null){ ... }                  // usually enough
}
// or batch: read all, then write all, and the engine lays out once
const widths = items.map(el => el.offsetWidth);
items.forEach((el, i) => { el.style.width = widths[i] + "px"; });

Prefer a class over an inline style for anything a stylesheet should be able to change. An inline style sits above every rule in every sheet with no specificity involved, so the only way to change it is to write to it again — which makes a state that CSS should be driving impossible to drive from a stylesheet.

// the pattern: CSS owns the state, JS toggles a hook
/* CSS */
.msg[data-state="open"]{
  opacity: 1;
  translate: 0 0;
}
.msg{
  opacity: 0;
  translate: 0 1rem;
  transition: opacity .2s, translate .2s;
}

/* JS - one attribute, and every visual detail lives in the sheet */
el.dataset.state = "open";        // or el.setAttribute("data-state","open")
// now a stylesheet change can alter what "open" looks like without
// touching the script, and the state is inspectable in dev tools

// toggling many, batched
for (const el of items) el.classList.add("is-ready");
// one layout, because the writes are batched by the engine until
// the next frame

Attributes, and the dataset proxy

// data-* goes through dataset, which camel-cases the name
el.dataset.userId = "42";            // writes data-user-id="42"
el.dataset.userId;                   // "42"
el.dataset["user-id"];               // undefined - the key is camelCase
el.setAttribute("data-user-id", "42");
el.getAttribute("data-user-id");     // "42"
el.hasAttribute("data-user-id");

// data- attributes are strings, always
el.dataset.count = 5;                // becomes "5"
el.dataset.count;                     // "5" - not the number 5

// and they are readable in the HTML source, so nothing secret
el.dataset.apiKey = "sk-live-...";   // visible in view-source

// classList, against className and setAttribute
el.classList.add("a", "b");          // several tokens at once
el.classList.replace("a", "b");
el.classList.replaceAll("is-x", "is-y");
el.classList.item(0);                 // the first token
[...el.classList];                    // all tokens, as an array
el.classList.length;
el.classList.value;                   // the whole string

// className on an SVG element was broken for a long time
el.className = "x";                   // NOT an SVGAnimatedString
el.setAttribute("class", "x");        // works on both

// and a class is not a place for a value that changes often -
// a data attribute or a property is
el.dataset.state = "open";            // inspectable, and CSS can hook it
el.style.setProperty("--pct", "0.4"); // for a value CSS reads
Setting style.height to a value read from the same element in the same expression forces a synchronous layout. And writing a custom property in a loop invalidates style for the whole subtree, because custom properties inherit — so el.style.setProperty("--pct", ...) on one element is a subtree-wide recalculation, not a local one.

Use classList, not className: it handles whitespace, does not overwrite, and works on SVG. Clear an inline style with el.style.prop = "" rather than setting a different value. Prefer a class or a data attribute over an inline style for anything a stylesheet should be able to change — an inline style outranks every rule with no way to override it except by writing to it again.

Use classList, not className — it handles whitespace, does not overwrite, and works on SVG. To undo an inline style, clear it with el.style.prop = "" rather than setting a different value. Reading getComputedStyle forces layout, so batch your reads. Where CSS should own the state, toggle a class or a data attribute and let the stylesheet do the rest.

4.4 Events, bubbling and delegation

An event travels in three phases, and knowing which one you are in is what makes bubbling, delegation and stopPropagation make sense instead of being folklore. Most of the confusing behaviour comes from expecting a listener on a child to fire before one on a parent.

The three phases

<div id="outer">
  <div id="middle">
    <button id="inner">Click</button>
  </div>
</div>

// capture: window -> document -> html -> body -> #outer ->
//          #middle -> #inner        (target phase, listeners in order)
// bubble:   #inner -> #middle -> #outer -> body -> html ->
//          document -> window

for (const type of ["click"]){
  document.getElementById("outer")
    .addEventListener(type, () => console.log("outer bubble"), false);
  document.getElementById("outer")
    .addEventListener(type, () => console.log("outer CAPTURE"), true);
}
document.getElementById("inner")
  .addEventListener("click", () => console.log("inner target"));

The third argument of addEventListener is useCapture, and it defaults to false. Capture runs before the target, bubble after. Most listeners you write are bubble phase, which is correct for delegation.

Delegation, and why it is the better default

// per-element: N listeners, and they must be added for elements
// created later too
document.querySelectorAll(".row").forEach(row => {
  row.addEventListener("click", onClick);      // N listeners
});

// delegated: ONE listener on a stable ancestor
document.querySelector("#table").addEventListener("click", (e) => {
  const row = e.target.closest("tr[data-id]");
  if (!row || !row.closest("#table")) return;   // outside the table
  handle(row.dataset.id);
});
// one listener, works for rows added later, and closest() means the
// row can contain any amount of markup without the selector mattering
One closest() check is not enough when the container can be nested inside itself. The pattern if (!e.target.closest("#table")) return; is wrong if a table contains a table: a click in the inner table passes the outer table's check too. Guard with a check that the found ancestor is the container itself, or test that the click did not start inside a nested instance.
// propagation control
el.addEventListener("click", (e) => {
  e.stopPropagation();      // stops BUBBLING to ancestors - the target
                            // listeners have already run, and capture
                            // listeners above have already run
  e.stopImmediatePropagation();  // also stops OTHER listeners on el itself
  e.preventDefault();       // cancels the default action - form submit,
                            // a link navigation, checkbox toggle
});
// they are independent: preventDefault does not stop propagation,
// and stopPropagation does not prevent the default

// once: remove after firing - the reliable way to avoid a leak
window.addEventListener("resize", onResize, { once: true });

// and AbortController, for several listeners at once
const ac = new AbortController();
window.addEventListener("resize", onResize, { signal: ac.signal });
document.addEventListener("keydown", onKey,  { signal: ac.signal });
// ...later, one call removes both
ac.abort();

// options worth knowing
el.addEventListener("click", fn, { passive: true });   // cannot preventDefault,
                                                     // so scrolling is not
                                                     // blocked - use on
                                                     // touch/wheel handlers
el.addEventListener("click", fn, { signal: ac.signal, once: true });
el.addEventListener("click", fn, { capture: true });   // equivalent to true

preventDefault and stopPropagation solve different problems and one does not imply the other. And a delegated listener is almost always the better design: one handler instead of N, correct for elements added later, and no cleanup when those elements are removed.

Events travel capture → target → bubble, and useCapture selects the phase. Prefer delegation with closest() over one listener per element, guarding properly for nested containers. preventDefault cancels the default action, stopPropagation stops bubbling, and they are unrelated. Use an AbortSignal to remove several listeners in one call.

4.5 Forms, inputs and preventDefault

Reading a form's values is one property per control, with different names for different control types and two traps around checkboxes and file inputs. Getting the value shape wrong is the most common cause of "my form submits nothing".

Reading values, by control type

ControlReadsWhen nothing is chosen
<input>.value — always a string""
<textarea>.value""
<select> single.value — the selected option's value"" if the first option has value=""
<select multiple>selectedOptions — a collectionEmpty collection
checkbox.checked is boolean; .value is the label.value is still set even when unchecked
radioThe .value of the checked one.value is "" if none is checked
<form>No .value. It has elements, submit and resetn/a
const form = document.querySelector("#signup");
const { name, email, bio, country, interests, terms } = form.elements;
// form.elements is an HTMLFormControlsCollection, named by the
// `name` attribute - NOT by id

// the checkbox trap: .value exists whether or not it is checked
terms.checked;         // false - this is the boolean
terms.value;           // "on" - the default label, ALWAYS present
// so sending value="on" for an unchecked box is the classic bug

const data = {
  name,
  email,
  bio,
  country,
  interests: [...interests.selectedOptions].map(o => o.value),
  terms: terms.checked ? terms.value : undefined,   // omit when false
};

// radios: form.elements returns a RadioNodeList for a shared name
const size = form.elements.size.value;   // "" when none is checked

// and the correct way to serialise, which handles all of the above
const params = new URLSearchParams(new FormData(form));
// FormData is what the browser itself submits, so it is exactly right
// for a form that is going to be POSTed
params.get("email");
[...params.getAll("interests")];          // repeated names -> getAll
params.has("terms");
new FormData(form) includes a file input's File object with an empty filename when nothing is selected, and it includes every named control regardless of whether it was filled. Filtering it is the caller's job — the browser's own submission has the same behaviour, so the server must not assume a present key means a real value.
// preventDefault on the form, and the two submission routes
form.addEventListener("submit", async (e) => {
  e.preventDefault();                 // stop the native POST

  const btn = form.querySelector("[type=submit]");
  btn.disabled = true;                // stop a double submit
  try {
    const res = await fetch(form.action, {
      method: form.method,            // "get" or "post", from the markup
      body: new FormData(form),       // for post; a URLSearchParams for get
      headers: { Accept: "application/json" },
    });
    if (!res.ok) throw new Error(res.status);
    form.reset();
  } catch (err) {
    showError(err.message);           // and re-enable the button
  } finally {
    btn.disabled = false;
  }
});

// the requestSubmit path, which RUNS validation first
form.requestSubmit();        // fires validation, then fires `submit`
form.submit();               // does NOT: skips validation and the event
// calling .submit() bypasses your own submit handler entirely, which is
// why a form whose submit button is type="submit" but is handled in JS
// sometimes appears to do nothing

A native form submission still works with JavaScript disabled — it is a URL and the browser handles the encoding, the file upload and the validation. Intercepting it with preventDefault means taking all of that on, so use fetch only when you need the response handled in the page, and let the browser do it otherwise.

Validation, before submitting

// the Constraint Validation API, which the browser uses itself
form.checkValidity();          // boolean, no UI
form.reportValidity();        // shows the browser's own bubbles and focuses the
                              // first invalid field
form.elements.email.validity.valueMissing;      // boolean
form.elements.email.validity.typeMismatch;      // boolean
form.elements.email.validationMessage;          // the browser's text

// setCustomValidity for a rule no attribute can express
const email = form.elements.email;
email.addEventListener("blur", async () => {
  if (!email.validity.typeMismatch && email.value){
    const taken = await api.emailExists(email.value);
    email.setCustomValidity(taken ? "That address is already registered." : "");
  }
});
// and clear it on input, or it sticks for ever
email.addEventListener("input", () => email.setCustomValidity(""));

// novalidate: suppress the native bubbles and use your own
<form novalidate>

// and the two attributes worth knowing
<input formnovalidate>   <!-- skips validation when submitted -->
<button formmethod="post"> <!-- overrides the form's method -->
Call checkValidity() on submit, and on failure reportValidity() — it focuses the first invalid field, which a scroll-and-hope approach will not do for a keyboard or screen reader user. Validate on blur rather than on every keystroke: telling someone their email is invalid while they are on the first character is noise.

Read values from form.elements, named by the name attribute. A checkbox's .value is always present, so use .checked and omit the key when false. Use new FormData(form) for anything submitted, requestSubmit() rather than submit() so validation runs, and always re-enable the submit button in a finally.

Read values from form.elements, named by the name attribute. A checkbox's .value is always present, so use .checked and omit the key when false. Use new FormData(form) for anything being submitted, and requestSubmit() rather than submit() when triggering programmatically.

5 · Modern ES6+ Syntax

5.1 let, const and block scoping5.2 Destructuring and rest in action5.3 Template literals and tagged templates5.4 Optional chaining, nullish coalescing and logical assignment5.5 Property and method shorthands

Ties the modern syntax together: let and const, destructuring, template literals, optional chaining, and the assignment shorthands. These are the features you will type every single day. Together they make code shorter, clearer, and safer by removing boilerplate and whole classes of bugs.

ES6 and its successors added decades of convenience in one move. This chapter consolidates the everyday syntax — some of it already appeared in earlier chapters, here you get the complete picture.

5.1 let, const and block scoping

Three declaration forms, and the differences between them are about scope, about initialisation timing, and about the timing of a class of error that var converts into a silent wrong answer.

The three, side by side

Propertyvarletconst
ScopeFunctionBlockBlock
RedeclarableYesNoNo
Before declarationundefinedReferenceErrorReferenceError
ReassignableYesYesNo
Loop body per-iterationNoYesYes
Global propertyYes, in a scriptNoNo
Usable as a parameter nameYesYesNo
// block scoping, which is the reason to prefer let
{
  var leaked = 1;
}
leaked;                  // 1 - visible outside the block

{
  let scoped = 1;
}
scoped;                 // ReferenceError

// which matters most in loops, for closures
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));   // 3, 3, 3
}
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++){
  setTimeout(() => console.log(i));   // 0, 1, 2
}

// and for a plain shadowing bug
let x = "outer";
function f(){
  { let x = "inner"; }
  return x;            // "outer" - the inner x left with the block
}
function g(){
  { var x = "inner"; }
  return x;            // "inner" - var leaked
}

The temporal dead zone

A let or const binding exists from the top of its block but cannot be read until the declaration statement runs. Reading it in between is a ReferenceError, not undefined. That difference is the point: var turns a mistake into a silent wrong value, let turns it into a loud error at the line that caused it.

console.log(a);   // undefined - no error
var a = 1;

console.log(b);   // ReferenceError: Cannot access 'b' before
let b = 1;        // initialization
console.log(b);   // 1

// the practical case: a function declaration that shadows a module
// binding, and the TDZ makes the mistake obvious
run();
function run(){ console.log(total); }   // fine - hoisted, and total is
                                       // initialised by now

// the case that bites
const config = parse(cfgString);   // ReferenceError if config is used
                                   // in a default above its declaration
function setup({ port = config.port } = {}){}   // config is in its TDZ
                                                 // during the default

// hoisting, for all three
console.log(f);   // the function - declarations hoist fully
function f(){}

console.log(g);   // undefined - let exists but is not initialised
let g = 1;

console.log(h);   // ReferenceError
const h = 1;
// const prevents REASSIGNMENT, not mutation
const arr = [1, 2];
arr.push(3);      // fine - the array's contents changed
arr = [4];        // TypeError: Assignment to constant variable

const obj = { a: 1 };
obj.a = 2;        // fine
obj = {};         // TypeError

// Object.freeze is the actual immutability, and it is shallow
const frozen = Object.freeze({ a: 1 });
frozen.a = 2;     // no effect in strict mode
frozen.nested.b = 3;   // WORKS - the nested object is not frozen

// and the const rules that are not about assignment
// params, let/const, class names and imports are all const
function f(param){ }        // params are assignable - a silent surprise
for (const x of items){ }   // const per iteration, rebinding is an error
for (const [k, v] of map){ }
try { } catch (e){ }        // e is block-scoped, and can be redeclared
The reason to prefer const by default is not that it prevents bugs — a const object can still be mutated. It is that it states intent at the point of declaration, so a reader knows the binding is not meant to move, and so the reassignment that would be wrong becomes a TypeError rather than a state change nobody intended.

Choosing per declaration

SituationUseBecause
Anything at allconstThe default. A reassignment that was not intended becomes an error
Reassigned in the code belowletNot for the whole function — only for the lines that reassign
A loop variablelet for a counter, const in for…ofconst per iteration is the safer default
A function declarationfunctionIt hoists. A const arrow does not
A callbackconst arrowIt is never reassigned, and it has lexical this
Never, in new codevarFunction-scoped, leaks out of blocks, shares one binding per loop
// const first, let where reassigned, and the comment explains why
const MAX = 100;
let page = 1;
page += 1;              // reassigned, so let

// and the reassignment is visible where it happens, which is the
// actual benefit: reading the diff tells you what changes
function next(list, at){
  let index = at;
  index = Math.min(index + 1, list.length - 1);   // visible here
  return index;
}

// versus const, where the reader has to search for the write
function next2(list, at){
  return Math.min(at + 1, list.length - 1);      // no hidden state
}

var is function-scoped and leaks out of blocks and shares one binding across loop iterations; let and const are block-scoped and throw a ReferenceError if read before initialisation. const prevents reassignment, not mutation — use Object.freeze for that, and know it is shallow.

5.2 Destructuring and rest in action

Destructuring with defaults is the most compact way to express "take these values, falling back to these if they are missing", and it composes with parameter lists, loops and assignments. The one thing to internalise is that a default fires on undefined and on nothing else.

Defaults, and what triggers them

const { retries = 3 } = opts;   // undefined -> 3
const { retries = 3 } = { retries: 0 };   // 0 stays 0
const { retries = 3 } = { retries: null }; // null stays null

// so if null should also fall back, it needs ??
const { retries = 3 } = { retries: null ?? undefined };
// or normalise first, which is clearer
const r = opts.retries ?? 3;
const { retries = 3 } = { retries: r };

// renaming and defaulting together
const { timeout: ms = 1000, retries: n = 3 } = opts;

// a computed default, evaluated at destructuring time
const { id = crypto.randomUUID() } = {};

// and a default that is a function CALL - it runs only if needed
let calls = 0;
const expensive = () => { calls++; return 1; };
const { a = expensive(), b = expensive() } = {};
calls;   // 2 - both were undefined
const { c = expensive(), d = expensive() } = { a: 1, b: 2 };
calls;   // 4 - both were undefined
// destructuring in every position

// parameters - and the = {} that makes an omitted argument safe
function connect({ host, port = 5432, ssl = false } = {}){ ... }

// function arguments at the call site
const [first, second] = pair();
render({ title, subtitle: sub });

// assignment, not declaration
let x, y;
({ x, y } = { x: 1, y: 2 });     // the parentheses are required:
                                  // a leading { is parsed as a block

// for-of, which is the readable way to index an array
for (const [i, item] of items.entries()){ }
for (const [key, value] of Object.entries(obj)){ }
for (const [key] of map){ }              // the value is optional

// the swap, with no temporary
[a, b] = [b, a];

// nested, and each level is a real optional chain risk
const { data: { items = [] } = {} } = response;
//     ^ this = {} makes data optional
//                    ^ this = [] makes items optional

// and a default that is not a literal
const { limit = MAX_PER_PAGE, offset = 0 } = query;
Nest defaults when the data can genuinely be partial — an API response, a query string, an optional options object. Each = {} or = [] costs one expression and removes one TypeError from a failure path that would otherwise only appear when the response shape changed.
// what destructuring does NOT do
const { items } = response;          // does not deep-clone
items === response.items;           // true - same array

const [first] = [1, 2, 3];          // first is 1, not [1]
const [all] = [[1, 2, 3]];          // all is [1,2,3] - nested
const [a, , c] = [1, 2, 3];         // a=1, c=3 - the gap is skipped
const { a: { b } } = { a: {} };     // TypeError - no default on the inner

// renaming is not a rename of the source
const { name: n } = { name: "Ada" };
// the source key is still "name"; only the local binding is n

// and destructuring a string, a Map or a Set works because they
// are iterable - and an object is not
const { length } = "abc";    // 3
const [k, v] = new Map([["a", 1]]);   // "a", 1
const { x } = new Map();    // undefined - Map is not indexable by key

Where it earns its keep

Destructuring is not sugar for convenience. It is a way of saying what a value is made of, and it is the difference between a caller knowing the shape and a calleer having to document it.

// a function that takes what it needs, and the reader sees it
function resize({ width, height = width * 0.75, ratio = 16 / 9 }){
  return { width, height };
}
resize({ width: 1600 });       // height defaults from width

// versus six positional parameters, where the call site is a
// row of bare values and nothing says what they are
function resize2(w, h, r, fit, crop, bg){ ... }
resize2(1600, undefined, 1.77, "cover", true, "#000");

// three call sites with different subsets, no undefined holes
const { sort = "name", dir = "asc", page = 1, limit = 20 } = query;
load({ sort, dir });            // page and limit take their defaults
load({ page: 3 });              // the rest default

// and destructuring an API response at the boundary, once
const { data, error, meta: { total } = {} } = await api.list();
// from here on the rest of the code deals in plain values

The strongest case is a function with optional parameters. With positional arguments every optional one needs an undefined placeholder at every call site, and skipping the first optional parameter means passing undefined for all the ones before it. With a destructured object, options are named, order does not matter, and a call site reads as a set of choices.

// and the aliasing case, for a rename that says why
const {
  created_at: createdAt,
  is_admin: isAdmin,
  first_name: firstName,
} = user;
// the snake_case-to-camelCase rename happens ONCE, here, rather
// than at every one of forty use sites - and the names are greppable

// which also means the caller's shape and the internal shape can
// differ deliberately, without a renaming layer
function fromRow({
  id: userId,
  display_name: name,
  avatar_url: avatarUrl,
}){
  return { userId, name, avatarUrl };
}
// the boundary does the translation; nothing downstream knows
// the API used snake_case

A default fires only on undefined, so a legitimate 0, "" or null survives — use ?? if you want null to fall back too. Defaults nest, and one = {} per level is cheaper than a chain of optional chaining. Destructuring is shallow and never clones.

5.3 Template literals and tagged templates

A tagged template is a function called with the template's literal text and its interpolated values as separate arguments. It is the mechanism behind styled-component libraries, HTML sanitising and small domain-specific languages, and it needs no library of its own.

The signature

// the tag receives strings as an array-like with a .raw property
function tag(strings, ...values){
  strings.raw;    // the source text, unescaped
  strings[0];     // the cooked text, escapes processed
  values;         // the interpolated expressions, already evaluated
  return "any string";
}

tag`a${1}b${2}c`;
// strings = ["a", "b", "c"], values = [1, 2]

// so a naive implementation just joins them
const plain = (s, ...v) => s.reduce((out, part, i) => out + (v[i] ?? "") + part);
plain`a${1}b`;   // "a1b"

// and a sanitising one, which is the real use
const SAFE = Symbol("safeHtml");
function html(strings, ...values){
  return strings.reduce((out, s, i) => {
    const v = values[i];
    const rendered = Array.isArray(v)
      ? v.map(x => x.raw).join("")     // a nested tag, already escaped
      : v && v[SAFE] ? String(v)    // explicitly trusted
      : escapeHtml(String(v));
    return out + rendered + s;
  });
}
const trust = (s) => ({ [SAFE]: true, toString: () => s });

html`<p>${userInput}</p>`;          // escaped
html`<p>${trust(authorBio)}</p>`;     // not escaped, deliberately
PartIsCan contain
strings[i]The cooked literalAnything, with escapes resolved
strings.raw[i]The source textEscape sequences written literally
values[i]The evaluated expressionAnything. Nested arrays for nested tags
Values arrive already evaluated, and a nested tag arrives as an array in values, not a string. A sanitising tag that does String(v) on a nested tag will emit a,b,c — the array joined by commas — instead of the joined markup. Check Array.isArray before stringifying.

What it is actually for

// 1. a tiny CSS-in-JS, with no runtime library
function css(strings, ...values){
  return strings.raw
    .reduce((acc, s, i) => acc + s + (i < values.length ? values[i] : ""), "")
    .replace(/--([w-]+)/g, (_, name) => values[0]?.[name] ?? "");
}
const btn = css`
  background: var(--bg);
  color: var(--fg);
`;

// 2. an HTML escaper, as above

// 3. a query builder, where the tag enforces the parameter style
function sql(strings, ...values){
  return strings.reduce((out, s, i) => {
    if (i === 0) return s;
    return out + "?" + i + s;      // a placeholder, never an interpolation
  }, "");
}
sql`select * from u where a = ${x} and b = ${y}`;
// "select * from u where a = ?1 and b = ?2" - safe by construction,
// because a VALUE can never reach the query text

// 4. a validation tag that reports, rather than throws
function validate(spec){
  return (strings, ...values) => {
    for (const [i, v] of values.entries()){
      const rule = spec[i];
      if (rule && !rule(v)) throw new TypeError(strings[i]);
    }
    return values.join(strings[0]);
  };
}
const checked = validate([isUser, isString]);

The SQL example is the strongest argument for tagged templates: because a tag receives values and text separately, it can emit a placeholder for each value. No value can reach the query text, so the injection is impossible by construction rather than by escaping — which is the difference between a rule and a mitigation.

A complete HTML tag, with the escaping that makes it safe

const ENT = { "&": "amp", "<": "lt", ">": "gt",
              '"': "quot", "'": "#39" };
const esc = (s) => String(s).replace(/[&<>"']/g, (c) => "&" + ENT[c] + ";");

const SAFE = Symbol("safeHtml");
const trust = (s) => ({ [SAFE]: true, toString: () => String(s) });

function html(strings, ...values){
  return strings.reduce((out, s, i) => {
    const v = values[i];
    let rendered;
    if (Array.isArray(v)){
      // a NESTED TAG: its parts were already escaped
      rendered = v.reduce((a, str, j) => a + str + (v.values[j] ?? ""), "");
    } else if (v && v[SAFE]){
      rendered = String(v);              // explicitly trusted
    } else if (v == null){
      rendered = "";
    } else {
      rendered = esc(v);                 // everything else
    }
    return out + rendered + s;
  }, "");
}

// and the nested case, which is the part that is easy to get wrong
const list = html`
  <ul>
    ${items.map(i => html`<li>${i.name}</li>`)}
  </ul>
`;
A tag that builds HTML is a small security boundary and it has to be right, not nearly right. The rule that matters: the literal text is trusted because you wrote it, and every value is escaped unless it is a nested tag — which escaped itself — or a value you have deliberately marked trusted. There is no third case, and no path where a reader's input reaches the literal text.

A tag receives the literal parts and the values separately, which is why it can escape values and leave literals alone — a guarantee string concatenation cannot give. Handle a nested tag as an array, escape everything else, and treat "trusted" as something you opt into explicitly rather than something inferred.

A tag receives the literal string parts and the interpolated values as separate arguments, so it can transform, escape or validate rather than concatenate — and a value can never reach the literal text. Check for a nested tag, which arrives as an array, before stringifying.

5.4 Optional chaining, nullish coalescing and logical assignment

Three operators added for working with data whose shape you do not control. Two of them are safe; the third one is a bug magnet, and knowing why is the way to never write it.

Optional chaining, and what it short-circuits

const res = await fetchUser();

res?.profile?.name;          // undefined if res or profile is nullish
res.profile?.name;           // fine - profile may be nullish

// it short-circuits the WHOLE remaining chain
obj?.a.b.c;                  // undefined if obj is nullish - never throws
obj.a?.b.c;                  // throws if obj is nullish

// and the call, and the index, and the assignment
getUser()?.notify?.();
list?.[0];
obj?.count = 5;              // no-op if obj is nullish, no throw

// it also short-circuits a DELETE - which is the surprise
obj?.handler = null;         // reads first: throws if obj is nullish
delete obj?.handler;         // safe: only deletes if obj exists
delete obj.a?.b;             // throws if obj is nullish
WrittenEvaluates
a?.ba.b if a is not nullish, else undefined
a?.b.cThe whole chain, short-circuited
a.b?.cThrows if a.b throws first
a?.b()Calls only if a.b is a function and not nullish
a?.[0]Index access, conditionally
a?.b = 1Throws if a is nullish
delete a?.bSafe: short-circuits
obj?.count = 5 throws rather than silently doing nothing, because the left-hand side of an assignment is read before it is written. Only the optional chain in the read position short-circuits. If you need conditional assignment, test for the object first, or use ??=, which does short-circuit.

The three ways to use it wrongly

// 1. optional chaining to avoid a null check that should be an
//    assertion - this hides the bug rather than handling it
if (user?.isAdmin && user.permissions.includes("delete")){ }
if (!user) throw new Error("not signed in");   // better: fail loudly

// 2. ?. to silence a function that should never return nullish
processFirst(getList()?.items);   // if getList() can be nullish, this
                                   // silently skips. Decide which.

// 3. ?. inside a chain where an early link CAN throw
session.user.preferences.theme;      // throws if session.user is undefined
session?.user?.preferences.theme;    // safe - but is that right?
// or
session.user?.preferences.theme;     // throws on session.user
// the ? must go on EVERY link from the root
// ?? and ||=, which are the assignment forms
let n;
n ??= compute();        // only evaluates compute() if n is nullish
n ||= compute();        // evaluates if n is FALSY - so 0 recomputes

const cfg = {};
cfg.theme ??= "dark";   // sets only if absent or null
cfg.theme ||= "dark";   // also overwrites "" and 0

// and the numeric pitfall that ?? was made for
let retries = 0;
retries ||= 3;    // 3 - WRONG, 0 was intentional
retries ??= 3;    // 0 - correct

// and short-circuit assignment, which does not throw on nullish
obj?.count ??= 5;       // TypeError: the LHS read still throws
// so conditional assignment needs ??= on the object itself:
const o = obj ?? {};
o.count ??= 5;

Optional chaining is for data of uncertain shape — an API response, a deeply nested config, a possibly-absent DOM node. It is not for avoiding an error you should be handling explicitly. If a nullish value means the program is in an invalid state, throw, and let the catch be the handler.

?. against a chain of checks

SituationUse
One optional linka?.b
Several, and any may be absenta?.b?.c?.d, on every link
A default when absenta?.b ?? fallback — note ??, not ||
Absence is a bugThrow. Do not use ?.
Absence is expected and has a fallback??
Absence means use a different branchAn if, not a chain of ?.
The object may be replaced entirely??= on the binding, not ?.
// the pattern to prefer: normalise once, then read plainly
const raw = await getSettings();
const { theme = "system", density = "comfortable", fontSize = 16 } = raw ?? {};
// three defaults, no optional chaining, and `raw` being nullish is
// handled in one place rather than at every read

// where ?. is genuinely right: a walk through unknown shape
const city = response?.data?.address?.city ?? "Unknown";

// and where it is wrong
function render(user){
  if (user?.isAdmin){ }              // hides a missing user
  if (!user) throw new Error("no user");   // states the invariant
  if (user.isAdmin){ }
}

// the assignment trap, restated
let state = null;
state?.count = 5;      // TypeError: the LHS is READ first
state = state ?? {};
state.count = 5;       // correct

// and ??= which does short-circuit
state ??= { count: 5 };
state.count ??= 10;    // only if count was absent

Optional chaining in a long expression is a signal that the data's shape is unclear. Destructuring with defaults at the top of a function states the shape once and makes every line below it unambiguous — which is both shorter and easier to change.

Put ?. on every link from the root when guarding a chain, but never on the left of an assignment, which throws. Use ?? for defaults so a legitimate 0 survives, ??= to assign only when absent, and throw rather than chaining ?. when absence means the program is in an invalid state.

?. short-circuits the whole chain, so put it on every link from the root — but not on the left of an assignment, which throws. Use ?? for defaults rather than || so a legitimate 0 survives, and use ??= to assign only when a value is absent.

5.5 Property and method shorthands

The modern object and class syntaxes exist to remove repetition at the point where repetition is most likely — and several of them remove more repetition than they first appear to.

The shorthand forms

// property shorthand
const name = "Ada", age = 36;
const o1 = { name, age };                  // { name: name, age: age }

// method shorthand - NOT an arrow, so this is dynamic
const o2 = {
  greet(){ return `Hello, ${this.name}`; },
  arrow: () => typeof this,             // lexical
};

// computed keys
const key = "role";
const o3 = { [key]: "admin", [`p${1}`]: true };

// and the destructuring-shaped one, which is not a shorthand
// at all but looks like one: { ...rest } is a rest, not a spread

// getters and setters, with a private backing variable
const temp = {
  #celsius = 0,
  get fahrenheit(){ return this.#celsius * 9 / 5 + 32; },
  set fahrenheit(v){ this.#celsius = (v - 32) * 5 / 9; },
  // a getter with no setter makes the property read-only
};
temp.fahrenheit = 100;      // TypeError in strict mode
temp.fahrenheit;            // 100

// static, and the two static blocks
class Registry {
  static #instances = 0;    // a private STATIC - one per class
  static {
    // runs once, when the class is defined. No `this`.
    this.version = "1.0";
  }
  #data = new Map();
  constructor(){ Registry.#instances++; }
  static get count(){ return Registry.#instances; }
}
// class fields, and what they are not
class Point {
  x = 0;                    // an INSTANCE field, set in the constructor
  static origin = { x: 0, y: 0 };   // a STATIC field
  #hidden = 1;              // truly private - not reachable outside

  // field initialisers run in declaration order, BEFORE the constructor
  // body but AFTER super(). So this is safe:
  constructor(x, y){
    super();
    this.x = x;             // overwrites the field
  }
}

// and the trap: a field declared after a method that reads it
class Broken {
  method(){ return this.later; }   // if called from the constructor,
                                   // `later` is not yet initialised
  later = 1;
}

// #private is enforced by the engine. There is no way to reach it
// from outside - not via bracket notation, not via JSON, not via
// Object.getOwnPropertyNames. A WeakMap or a closure is the older
// equivalent, and slower.
A class field holding an arrow function creates one closure per instance, which is the cost of the convenience: a bound method that survives being passed as a callback, in exchange for memory proportional to the number of instances. For a class with thousands of instances and a method that is only called as obj.method(), a prototype method is the better shape.
// destructuring in, and what it saves
class Server {
  constructor({ host, port = 80, secure = false, headers = {} } = {}){
    this.host = host;
    this.port = port;
    this.secure = secure;
    this.headers = headers;
  }
}
// versus six positional parameters, where only the reader knows
// what the third one is

// and the optional chain plus nullish on a field
class Config {
  #raw = {};
  get timeout(){ return this.#raw?.network?.timeout ?? 1000; }
  get retries(){ return this.#raw?.network?.retries ?? 3; }
  // no constructor needed - the field initialiser is enough
}

// a private method, for something only the class calls internally
class Account {
  #validate(amount){
    if (amount < 0) throw new RangeError("negative");
  }
  withdraw(n){ this.#validate(n); this.balance -= n; }
}

Every one of these shorthand forms is doing the same thing: moving a name that appears twice to appear once. That is the value — not fewer characters, but one fewer place for the two to disagree. It is the same argument that makes const worth using for a binding that is not reassigned.

Class bodies, in full

FormKeywordWhere it lives
Instance fieldnoneOwn property, per instance, set before the constructor body
Static fieldstaticOwn property of the constructor
Private field#nameOwn property, unreachable from outside
GettergetThe prototype. No own property until assigned
MethodnoneThe prototype
Private method#nameThe prototype, unreachable from outside
Static methodstaticThe constructor
Computed method[name]The prototype, chosen at class definition
Initialiser blocknoneRuns per instance, in order
Static blockstatic {}Runs once, when the class is defined
class Queue {
  static #count = 0;
  #items = [];

  static {
    // once, at definition. `this` is the class.
    Queue.capacity = 1000;
    globalThis.registerQueue ??= new Map();
  }

  constructor(cap = 10){
    this.cap = cap;
    Queue.#count++;
    globalThis.registerQueue.set(this.id ??= Symbol(), this);
  }

  push(item){
    if (this.#items.length >= this.cap) throw new RangeError("full");
    this.#items.push(item);
    return this.size;
  }

  get size(){ return this.#items.length; }
  set size(v){ throw new TypeError("size is read-only"); }

  *[Symbol.iterator](){ yield* this.#items; }   // spreadable
