  static get count(){ return Queue.#count; }

  #isFull(){ return this.#items.length >= this.cap; }
}

A getter with no matching setter makes a property read-only, and a setter that throws makes it read-only with an explanation. Together they are more expressive than Object.freeze on a single property, because the freeze applies at once and silently, while a pair of accessors can validate.

Every one of these shorthand forms removes a duplicated name, which removes a place for the two to disagree. Class fields run per instance before the constructor body, static { } once at definition, methods on the prototype. A field holding an arrow is bound but costs a closure per instance.

Property, method, computed-key and getter/setter shorthand all remove a duplicated name, which removes a place for the two to disagree. Class fields run per instance before the constructor body; static { } runs once at definition. A field holding an arrow is bound but costs a closure per instance, and #private is enforced by the engine rather than by convention.

6 · Modules & Tooling

6.1 ES modules: import and export6.2 Default vs named exports6.3 Dynamic import6.4 npm and package.json6.5 Bundlers, and Node vs browser

Splits code across files with ES modules and introduces the npm toolchain around JavaScript projects. Modules give dependencies explicit names, while tooling installs packages and runs scripts. In practice you export from one file, import in another, and orchestrate builds with npm commands.

Real projects are many files. ES modules make the splits explicit, npm manages the packages, and bundlers turn it all into code the browser can load.

6.1 ES modules: import and export

An ES module has its own top-level scope, is always strict, and is deferred by default. Those three facts are the whole difference from a classic script, and each one removes a category of bug.

What a module is

// modules.mjs
const secret = "not exported";     // module-scoped
export const shared = "exported";

// two classic scripts doing the same thing
<script src="a.js"></script>   // var leaked = 1  -> window.leaked
<script src="b.js"></script>   // function f(){} -> window.f

// so in a module:
// - every top-level binding is module-scoped, never on window
// - the code is always strict mode
// - it is deferred, so the DOM is parsed before it runs
// - top-level await is allowed, because of the deferral

// which also means a module cannot see a page's globals
window.someVar;      // ReferenceError inside a module
// and the reverse: inline scripts cannot see a module's scope
// named exports - explicit, and the form to prefer
export const VERSION = "1.0";
export function helper(){ }
export class Widget { }
export { helper as help, Widget as W };      // renamed on export
const internal = 1;
export { internal };

// importing
import { helper, Widget as W } from "./m.mjs";
import { VERSION as v } from "./m.mjs";
import * as everything from "./m.mjs";
everything.helper();
everything.VERSION;

// the namespace object is frozen and has a Symbol.toStringTag
Object.isFrozen(everything);        // true
String(everything);                 // "[object Module]"

// re-exporting, which is how a barrel file works
// index.mjs
export { helper } from "./a.mjs";
export { Widget } from "./b.mjs";
export * from "./c.mjs";            // everything from c, except its default
export * as c from "./c.mjs";       // the namespace under one name

// import attributes, for non-JS files
import data from "./data.json" with { type: "json" };
import sheet from "./s.csv" with { type: "csv" };
A module cannot be loaded from file://. ES module fetches use CORS semantics, and the origin of a local file is opaque, so import fails silently with a console error and nothing happens. Every page in this library is therefore classic scripts rather than modules — a deliberate choice, because the library is meant to open by double-clicking.
// the live binding, which surprises people
// counter.mjs
export let count = 0;
export function inc(){ count++; }

// app.mjs
import { count } from "./counter.mjs";
inc();
console.log(count);      // 1 - the binding is live, not a copy

// this works only for let, not const, and not for primitives
// re-assigned later through a function

// and the import order - a module graph, evaluated depth-first
// with cycles handled by the TDZ
// a.mjs imports b.mjs; b.mjs imports a.mjs
// a.mjs starts, hits the import, b.mjs evaluates, which hits
// the import of a.mjs - a.mjs is already in progress, so it
// sees the module record but its bindings are in the TDZ.
// Using a.mjs's export at that moment throws. Using it after
// b.mjs finishes is fine. So: keep cycles acyclic.

Cycles, and the TDZ they hit

A cycle is legal and the evaluation order is defined, which is precisely what makes it dangerous: one module's bindings exist but are uninitialised while the other runs, and touching one throws rather than returning undefined.

// a.mjs
import { bValue } from "./b.mjs";
export const aValue = "a";
console.log(bValue);      // fine if b.mjs finished

// b.mjs
import { aValue } from "./a.mjs";
export const bValue = "b";
console.log(aValue);      // ReferenceError if a.mjs is mid-evaluation

The order is depth-first from the entry: a.mjs starts, hits its import, b.mjs starts, hits its import of a.mjs, finds it already in progress and reads the module record — whose bindings are still in their temporal dead zone. The module is available; the bindings are not.

// the fix: one direction. Put the shared thing in a third module.
// shared.mjs
export const VERSION = "1.0";

// a.mjs and b.mjs both import from shared.mjs and never from
// each other. No cycle, no TDZ surprise.

// the other fix: make the import lazy, so the cycle is not on
// the evaluation path
b.mjs
import("./a.mjs").then(m => m.aValue);
// a dynamic import inside a function body is evaluated when
// called, not at module evaluation, so the cycle resolves

// and in a bundler, a cycle usually still works but can defeat
// tree-shaking, because the analysis cannot order the two
// modules' side effects
// the file:// constraint, which decides this library's shape
// script type="module" src="app.mjs"
//   -> Failed to load module script: Expected a JavaScript module
//      script but the server responded with a MIME type of
//      text/html     (or a CORS error, on a file:// origin)

// so: classic script, deferred, no import, no top-level await
<script src="assets/shell.js" defer></script>

// and no fetch of a local file either
fetch("data.json");   // blocked on a file:// origin

// which is why the guide data lives in the markup and the theme
// lives in a stylesheet the browser parses normally

A module is module-scoped, always strict, and always deferred. Imports are live bindings, so import { count } reflects later changes. A cycle is legal but one module's bindings sit in the TDZ while the other evaluates — break it with a third module or a lazy import. And a module cannot load from file://, which is why this library uses classic scripts.

A module is module-scoped, always strict, and always deferred — which is why top-level await works. Prefer named exports so the import site shows what it needs, and remember imports are live bindings rather than copies. A module cannot load from file://, which is why this library uses classic scripts.

6.2 Default vs named exports

A default export is the one export a module offers without naming it. The choice is between naming both things and giving up tree-shaking, or naming one thing and accepting an unnamed import.

The four forms

FormExampleUse for
Named onlyexport function a(){}The default. Explicit, greppable, tree-shakeable
Default onlyexport default function a(){}One thing, the module's purpose
Default anonymousexport default function(){}Almost never. The name is lost to stack traces and tooling
Bothexport default x; export { x as named };When a consumer may want a name
// named - the form to prefer
export function parse(input){ ... }
import { parse } from "./parser.mjs";   // the name is here

// default - fine for a single thing
export default class Widget { }
import Widget from "./widget.mjs";

// default anonymous loses the name
export default function(){ }
import fn from "./fn.mjs";
fn.name;      // "default" - useless in a stack trace, and some
              // tooling cannot show the source

// default CANNOT be re-exported by export *
export * from "./m.mjs";        // does NOT include the default
export { default } from "./m.mjs";   // explicit, and the only way

// and a default cannot be imported under two names
import Button from "./button.mjs";
import Btn from "./button.mjs";   // two identifiers, one binding

Tree-shaking, and what defeats it

Tree-shaking removes exports nothing imports, and it needs the export to be statically analysable. A default export of a call is not analysable, which is why a barrel file with default exports often ships everything.

// shaken out - a named function nothing imports is dropped
export function used(){}
export function unused(){}

// NOT shaken out - the module's shape is unknown at build time
export default buildSomething({ a: 1, b: 2 });
// or
export default { parse, format, validate };   // all three, always

// the barrel file that defeats it
// index.mjs
export * from "./a.mjs";
export * from "./b.mjs";
import { parse } from "./index.mjs";
// a bundler must load a.mjs AND b.mjs to know what index exports,
// so b.mjs's unused exports may still end up in the bundle

// the direct import, which is analysable
import { parse } from "./a.mjs";
// and the deep path, which some teams prefer for exactly this reason
Do not re-export a namespace object from a barrel file. export * as ns from "./x.mjs" is convenient and it defeats analysis, because the bundler cannot know which properties of the namespace the consumer will use.

Named exports also make a rename findable. A default import can be renamed at any import site without the tool complaining, and nothing connects the two names. That matters most in a library with hundreds of consumers, where grep for a name is the mechanism that keeps a rename safe.

Why the name matters

// the name survives into things you will read in an error
export default function parseConfig(s){ ... }
// stack trace: "at parseConfig (parser.mjs:12:9)"

// versus
export default function(){ ... }
// stack trace: "at parser.mjs:12:9"   - and in some minifiers,
//                                     at anonymous.mjs:12:9

// which matters most in an error you did not write
TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined
    at parser.mjs:12:9          // where?
    at Object.load (app.mjs:40:2)

// and Function.name works from a default export, which means a
// name is available for debugging even without the declaration
const m = await import("./parser.mjs");
m.default.name;   // "parseConfig" - preserved, unlike a classic default

// but an anonymous default loses it at the point of definition
export default function(){ ... }
(await import("./p.mjs")).default.name;   // "default"

Naming a default export is nearly free and it buys a legible stack trace, a Function.name that survives minification, and a hook for dev tools that group by function name. The cost is one identifier that is otherwise unused, and an unused-export lint rule is the usual reason it gets dropped — which is worth overriding for this case.

Prefer named exports: the import site shows what it needs, the name survives into stack traces and survives minification, and a rename is greppable across every consumer. A default export is fine for a module whose whole purpose is one thing — and note that export * never includes a default.

Prefer named exports: the import site shows what it needs, the name survives into stack traces, and a rename is greppable. A default export of an anonymous function loses its name entirely. Note that export * never includes a default, and that it is what usually defeats tree-shaking.

6.3 Dynamic import

Dynamic import loads a module at runtime and returns a promise for its namespace object. It is the one way to split code by need rather than by declaration, and the loading strategy is part of the call.

The call

// returns a promise for the module NAMESPACE, so .default for a default
const mod = await import("./parser.mjs");
mod.parse("{...}");
mod.default("{...}");

// the specifier is resolved at runtime, so a variable works
const lang = userSetting;
const m = await import(`./i18n/${lang}.mjs`);
// bundlers can follow this with a static pattern: the directory
// is known, so every file in it becomes a chunk

// and it can be non-lazy, for a module already in the graph
const eagerly = await import("./always-needed.mjs");

// in CommonJS, the whole namespace
const ns = await import("./m.cjs");
ns.named;  ns.default;
// and the pattern it exists for: a button that loads code on click
document.querySelector("#export").addEventListener("click", async () => {
  const { toCsv } = await import("./export.mjs");
  download(toCsv(rows));
});
// nothing is fetched until the reader clicks, and export.mjs never
// has to be in the critical path

// with a guard, because the reader can click twice
let loading = null;
async function getEditor(){
  loading ??= import("./editor.mjs");
  return loading;              // ??= so a second click reuses the promise
}
const { Editor } = await getEditor();

// and a deliberate failure path, since it can now fail
try {
  const { heavy } = await import("./heavy.mjs");
  heavy();
} catch (err) {
  showMessage("That part is unavailable offline.");
}
// a dynamic import CAN fail: a network error, or a parse error in
// the fetched file - neither of which can happen at build time

The second argument

StrategyFetchesUse
defaultImmediately on importNormal use
"lazy"Nothing until the module is actually usedAlways correct, and supported for the Web platform
// prefetching, so the click is instant later
<link rel="modulepreload" href="/js/editor.mjs">
<link rel="prefetch" href="/js/editor.mjs" as="script">
// and in script:
import("./editor.mjs");            // warm it during idle time

// a visibility-based strategy, which is a hint
<script type="speculationrules">
{
  "prefetch": [{
    "eagerness": "moderate",
    "where": { "href_matches": "/*/api/*" }
  }]
}
</script>
// eagerness: immediate, moderate (on interaction), conservative (on hover
// or pointerdown), and nothing else

// a "while visible" pattern, done by hand
const observer = new IntersectionObserver((entries) => {
  for (const e of entries){
    if (e.isIntersecting){ import("./heavy-widget.mjs"); observer.disconnect(); }
  }
});
observer.observe(document.querySelector("#lower-half"));

Dynamic import resolves relative to the module doing the importing, not relative to the document. A classic script on the page uses the document's base URL, so a path that works in a module fails in an inline script and vice versa. That difference accounts for most "my import path works in dev and not in production".

The base URL, which is the real gotcha

A dynamic import resolves its specifier against the URL of the module doing the importing — not against the document, and not against the importing file's directory on disk. That is right in almost every deployment and wrong in exactly the cases below.

// a module at /js/app.mjs doing this:
//   import("./editor.mjs")
// resolves to /js/editor.mjs   - correct

// a CLASSIC script in the page doing the same:
//   import("./editor.mjs")
// resolves against the document URL, so at /index.html it becomes
// /editor.mjs - NOT /js/editor.mjs

// so the same expression means different things in the two contexts,
// and moving a file between them silently breaks it

// the fix, when you cannot control the context
const base = new URL("./", import.meta.url);   // modules only
await import(new URL("./editor.mjs", base));
// import.meta.url is the module's own URL - it does not exist in
// a classic script at all

// and a <base href> changes the document's resolution
<base href="https://cdn.example/app/">
// ...and now every relative import in a classic script resolves
// under the CDN, which is occasionally the intent and usually a bug
// prefetching without the speculation rules API
function warm(path){
  const link = document.createElement("link");
  link.rel = "prefetch";
  link.as = "script";
  link.href = path;
  document.head.append(link);
}

// and warming on intent rather than on idle, which is more accurate
document.querySelector("#export")
  .addEventListener("pointerenter", () => warm("./export.mjs"), { once: true });
// one fetch on hover, then the click is instant, and on a touch
// device pointerenter fires at tap time so there is no wasted fetch

// the tradeoff, stated plainly
// nothing prefetched: first click waits for the network
// everything prefetched: the reader downloads code they may never run
// prefetch on intent: one wasted request for a reader who hovers
// and leaves - which is the right trade for anything expensive
// and the wrong one for anything small

Dynamic import returns a promise for the module namespace — use .default for a default export. It can now fail, so handle the rejection, and cache the promise rather than the result. The specifier resolves against the module doing the importing, which differs between a module and a classic script.

Dynamic import returns a promise for the module's namespace object, so use .default for a default export. It can now fail — a network or parse error — so handle the rejection. Cache the promise rather than the result if a second call is possible, and use modulepreload or a speculation rule to make the first click instant.

6.4 npm and package.json

package.json is a manifest and a resolution record. The fields that matter for a browser application are few, and the ones people get wrong are the version range and the module type.

The fields

FieldPurposeNotes
nameThe package identifierLowercase, no spaces. Scoped as @org/name
versionSemVer1.2.3 — MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH
type"module" makes .js an ES moduleThe single most consequential field for a browser project
mainThe CommonJS entry pointFor Node. "type": "module" changes the default
moduleThe ESM entry pointNon-standard, widely honoured by bundlers
exportsThe modern resolution mapReplaces main and browser
sideEffectsWhether importing has effectsfalse lets a bundler drop unused imports
filesWhat is publishedA whitelist. Everything else is excluded
enginesSupported Node versionsAdvisory unless engine-strict is set
dependenciesRuntime dependenciesInstalled in production too
devDependenciesBuild and test onlyNot installed in production
{
  "name": "acme-app",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "type": "module",

  "exports": {
    ".":        "./src/index.js",
    "./parser": "./src/parser.js",
    "./internal/*": "./src/internal/*.js",
    "./package.json": "./package.json"
  },
  "sideEffects": false,

  "scripts": {
    "start": "vite",
    "build": "vite build",
    "test":  "node --test"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "vite": "^6.0.0"
  }
}
// the field that decides whether .js is ESM or CJS
// absent          -> .js is CommonJS, .mjs is ESM
// "type": "module" -> .js is ESM,     .cjs is CommonJS

// so this file is an ES module:
{ "type": "module" }        import x from "./x.js";     // works
{}                         import x from "./x.js";     // FAILS - must use
                                                          // require(), or
                                                          // rename to .mjs

// and it is inherited by every .js in the package AND by any
// package without its own package.json, so a nested package
// needs its own to opt out of it

// version ranges
"^1.2.3"    >=1.2.3 <2.0.0   // the usual choice
"~1.2.3"    >=1.2.3 <1.3.0
"1.2.3"     exactly
">=1.2.3"   no upper bound
"latest"    a tag, not a range - never in a manifest

// and why ^ is right: a major version bump is the signal that
// something breaks, and it is the only signal
Adding "type": "module" to an existing package is a breaking change for Node: every .js file that used require stops working, including inside dependencies that were never converted. Add it to a new package, or convert the files and rename them to .cjs in one commit.

The exports map, which is the modern field

EntryResolves toFor
"."The package rootimport x from "pkg"
"./parser"One subpathimport { p } from "pkg/parser"
"./internal/*"A patternAny file under internal
"./package.json"The manifest itselfExplicitly re-exported, because exports blocks everything else by default
anything unlistedBlockedWhich is the point — it makes the public API explicit
{
  "exports": {
    ".": {
      "types": "./dist/index.d.ts",
      "browser": "./dist/browser.js",
      "node":    "./dist/node.js",
      "default": "./dist/index.js"
    },
    "./parser": "./dist/parser.js",
    "./package.json": "./package.json"
  }
}
// the keys are CONDITIONS, tried in order: the first whose
// environment matches wins. "default" is the fallback.

{
  "exports": {
    ".": {
      "import":  "./dist/esm.mjs",
      "require": "./dist/cjs.cjs"
    }
  }
}
// the two that matter for a dual package, and why the filenames
// must differ: a .cjs file is CommonJS even under "type":"module",
// and a .mjs file is ESM even under "type":"commonjs"
The exports field being a whitelist is the useful part. Before it, anything in the package was importable by path, so every file was effectively public whether or not it was meant to be. With it, adding an entry is a deliberate act, and a consumer who reaches into pkg/internal/x gets an error rather than an undocumented dependency.

"type": "module" decides whether .js is ESM and is inherited by everything without its own manifest. Prefer exports over main — it whitelists the public API and lets you pick a different file per environment. Use ^ for dependencies so a major bump is the only breaking signal.

"type": "module" decides whether .js is an ES module, and it is inherited by everything without its own manifest. Prefer exports over main, set sideEffects: false so unused imports can be dropped, and use ^ for dependencies so a major bump is the only breaking signal you get.

6.5 Bundlers, and Node vs browser

A bundler resolves the module graph, transforms source, and emits output. For a library that must open from a disk, the relevant question is not which bundler is best — it is whether you need one at all.

What a bundler actually does

StageWork
ResolutionFollow import through node_modules, honouring exports and conditions
TransformTypeScript, JSX, Sass, and syntax the target does not support
Tree-shakingRemove exports nothing imports — needs static analysis
ChunkingSplit into files, and decide what is shared
EmittingMinify, hash, and optionally inject into HTML
// the classic: an import graph, flattened into one file
// src/main.js
import { parse } from "./parser.js";
import { render } from "./view.js";
render(parse(data));

// after a bundler, one file, no module syntax
(function(){ ... })();

// the modern: a graph, split along the boundaries that matter
// entry.js -> [a.mjs, b.mjs]
// a.mjs   -> [shared.mjs]      <- the shared chunk is emitted once
// b.mjs   -> [shared.mjs]
// and shared.mjs is fetched once and cached by both

// dynamic import becomes a separate chunk, loaded on demand
button.onclick = () => import("./heavy.mjs").then(m => m.run());
NeedAnswer
Open by double-clicking from a diskNo bundler. ES modules cannot load from file://
Write TypeScript or JSXA build step, but the output can still be plain classic scripts
Bundle npm dependencies for the browserA bundler, and the output served over HTTP
MinifyA minifier alone. Not a bundler
Hash and cache-bustPart of a build step
TestA test runner. Not a bundler
The reason this library has no build step is that it is opened from a disk. CMD.bat runs start, which opens index.html over file://, and a file:// origin is opaque — so import, fetch and XHR are all blocked by CORS. A bundler producing a single classic script is the only thing that survives that, and the alternative is a server, which changes what the library is.
// the shape that works from a disk
<script src="assets/shell.js" defer></script>
<!-- no type="module", no import, no fetch of local files -->
<!-- two files, one stylesheet, one script, and it works
     from C:, from a USB stick, and from a web server -->

// and what would break it
<script type="module" src="app.mjs"></script>   // CORS: blocked
fetch("data.json");                                   // CORS: blocked
const xhr = new XMLHttpRequest(); xhr.open("GET","d.json");  // blocked
new Worker("w.js");                                   // blocked
const m = await import("./lazy.mjs");                  // blocked

// which is why the data is inline in the markup rather than
// loaded, and why every value is a token in shell.css rather
// than a fetched stylesheet

What to use instead, and the trade each makes

NeedToolOutputTrade
No build at allNothingThe sourceNo TypeScript, no npm in the browser, but works from file://
TypeScript onlytscPlain .js, classicCompilation and type checking only. No bundling
Minify onlyA minifierThe same file, smallerSmallest change. Keeps the module graph intact
A dependency graphesbuild, rollup, webpackBundlesBreaks file://
ESM served over HTTPA bundler or noneModulesWorks in every modern browser; needs a server
A progressive web appA bundlerManifest, service workerNecessarily over HTTPS
// what TypeScript alone emits, and it is enough for a lot of sites
// source
export interface User { name: string }
export function greet(u: User): string { return `Hi ${u.name}`; }

// after tsc with target ES2020 and module ES2020
export function greet(u) { return `Hi ${u.name}`; }
// the types are gone, the code is identical, and nothing was bundled

// the "two tools, not one" arrangement:
//   tsc --watch        compiles on change
//   a minifier         rewrites the compiled output
//   a static server    serves the directory
// ...and no module syntax appears anywhere, so it works from disk
// with an extra step, or from a server without one

A bundler resolves, transforms, tree-shakes, chunks and emits. The question here is whether one is needed: this library opens from file://, where import, fetch, XHR and Worker are all blocked, so the output must be classic scripts with inline data. tsc alone gives types without bundling, which covers a lot of cases.

A bundler resolves, transforms, tree-shakes, chunks and emits. The relevant question here is whether one is needed at all: this library opens from file://, where import and fetch are both blocked by CORS, so the output must be classic scripts with inline data. A single <script defer> and a stylesheet is the shape that satisfies it.

7 · Asynchronous JavaScript

7.1 Call stack, event loop, microtasks and macrotasks7.2 Callbacks and callback hell7.3 Promises and then/catch/finally7.4 async/await7.5 Promise combinators

Explains how JavaScript handles time: the event loop, then promises and async and await for clean concurrent code. Most real bugs live in the async gap, so this chapter pays off. You schedule work, turn callbacks into promises, and await results without blocking the main thread.

JavaScript runs one task at a time and schedules everything else. This chapter explains the ordering rules, then replaces nested callbacks with promises and async/await.

7.1 Call stack, event loop, microtasks and macrotasks

JavaScript runs on one thread with a task queue, and the entire async model follows from that: one piece of code runs at a time, to completion, and anything that waits goes to a queue. Every ordering surprise in the language is a queue-ordering surprise.

Three structures, not one

StructureHoldsOrderDrained when
Call stackFrames of functions currently executingLIFOA function returns
Task queue (macrotask)Callbacks scheduled as whole tasksFIFOThe stack empties
Microtask queuePromise reactions and queueMicrotask callbacksFIFOAfter the current task, and fully before the next task
console.log("1");

setTimeout(() => console.log("2"), 0);      // a TASK
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("3"));  // a MICROTASK
queueMicrotask(() => console.log("4"));          // a MICROTASK
console.log("5");

// 1
// 5
// 3
// 4
// 2      <-- microtasks drain completely before the next task,
//         so 3 and 4 both precede 2 even though 2 was queued first
// the rule, stated once
/*
  run the current task to completion
  then drain the ENTIRE microtask queue, including microtasks
  added by microtasks
  then take the next task
  then drain the microtask queue again
  repeat
*/

// which is why this hangs forever
function loop(){
  console.log("tick");
  Promise.resolve().then(loop);      // a microtask that re-queues
}
loop();
// a microtask that queues a microtask never yields to the task
// queue, so nothing ever paints and the page freezes

// and this does not
function loop2(){
  console.log("tick");
  setTimeout(loop2, 0);              // a TASK - the queue gets a turn
}
loop2();

// the blocking version, for contrast
function loop3(){
  console.log("tick");
  loop3();                           // the stack grows until it overflows
}
loop3();
// RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded

To let the page render, yield to the task queue, not the microtask queue. await null yields a microtask and does not paint; setTimeout(fn, 0) yields a task and does. For a loop that must not freeze the page, the tool is chunking with setTimeout or requestAnimationFrame, not a promise chain.

// measuring what the loop does
console.time("chunked");
processInChunks(items, 1000);
console.timeEnd("chunked");

function processInChunks(list, size){
  let i = 0;
  (function next(){
    const end = Math.min(i + size, list.length);
    for (; i < end; i++) handle(list[i]);
    if (i < list.length) setTimeout(next, 0);
  })();
}

// and yielding to the frame rather than the queue, which is what
// a progress indicator needs
async function animate(items){
  for (const item of items){
    render(item);
    await new Promise(requestAnimationFrame);   // one update per frame
  }
}

One task at a time, to completion. When the stack empties, the entire microtask queue drains — including microtasks queued by microtasks — before the next task. So a promise chain never yields to a setTimeout, and a loop built on promises freezes the page.

7.2 Callbacks and callback hell

A callback is a function passed to code that will call it later. That contract is fine; what made callback code notorious was nesting errors inside nesting errors, each one indented one level deeper, with no way to say "then".

Why an error skips the rest of a nested callback

// the shape that goes wrong
getUser(id, (err, user) => {
  if (err) throw err;              // throwing here does NOT reach
  getOrders(user.id, (err2, orders) => {   // the caller below
    if (err2) throw err2;
    getItems(orders[0], (err3, items) => {
      if (err3) throw err3;
      render(items);                // never runs if err2 or err3 fired
    });
  });
});

// try/catch does not help, because the inner callbacks run after
// the outer function has already returned
try {
  getUser(id, (err, user) => {
    if (err) throw err;
  });
} catch (e) {
  // never reached
}

// which is why a synchronous-looking API must be synchronous: if
// getUser might call back later, an exception in it cannot
// propagate to the caller, ever
// and the mirror-image bug
getUser(id, (err, user) => {
  if (!err) return;
  render(user);       // runs on ERROR, because the guard is
});                    // inverted - the worst shape, because it
                       // fails silently rather than loudly

// the shape that is right
getUser(id, (err, user) => {
  if (err) return handle(err);     // bail out on error
  render(user);                    // only reached on success
});
The inverted guard — if (!err) return; — is the most damaging callback bug in ordinary application code, because nothing throws, nothing logs, and the feature simply never appears to work. When every nested callback starts with a guard, write it as if (err) return handle(err); so the error path is the one that returns.

The Node convention, and why it exists

StyleSuccessFailureRisk
Error-first (Node)(null, value)(error)Easy to invert the guard
Success-first(value)(null, error)Just as easy to invert
Single object{ value }{ error }Hardest to invert — no positional confusion
ThrowReturnsThrowsOnly works for synchronous code
PromiseresolvesrejectsCannot fail synchronously. The modern answer
// the single-object form removes the inversion risk entirely
function readConfig(cb){
  fs.readFile("config.json", (err, data) => {
    if (err) return cb({ error: err });
    try {
      cb({ value: JSON.parse(data) });
    } catch (e) {
      cb({ error: e });              // a PARSE error, handled too
    }
  });
}
readConfig(({ value, error }) => {
  if (error) return show(error);
  apply(value);
});
// there is no way to read this backwards - the destructured names
// tell you which is which

// which is exactly what the next section replaces: promises cannot
// be inverted, because there is no second argument

Callbacks have not gone away. Anything that takes a completion handler — an event listener, a Node stream, a Web Animation, a requestIdleCallback — is a callback, and the guard rules are the same. The problem was never the callback; it was expressing a sequence of them.

The error-skipping problem is that throw inside a callback cannot reach the caller's try, because the callback runs later. Guard with if (err) return handle(err) — never the inverted form, which fails silently. A single { value, error } result removes the inversion risk, which is the idea promises make structural.

7.3 Promises and then/catch/finally

A promise is an object for a value that will exist. It has exactly three states and can only move one way, and the API is built so that settling twice is silently ignored rather than an error.

The three states

// pending  -> settled. One way only.
let p = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
  resolve("done");        // settling again is IGNORED, not an error
  reject(new Error("x")); // this does nothing
});
p.then(v => console.log(v));    // "done"

// the executor runs SYNCHRONOUSLY, immediately
new Promise((res) => { console.log("a"); res(); console.log("b"); });
// a, b  - res() does not defer the following line

// then returns a NEW promise, and it resolves with the return
// value of the handler - which is what chains
Promise.resolve(1)
  .then(v => v + 1)      // 2
  .then(v => { throw new Error("no"); })  // rejects
  .catch(e => e.message) // "no"
  .then(v => console.log(v));   // "no" - the catch RESOLVED it

// and the three terminal handlers
p.then(onOk, onErr);       // only runs on fulfilment
p.catch(onErr);            // equivalent to .then(undefined, onErr)
p.finally(cleanup);        // runs on both, and passes the value through
// finally is for cleanup. It does NOT swallow a rejection - returning
// a value from it re-resolves the chain
// the trap that catches everyone: a then inside a then
Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => {
    doSomethingAsync();          // NO return - fire and forget
  })
  .then(() => {
    console.log(useTheResult()); // the second then does NOT wait
  });

// correct
Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => doSomethingAsync())   // return it
  .then(result => useTheResult(result));

// and forEach is the worst offender, because map is the fix
list.forEach(async (item) => {
  await save(item);          // each is independent; nothing waits
});
await Promise.all(list.map(item => save(item)));   // this waits

// and Promise.all rejects on the FIRST rejection, abandoning the
// rest. If you need every outcome, use allSettled.
const [r1, r2] = await Promise.allSettled([a(), b()]);
r1.status;   // "fulfilled" or "rejected"
r1.value;    // or r1.reason
A missing return in a then is the single most common promise bug. The chain does not wait for the promise you created unless you return it, and nothing reports it — the code simply runs in the wrong order. The same applies to forEach with an async callback: it returns promises nobody collects.
// the shape that reads well
async function run(){
  const user  = await getUser(id);
  const orders = await getOrders(user.id);        // depends on user
  const items  = await getItems(orders[0].id);    // depends on orders
  return render(items);
}

// and where the independence is real, say so with Promise.all
async function run2(){
  const [user, prefs] = await Promise.all([getUser(id), getPrefs(id)]);
  return render(user, prefs);
}
// this is faster because they run concurrently, and it is correct
// only because neither depends on the other

// unhandled rejection, which is a real failure not a warning
process.on("unhandledRejection", (reason) => { report(reason); });
// or in a browser:
window.addEventListener("unhandledrejection", (e) => {
  e.preventDefault();
  report(e.reason);
});
// an unhandled rejection usually means a missing catch on a
// fire-and-forget chain

A promise settled twice is ignored rather than throwing, which means a double resolve fails silently. If the second call carries meaningful data — two callbacks both firing, a timeout and a response both arriving — that is a design error in the code that created the promise, and nothing in the promise API will surface it.

A promise moves pending to settled once, and settling again is ignored rather than an error. Every then returns a new promise and resolves with the handler's return value, so a missing return is the commonest bug — and forEach with an async callback is the worst instance. Use Promise.all for real independence and allSettled when you need every outcome.

7.4 async/await

async/await is syntax over a promise, and it changes nothing about ordering or concurrency. What it changes is that a linear-looking piece of code can suspend, and that a try/catch around it catches a rejection.

What it is

// these are the same function
async function a(){ return 1; }
function a(){ return Promise.resolve(1); }

// an async function always returns a promise, whatever you return
async function b(){ return 1; }        // resolves to 1
async function c(){ throw new Error("x"); }  // REJECTS, not throws
async function d(){ }                  // resolves to undefined

// await unwraps one level
async function e(){
  return Promise.resolve(1);   // resolves to 1, not to a promise
}
// so async + return promise does NOT double-wrap - this surprises
// people who expect a promise of a promise
// try/catch DOES work, because await re-throws in the frame
async function run(){
  try {
    const user = await getUser(id);    // suspends here
    render(user);                      // ...and resumes here
  } catch (err) {
    showError(err);                    // a rejection lands here
  } finally {
    hideSpinner();                     // on either path
  }
}

// try/catch does NOT work here
try {
  getUser(id).then(render);            // the .then runs LATER, outside
} catch (err) {
  showError(err);                      // this try has already exited
}

// and a rejection with no await lands nowhere at all - it becomes
// an unhandled rejection, which is why the try must wrap the await
Put await inside the try, never a .then chain outside it. try { doAsync(); } catches nothing, because the try block has already finished by the time the callback runs. This is the same error-skipping problem from the callbacks section, and await is the fix.

Sequential against concurrent

// sequential: three round trips, in series
const user   = await getUser(id);
const orders = await getOrders(user.id);
const items  = await getItems(orders[0].id);

// concurrent: one round trip, for independent calls
const [user, prefs] = await Promise.all([getUser(id), getPrefs(id)]);

// start them all, then await together - same effect, and sometimes
// clearer when there are many
const p1 = getUser(id);
const p2 = getPrefs(id);
const p3 = getFlags(id);
const [u, pr, f] = await Promise.all([p1, p2, p3]);

// sequential on purpose, when one result feeds the next
const user = await getUser(id);
const orders = await getOrders(user.id);   // needs user.id

// and the mistake that looks like concurrency
async function load(){
  const results = [];
  for (const id of ids){
    results.push(await getUser(id));   // awaits INSIDE the loop:
  }                                   // strictly sequential
  return results;
}
async function load2(){
  return Promise.all(ids.map(getUser));   // concurrent
}
// top-level await: allowed in a module
const cfg = await loadConfig();     // the module waits
const app = await start(cfg);

// NOT allowed in a classic script - and this library uses those,
// which is why nothing here awaits at the top level

// and the abortable version, which is what you want for a search box
async function search(q, signal){
  const res = await fetch(`/api?q=${q}`, { signal });
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(res.status);
  return res.json();
}
const ac = new AbortController();
search("a", ac.signal);
search("ab", ac.signal);   // abort the first when the second starts
ac.abort();

An async function always returns a promise, and return inside it unwraps one level rather than double-wrapping. Put await inside the try — a .then chain outside it is not caught. Independent calls belong in a single Promise.all; an await inside a loop is strictly sequential.

7.5 Promise combinators

Four static methods on Promise, each answering a different question about several promises at once. Choosing wrong produces either wasted requests or a rejection that takes down work that had already succeeded.

The four, and what each does on rejection

MethodResolvesRejectsResult shape
Promise.allWhen all fulfilOn the first rejectionAn array of values
Promise.allSettledAlwaysNeverArray of {status, value|reason}
Promise.anyOn the first fulfilmentWhen all rejectThe single fulfilled value
Promise.raceOn the first to settleOn the first to rejectWhichever came first
const a = () => Promise.resolve("a");
const b = () => Promise.reject(new Error("b"));

// all: all-or-nothing. one failure discards the successes.
await Promise.all([a(), b()]);      // throws "b"
// right when the results are only useful together - a page that
// needs every panel

// allSettled: every outcome, no rejection
const [x, y] = await Promise.allSettled([a(), b()]);
x;   // { status: "fulfilled", value: "a" }
y;   // { status: "rejected", reason: Error("b") }
// right for independent work where a partial result is useful:
// a dashboard whose panels load separately

// any: first SUCCESS, failing only if all fail
await Promise.any([mirror1(), mirror2(), mirror3()]);
// right for redundant sources - a CDN, a set of replicas

// race: first to SETTLE, either way
await Promise.race([fetchIt(), timeout(3000)]);
// right for a timeout. Note the loser is NOT cancelled - see below
Promise.race does not cancel the losers. A setTimeout that wins a race keeps running, and a fetch that loses still completes and writes its data. To actually stop work, pass an AbortSignal to each operation and abort it once the race is decided — otherwise a "timeout" leaves the original request in flight and its response can still arrive and overwrite the timeout result.
// a real timeout that actually cancels
async function withTimeout(fn, ms){
  const ac = new AbortController();
  const timer = setTimeout(() => ac.abort(new DOMException("timeout", "TimeoutError")), ms);
  try {
    return await fn({ signal: ac.signal });
  } finally {
    clearTimeout(timer);          // so a success does not leak a timer
  }
}
await withTimeout((o) => fetch("/slow", o), 3000);

// allSettled, with the result shape made readable
const results = await Promise.allSettled(ids.map(load));
const failures = results
  .map((r, i) => ({ r, id: ids[i] }))
  .filter(({ r }) => r.status === "rejected");
if (failures.length){
  log(failures.map(({ r, id }) => `${id}: ${r.reason.message}`));
}
const values = results
  .map(r => r.status === "fulfilled" ? r.value : null)
  .filter(v => v !== null);
// a typed helper, which is what makes this readable in TypeScript
interface Settled<T> { ok: true; value: T }
interface Failed  { ok: false; error: unknown }

async function allSettledTyped<T>(
  tasks: (() => Promise<T>)[]
): Promise<(Settled<T> | Failed)[]>{
  const raw = await Promise.allSettled(tasks.map(t => t()));
  return raw.map((r, i) =>
    r.status === "fulfilled"
      ? { ok: true, value: r.value }
      : { ok: false, error: r.reason });
}

for (const r of await allSettledTyped([loadA, loadB, loadC])){
  if (r.ok) render(r.value);
  else report(r.error);
}

The choice comes down to whether a partial result is useful. If the pieces only mean something together, all. If each is independently valuable — separate dashboard panels, separate uploads — allSettled, because all throws away work that already succeeded.

all is all-or-nothing and discards the successes on the first rejection; allSettled always resolves and reports each outcome; any takes the first success; race takes whichever settles first and does not cancel the losers — pass an AbortSignal if you need the work actually stopped.

8 · Fetch, APIs & JSON

8.1 fetch basics8.2 Methods, headers and POSTing JSON8.3 Error handling and status codes8.4 AbortController and timeouts8.5 JSON round trips at the edges

Talks to real web services: the fetch API, JSON serialization, and robust error handling. This is where your app starts exchanging data with the world. You make requests, parse responses, and handle both HTTP statuses and network failures with structured code.

fetch is the modern way to make HTTP requests. This chapter covers reading responses, sending JSON, handling failures, cancelling requests, and the JSON rules.

8.1 fetch basics

fetch resolves as soon as the response headers arrive. It does not wait for the body, it does not reject on an HTTP error status, and it has no timeout. Every one of those is a design decision you have to account for explicitly.

Three things it does not do

ExpectRealityConsequence
Rejects on a 404 or 500Resolves. Only network failure, CORS and abort rejectYou must check res.ok yourself
Waits for the bodyResolves on headers; the body streams afterwardsawait res.json() is a second wait
Times outNever. A hung request hangs for everUse AbortSignal.timeout()
// the minimum correct call
async function getJSON(url, options){
  const res = await fetch(url, options);
  if (!res.ok){
    // build an error that CARRIES the status, so the caller can act
    const body = await res.text();
    throw new HttpError(res.status, res.statusText, url, body);
  }
  return res.json();        // this is the second wait
}

class HttpError extends Error {
  constructor(status, statusText, url, body){
    super(`${status} ${statusText} for ${url}`);
    this.name = "HttpError";
    this.status = status;
    this.url = url;
    this.body = body;
  }
}

// and the body can only be read ONCE
const res = await fetch(url);
const a = await res.json();
const b = await res.json();      // TypeError: body already used
// so if you need it twice, read once and parse:
// const text = await res.text();
// const data = JSON.parse(text);
Reading the body twice throws, and the error says only "body stream already read" with no mention of fetch. If you need both a parsed object and the raw text — for logging, or for a field that only parses sometimes — call res.text() once and JSON.parse that.
// the three bodies, and which to use
await res.json();    // application/json, throws if it is not JSON
await res.text();    // always works
await res.formData();// multipart, for a file upload
await res.blob();    // binary: an image, a PDF
await res.arrayBuffer();  // binary as bytes

// GET, and the options
fetch("/api/users?page=2&q=" + encodeURIComponent(term));
// or, which builds the encoding for you:
const url = new URL("/api/users", location.origin);
url.searchParams.set("page", 2);
url.searchParams.set("q", term);
fetch(url);          // a URL object is accepted

// credentials
fetch("/api/me", { credentials: "same-origin" });   // the DEFAULT
fetch("/api/me", { credentials: "include" });       // cookies cross-origin
// and cross-origin cookies need SameSite=None; Secure on the server

The default mode is "cors", which means a cross-origin request is made as a simple request without credentials and the response must carry permissive CORS headers. For a cross-origin request with cookies or an Authorization header, the server must answer a preflight — and it cannot answer it from a cached response that lacks the right headers.

fetch resolves on headers, not on the body, and does not reject on a 4xx or 5xx — so check res.ok and throw an error that carries the status. It never times out, so pass an AbortSignal. The body can be read only once.

8.2 Methods, headers and POSTing JSON

A request with a body or a custom header is not a simple request, so the browser sends a OPTIONS request first and waits for permission. That preflight is why an API that works with curl fails from a page, and it is a server configuration issue rather than a client one.

What triggers a preflight

RequestPreflight?Why
GET with no custom headersNoSimple
POST with Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencodedNoOne of the three simple content types
POST with application/jsonYesNot a simple content type
Any request with AuthorizationYesNot a CORS-safelisted header
Any request with a custom X- headerYesOnly a few headers are safelisted
// the OPTIONS request the browser sends, and what it needs answered
OPTIONS /api/users
Access-Control-Request-Method: POST
Access-Control-Request-Headers: content-type, authorization

// the response it needs
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.example
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: content-type, authorization
Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true
Access-Control-Max-Age: 600        <-- cache the preflight for 10 min

// without Access-Control-Allow-Headers, the real request is
// never sent - the browser stops at the preflight
// and the console message is deliberately vague
Access-Control-Max-Age is the single highest-value header. Without it the browser sends an OPTIONS before every request, which doubles the round trips. Setting it to a few hundred seconds removes them for that long.

POSTing JSON, and the header that must match

// the correct JSON POST
await fetch("/api/users", {
  method: "POST",
  headers: {
    "Content-Type": "application/json",       // required, and must
                                             // match the server's parser
    "Accept": "application/json",
    // "Authorization": `Bearer ${token}`,   // triggers a preflight
  },
  body: JSON.stringify(payload),
});
// omit Content-Type and the server may parse it as text, or reject it.
// Note it is NOT multipart/form-data - that is a FormData body and
// you must NOT set Content-Type at all, because the browser adds it
// WITH the boundary, and a hand-set one omits the boundary and breaks.

async function send(url, method, payload){
  const res = await fetch(url, {
    method,
    headers: payload === undefined ? { Accept: "application/json" }
      : { "Content-Type": "application/json", Accept: "application/json" },
    body: payload === undefined ? undefined : JSON.stringify(payload),
  });
  if (!res.ok) throw new HttpError(res.status, "", url, await res.text());
  return res.status === 204 ? null : res.json();   // 204 has NO body
}

// PATCH, PUT, DELETE - all need a preflight
await fetch("/api/users/1", { method: "PATCH", headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
                              body: JSON.stringify({ name: "New" }) });

// and the read-modify-write race, which no header solves
// a PATCH is not atomic: two concurrent PATCHes to the same field
// lose one. The server needs versioning or an ETag.
const res = await fetch("/api/users/1");
const etag = res.headers.get("ETag");
await fetch("/api/users/1", { headers: { "If-Match": etag }, method: "PATCH",
                              body: JSON.stringify({ name: "New" }) });

A request with a body, an Authorization header or a non-simple content type triggers an OPTIONS preflight, and without a matching Access-Control-Allow-Headers the real request is never sent. Set Access-Control-Max-Age so it is not repeated. For JSON, set Content-Type; for FormData, set nothing at all.

8.3 Error handling and status codes

A network failure and an HTTP error status are different things, arrive at different points, and need different handling. Conflating them is why "my error handling does not work" is a common report — the handler is only ever reached for the first of the two.

Where each failure surfaces

FailureWhereWhat fetch does
DNS failure, connection refusedNetworkRejects with a TypeError
CORS blockedNetworkRejects. Deliberately vague, for information hiding
Aborted by the callerCallerRejects with AbortError
Timed out via AbortSignalCallerRejects with TimeoutError
404, 500, 422HTTPResolves. You check res.ok
Body is not JSONParsingRejects at res.json(), not at fetch
Body stream already readParsingRejects at the second read
class ApiError extends Error {
  constructor(message, { kind, status, url, body } = {}){
    super(message);
    this.name = "ApiError";
    this.kind = kind;         // "network" | "timeout" | "abort"
                              // | "http" | "parse"
    this.status = status;     // set only for kind === "http"
    this.url = url;
    this.body = body;         // the raw response, for the server message
  }
}

async function request(url, options = {}){
  let res;
  try {
    res = await fetch(url, { signal: options.signal ?? AbortSignal.timeout(10000), ...options });
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.name === "TimeoutError") throw new ApiError("The request timed out", { kind: "timeout", url });
    if (err.name === "AbortError")  throw new ApiError("Cancelled", { kind: "abort", url });
    // a bare TypeError from fetch is a network or CORS failure
    throw new ApiError("Could not reach the server", { kind: "network", url, });
  }

  const text = await res.text();          // once
  let data;
  try { data = text ? JSON.parse(text) : null; } catch { /* not JSON */ }

  if (!res.ok){
    throw new ApiError(data?.message ?? res.statusText, {
      kind: "http", status: res.status, url, body: data ?? text,
    });
  }
  return data;
}

A TypeError from fetch means the network failed or CORS blocked it, and the browser will not say which — that is deliberate, so a page cannot probe an internal network. It is also the most common reason a request "works in Postman and fails in the browser": in both cases the preflight response is missing something, and a non-browser client does not send one.

// handling, and why the kinds get different treatment
try {
  const user = await request("/api/me");
  render(user);
} catch (err) {
  switch (err.kind){
    case "timeout":  showRetry("That took too long."); break;
    case "abort":    break;                      // the caller cancelled -
                                               // not worth reporting
    case "network":  showOffline(); break;
    case "http":
      if (err.status === 401) return redirectToLogin();
      if (err.status === 403) return showForbidden();
      if (err.status === 429) return showRateLimited(err.body?.retryAfter);
      if (err.status >= 500) return showServerProblem(err.message);
      return showValidationErrors(err.body);    // 400, 422
    case "parse":    report(err); break;
  }
}

// the one thing not to do
catch (err) { console.error(err); }     // the user sees nothing at all,
// and the error may have been an abort the caller intended

A network failure and an HTTP error status arrive at different points: fetch rejects only for the first, and resolves for the second. Catch, then classify, then handle each kind differently — 401 is a redirect, 429 is a wait, an abort is usually deliberate. A bare TypeError means the network failed or CORS blocked it, and the browser will not say which.

8.4 AbortController and timeouts

Without a signal, a fetch that never receives a response never returns, and the UI waits for ever. AbortController is the cancellation primitive, and one controller can cancel several requests at once.

Timeout, in one line

// the built-in, which is the whole reason to use fetch over XHR
await fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000) });

// combining a timeout with a caller's own signal, which is the
// common case: the reader navigates away while the request is in flight
const ac = new AbortController();
const timer = setTimeout(() => ac.abort(), 5000);
try {
  res = await fetch(url, { signal: ac.signal });
} finally {
  clearTimeout(timer);
}

// or AbortSignal.any, which does the combining for you
const combined = AbortSignal.any([
  callerSignal,
  AbortSignal.timeout(5000),
]);
await fetch(url, { signal: combined });
// the pattern: one controller per piece of work, aborted when
// it is no longer needed
function searchBox(input, results){
  let ac = null;
  let seq = 0;

  input.addEventListener("input", async () => {
    ac?.abort();                 // cancel the previous search
    ac = new AbortController();
    const mine = ++seq;         // and a sequence number, because an
                                 // abort can lose a race
    const q = input.value.trim();
    if (!q) return results.replaceChildren();

    try {
      const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`,
                              { signal: ac.signal });
      if (!res.ok) throw new Error(res.status);
      const data = await res.json();
      if (mine !== seq) return;  // a newer search has started
      render(data);
    } catch (err) {
      if (err.name === "AbortError") return;   // expected, not an error
      showError(err);
    }
  });
}

// why BOTH the abort and the sequence number: aborting does not
// stop the response already in the network buffer from being
// processed, so without the sequence check an older response can
// still overwrite a newer one
Aborting is a request, not a guarantee. The fetch does reject, but code after the await may already have been scheduled, and a response that arrived before the abort still parses. A monotonic sequence number checked after every await is the reliable guard against an out-of-order response, and it costs three lines.
// cancelling a group: one controller, many listeners
const ac = new AbortController();
el.addEventListener("pointermove", onMove, { signal: ac.signal });
el.addEventListener("pointerup",   onUp,   { signal: ac.signal });
const timer = setInterval(poll, 1000, { signal: ac.signal });
// ...
ac.abort();     // removes all three, and stops the interval

// and a helper that cleans up after itself
async function withSignal(fn){
  const ac = new AbortController();
  try {
    return await fn(ac.signal);
  } finally {
    ac.abort();                 // or not: for a long-lived work,
  }                             // abort when the caller cancels
}

// AbortSignal.timeout and any() accept a reason, which reaches the catch
try {
  await fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(1000) });
} catch (err){
  err.name;          // "TimeoutError"
  err.message;       // "The operation was aborted due to timeout"
}

fetch has no timeout of its own — pass AbortSignal.timeout(ms), or combine it with a caller's signal using AbortSignal.any. Aborting is a request, not a guarantee: a response already in the buffer still parses, so add a sequence number checked after every await. One controller passed as a signal to several listeners removes all of them on abort().

8.5 JSON round trips at the edges

JSON is the wire format, and every use of it in an application is an edge: something on the other side is untrusted. Parsing is where a type claim becomes a fact, and it never was one.

Validate at the boundary, or never

// the data is whatever the server sent. Not what the types say.
const data = await res.json();
// data is `unknown` until proven otherwise. A TypeScript cast is a
// claim you make, not a check the language performs:
const user = data as User;   // compiles, and is wrong if the server changed

// the honest version: a schema check
function parseUser(raw){
  if (typeof raw !== "object" || raw === null) throw new Error("not an object");
  if (typeof raw.id !== "number")   throw new Error("id must be a number");
  if (typeof raw.name !== "string") throw new Error("name must be a string");
  if (!Array.isArray(raw.tags))     throw new Error("tags must be an array");
  return {
    id: raw.id,
    name: raw.name,
    tags: raw.tags.filter(t => typeof t === "string"),
  };                               // and the extra keys are dropped
}

// or a library, which is the scalable answer
import { z } from "zod";
const User = z.object({
  id: z.number(),
  name: z.string().min(1),
  tags: z.array(z.string()).default([]),
});
const user = User.parse(data);     // throws on a mismatch, and the
                                   // RESULT is typed from the schema
A cast such as data as User is a claim with no check. It compiles, it silences the compiler, and it is wrong the moment the server adds a field, renames one, or returns a string where the type said a number — which it will, eventually, and silently. Validate at the boundary so the type is earned.
JSON.parseNotes
Strips __proto__ from the resultDeliberate: it blocks prototype pollution through parse
Accepts a reviverThe only way to rebuild Dates and Maps
Allows duplicate keys, last winsUnlike a JS object literal, where the last also wins
Does not accept comments or trailing commasStrict JSON. A JSON5 parser accepts both
Allows a top-level string, number or nullNot just objects. Validate the shape, not the validity
Loses undefined, functions, NaN, InfinityAnd turns them into null
Turns a Date into a stringBecause toJSON is called on it
// stringify, and what it cannot represent
JSON.stringify({
  undef: undefined,     // key OMITTED
  fn(){},               // omitted
  sym: Symbol("x"),     // omitted
  nan: NaN,             // null
  inf: Infinity,        // null
  date: new Date(0),    // "1970-01-01T00:00:00.000Z" - a STRING
  map: new Map([[1,2]]),// {} - the entries are not own properties
  set: new Set([1,2]),  // {}
  err: new Error("x"),  // {}
});                     // and a cyclic object throws

// toJSON, which is why a Date silently becomes a string
class Money { constructor(c){ this.c = c; } toJSON(){ return this.c / 100; } }
JSON.stringify({ price: new Money(1999) });   // {"price":19.99}

// the replacer for the rest
JSON.stringify(state, (k, v) => {
  if (typeof v === "bigint") return v.toString();
  if (v instanceof Map)    return Object.fromEntries(v);
  if (v instanceof Set)    return [...v];
  if (v instanceof Error)  return { name: v.name, message: v.message };
  return v;
});

// and the indentation argument, which is a real cost
JSON.stringify(x);                       // minified
JSON.stringify(x, null, 2);              // 2-space - bigger
JSON.stringify(x, null, 0);              // no whitespace at all

JSON.parse returns unknown in practice — a cast is a claim, not a check, so validate at the boundary with a schema. stringify silently omits undefined and functions, nulls NaN, turns a Date into a string via toJSON, empties a Map or Set, and throws on a cycle. Use a replacer for any of those.

9 · Classes, Prototypes & OOP

9.1 The prototype chain9.2 Class syntax and constructors9.3 Getters, setters and static members9.4 Private fields and instanceof9.5 Inheritance, super and mixins

Shows how objects inherit from other objects: the prototype chain, class syntax, private fields, and inheritance. JavaScript’s object model sits behind every object you touch. You shape objects with classes or plain prototypes, then reuse and extend behavior through the chain.

Under every object in JavaScript sits a prototype chain. This chapter explains it, then shows the class syntax that builds on it and the OOP features you will use in real code.

9.1 The prototype chain

Every object in JavaScript has a prototype, and property lookup walks a chain rather than a table. That one fact explains why adding a property to Object.prototype works, why hasOwnProperty exists, and why a class is a convenience layered on top of an object with a prototype rather than a separate kind of thing.

The chain, walked

const animal = {
  legs: 4,
  speak() { return `${this.name} makes a noise`; },
};
const dog = Object.create(animal);      // dog -> animal -> Object.prototype
dog.name = "Rex";

dog.legs;               // 4     - not its own, found up the chain
dog.speak();            // works, with `this` bound to dog
Object.hasOwn(dog, "legs");        // false
"legs" in dog;                      // true  - `in` walks the chain

// the chain ends at null, and Object.prototype has no prototype
Object.getPrototypeOf(Object.prototype);   // null

// and Object.prototype itself is an ordinary object, which is why
Object.prototype.toString;            // "[object Object]"
Object.prototype.toString.call([]);   // "[object Array]" - the tag
// is how Array.isArray and JSON.stringify tell an array from an object
// the four operations, and what each is for
const proto = { greet() { return "hi"; } };

Object.create(proto);        // a new object with proto as its prototype
Object.getPrototypeOf(obj);  // who is obj's prototype
Object.setPrototypeOf(a, b);// change it - SLOW, see below
"x" in obj;                 // does the chain have it
Object.hasOwn(obj, "x");    // is it the object's OWN property
Object.keys(obj);           // own ENUMERABLE string keys
Object.getOwnPropertyNames(obj);   // own, including non-enumerable

// inherited keys are excluded from every own-property listing
const child = Object.create({ inherited: 1 });
child.own = 2;
Object.keys(child);                  // ["own"]
Object.getOwnPropertyNames(child);   // ["own"]
for (const k in child) console.log(k);   // "own" THEN "inherited"
Setting a prototype after an object exists is Object.setPrototypeOf, and it deoptimises every object that inherits from it in current engines. Nothing in this library uses it. Adding a property to Object.prototype after code has started has the same cost — V8 invalidates the inline caches for that property name library-wide, so a polyfill loaded late is a measurable slowdown for every object in the program.

Shadowing, and why it is not inheritance

const base = { describe() { return "base"; } };

// copying a method onto the child: the two are now unrelated
const copied = Object.create(base);
copied.describe = base.describe;      // same function, different object
copied.describe === base.describe;   // true
copied instanceof base;              // true  - still inherits

// overriding with a wrapper is the useful form
const counted = Object.create(base);
let calls = 0;
counted.describe = function () {
  calls++;
  return base.describe.call(this) + ` (x${calls})`;
};

// and the trap: an arrow has no `this`, so it cannot be a method
const broken = Object.create(base);
broken.describe = () => `${this.name}`;   // `this` is not the object
// an arrow on the prototype is a property of the object holding it, not a
// method of anything, and `this` resolves lexically to wherever the arrow
// was written

A copy is not inheritance. Two objects holding the same function have no link between them: change base.describe afterwards and the copy keeps the old one. Only Object.create gives you a live link, which is why Object.assign and the spread copy values and are for merging, while Object.create is for deriving. Using the wrong one is why "my method override stopped working when the parent changed" happens.

Property lookup walks the prototype chain, so hasOwn and in answer different questions and a plain obj.x cannot tell you which one you got. Object.keys and getOwnPropertyNames list only own keys, while for…in includes inherited ones. Never change a prototype after creation, and never put anything enumerable on Object.prototype — the cost is a library-wide deoptimisation.

9.2 Class syntax and constructors

A class is sugar over three things JavaScript already had: a constructor function, a prototype object holding the methods, and a hidden link between them that only class syntax can create. Understanding the desugaring is what makes extends, super and static predictable rather than magical.

What a class becomes

class Point {
  #secret = 0;
  constructor(x, y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
  get length() { return Math.hypot(this.x, this.y); }
  move(dx, dy) { this.x += dx; this.y += dy; return this; }
  static origin() { return new Point(0, 0); }
}

// what the engine is actually running
function Point(x, y) {
  this.x = x; this.y = y;
  Object.defineProperty(this, "#secret", { value: 0, writable: true });
}
Object.defineProperty(Point.prototype, "length", {
  get() { return Math.hypot(this.x, this.y); },   // getter, not a method
  configurable: true,
});
Point.prototype.move = function (dx, dy) {
  this.x += dx; this.y += dy; return this;
};
Point.origin = () => new Point(0, 0);
Point.prototype.constructor === Point;   // true - the back-link
// so the three differences from a function are the whole story

// 1. the body of a class is ALWAYS strict mode, even in a sloppy script
class Lenient { m() { return (function () { return this; })(); } }
Lenient.prototype.m.call(undefined);
// undefined, because class code is strict - in a plain function it would
// be the global object

// 2. calling without `new` is a TypeError, not a silent global write
Point(1, 2);      // TypeError: Class constructor Point cannot be invoked
                   // without 'new'

// 3. `this` is undefined until super() returns, so a derived class cannot
//    touch `this` before its parent is constructed
class Bad extends Point {
  constructor() { this.x = 1; super(); }   // ReferenceError
}
A class field holding an arrow function costs one closure per instance. For a method that is only ever called as obj.method(), a prototype method is strictly better: one function for all instances, and it can be borrowed by any other object with the right shape.

What new does, precisely

function Point(x, y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }

const p = new Point(1, 2);
// is exactly these four steps:
//   1. create an empty object whose prototype is Point.prototype
const o = Object.create(Point.prototype);
//   2. call the function with `this` bound to it, and the arguments given
Point.call(o, 1, 2);
//   3. if the call returned an OBJECT, use that object instead
//   4. otherwise use `o`
p === o;   // true, because the constructor returns undefined

// and the consequences, which are the useful part
function Widget() {
  this.a = 1;
  return { b: 2 };        // an object -> THIS becomes the new object
}
new Widget();             // { b: 2 } - `a` was written to a discarded object

function Gadget() { return 42; }   // a primitive -> ignored
new Gadget().a;          // undefined - the primitive return is discarded
// so a constructor that accidentally returns an object silently produces
// something else entirely, and `new` on an arrow function throws because
// arrows cannot be constructed

A class is a constructor function with strict-mode body, a new requirement, and no this before super(). Its methods live on the prototype, so a field holding an arrow costs a closure per instance while a method costs one function for all of them. new returns the constructor's object return value if there is one, which is the one rule that silently discards everything the constructor set up.

9.3 Getters, setters and static members

A getter is a property that computes its value when read, and a setter is a property that intercepts a write. They are defined with keywords on the object literal or the class body, they live on the prototype, and they are how a value gets validated without every caller remembering to validate it.

The four placements

FormWhere the function livesCost per instance
get x() {} in a class bodyThe prototypeNone
A field holding an arrowThe instanceOne closure
Object.definePropertyWherever you put itDepends
Object.definePropertiesSeveral at onceDepends
class Temperature {
  #c = 0;                       // a private backing field

  get celsius() { return this.#c; }
  set celsius(v) {
    if (typeof v !== "number") throw new TypeError("not a number");
    if (v < -273.15) throw new RangeError("below absolute zero");
    this.#c = v;                // no recursion: the two names differ
  }

  get fahrenheit() { return this.#c * 9 / 5 + 32; }
  set fahrenheit(v) { this.celsius = (v - 32) * 5 / 9; }   // reuse the validation

  get kelvin() { return this.#c + 273.15; }               // read-only: no setter
}

const t = new Temperature();
t.celsius = 100;
t.fahrenheit;      // 212
t.fahrenheit = 32; // t.celsius is now 0 - one validation path, not two
t.kelvin = 0;      // TypeError in strict mode, silently ignored in sloppy
// a getter with no setter makes a property READ-ONLY, and that is the
// only way to get a genuinely read-only property on a plain object
const config = {};
Object.defineProperty(config, "endpoint", {
  get() { return `${this.base}/api`; },
  set() { throw new TypeError("endpoint is derived from base"); },
  enumerable: true,        // needed for JSON.stringify and spread to see it
  configurable: false,     // the default: cannot be redefined or deleted
});
config.base = "https://api.example.com";
config.endpoint;           // https://api.example.com/api
Object.keys(config);       // ["base", "endpoint"]

// enumerability is the difference between a property that exists and one
// that participates in iteration, spreading and serialisation
const hidden = {};
Object.defineProperty(hidden, "secret", { value: 42 });   // not enumerable
Object.keys(hidden);       // []
JSON.stringify(hidden);     // "{}"
hidden.secret;             // 42 - it is there, it is just not listed

// which is why a class getter declared on the prototype IS enumerable by
// default in a class body, while defineProperty defaults it to false
class C { get g() { return 1; } }
Object.keys(Object.getPrototypeOf(new C()));   // [] - it is on the proto
// ...so spreading an instance copies own fields but NOT the getters.
// That is correct, and it surprises people.
An enumerable defineProperty getter and the spread operator disagree in a way that matters: { ...instance } copies own enumerable properties, and a prototype getter is not an own property. So spreading a class instance silently loses every accessor. Copy the fields you need, or serialise through a method that returns them explicitly.
// defineProperties, and the descriptor that makes a property behave
const target = {};
Object.defineProperties(target, {
  id:   { value: 1, enumerable: true },
  name: { value: "Ada", enumerable: true, writable: true },
  slug: { get() { return this.name.toLowerCase(); }, enumerable: true },
});

const flags = {};
Object.defineProperty(flags, "locked", {
  value: true,
  writable: false,      // assignment is ignored, or throws in strict mode
  enumerable: false,    // hidden from keys, spread and JSON
  configurable: false,  // cannot be deleted or redefined
});
flags.locked = false;   // no effect
delete flags.locked;    // false - and in strict mode, TypeError
Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(flags, "locked");

// the descriptor is the storage format. A normal property is exactly:
// { value: v, writable: true, enumerable: true, configurable: true }
// and `value` and `get`/`set` are mutually exclusive

Reach for an accessor when the value is derived or must be validated, not merely to make code shorter. A getter that does real work is a property that can throw, blocks the object for layout in some engines, and cannot be memoised for you — so if a getter is doing more than arithmetic over other fields, it wants to be a method.

A getter lives on the prototype, so it costs nothing per instance; a field holding an arrow costs a closure each. A getter with no setter is read-only, which is the only read-only a plain object has. Watch enumerable: defineProperty defaults it to false, and a prototype getter is not an own property — so spreading an instance drops every accessor.

9.4 Private fields and instanceof

Private state is the point of encapsulation: a value that cannot be read or written from outside, so the only way to change it is through a method that keeps it consistent. JavaScript offers three mechanisms, and they are not equivalent.

Three mechanisms, in order of strength

MechanismEnforced byReachable at runtime
A leading underscore, by conventionNothingTrivially
A WeakMap beside the classNothing, but it is hiddenOnly with a reference to the map
#fieldThe engineNot at all, by any means
// 1. convention: nothing stops it, but everyone reads the underscore
class Account {
  #balance = 0;         // real privacy, below
  _balance = 0;         // a convention, and a lie the type system cannot enforce
}
const a = new Account();
a._balance = 999;       // works. No error, no warning.

// 2. WeakMap: the key IS the instance, so the value cannot be enumerated
const store = new WeakMap();
class Vault {
  constructor(secret) { store.set(this, secret); }
  read() { return store.get(this); }
}
const v = new Vault("hunter2");
v.read();               // "hunter2"
v.secret;               // undefined
Object.keys(v);         // []
console.log(store);      // WeakMap(1) - the size, never the contents
// store.get(someOtherObject);   // undefined - a different key
// the cost: an extra lookup per access, and no way to enumerate instances
// 3. #private: enforced by the engine, and unreachable by any means
class Account {
  #balance = 0;
  #history = [];

  deposit(n) {
    if (n <= 0) throw new RangeError("must be positive");
    this.#balance += n;
    this.#history.push(n);
    return this.#balance;
  }
  get balance() { return this.#balance; }        // a read-only window onto it
  get statements() { return [...this.#history]; }  // a COPY, not the array
}

const acct = new Account();
acct.deposit(50);
acct.balance;                  // 50

acct.#balance;                 // SyntaxError - not even a runtime lookup
acct["#balance"];              // undefined
acct["#bal" + "ance"] = 999;   // undefined: still no such property
JSON.stringify(acct);          // "{}" - nothing private is ever serialised
Object.getOwnPropertyNames(acct);   // []
Object.getOwnPropertySymbols(acct); // []
Object.assign({}, acct);      // {}
structuredClone(acct);         // throws DataCloneError on a private field
// ...which is the one real cost: it cannot be cloned, so an instance with
// private state cannot be sent through postMessage or stored in IndexedDB
// without a toJSON or a manual copy
Prefer #private for anything that must not be written from outside, and expose intent through a method rather than a writable property. A getter that returns a copy of a mutable collection — return [...this.#history] — closes the last hole, because a getter returning the array itself hands out the array whatever the field's privacy.

What instanceof actually tests

class Base {}
class Derived extends Base {}
const d = new Derived();

d instanceof Derived;    // true
d instanceof Base;       // true - the whole chain is walked
