const fetchWithTimeout = (ms, url) => fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(ms) });

// the case for currying in real code: a configured pipeline step
const request = (base) => (path) => (init) =>
  fetch(base + path, init);
const api = request("https://api.example.com");
api("/users")({ credentials: "include" });

// the case against: it hides arity, and arity is the documentation
// (a, b, c) => ...   says three things at a glance
// a => (b) => (c) => ...   says one, and the reader must unfold it
// which matters more as a team gets larger, not less
Partial application is a readability feature: binding an argument once and naming the result turns a call site into a phrase. Currying is a structural feature, and it is worth it where the chain is the explanation — a request builder, a validator, a pipeline stage — and a tax everywhere else, because it turns a signature you can read at a glance into one you have to unfold.

Arity, and the code that breaks it

// the arity contract, precisely
function a(x, y, z){ }          a.length;   // 3
function b(x, y, z = 1){ }      b.length;   // 2 - a default STOPS the count
function c(x, ...rest){ }       c.length;   // 1 - a rest STOPS the count
function d(x, { y } = {}){ }    d.length;   // 1 - same
function e(...all){ }           e.length;   // 0
const f = (x, y) => x + y;    f.length;   // 2 - no difference for arrows

// so a curry on fn.length is wrong for three of those five
function curry(fn){
  const arity = fn.length;
  return function curried(...args){
    if (args.length >= arity) return fn.apply(this, args);
    return (...more) => curried(...args, ...more);
  };
}
curry((x, y, z = 1) => x + y + z)(1)(2);   // fires after ONE argument
// -> z is undefined, so the default applies and the total is wrong
// silently, because the default is what made it "work"

// the fix: pass the arity, or declare curried functions plainly
const curryN = (n, fn) => {
  const go = (...args) => (args.length >= n ? fn(...args) : (...m) => go(...args, ...m));
  return go;
};
curryN(3, (x, y, z = 1) => x + y + z)(1)(2)(3);   // correct

// and the other trap: `this` through a curried chain
const obj = {
  base: 100,
  add: function (n){ return this.base + n; },
};
obj.add(5);                        // 105
obj.add.bind(obj)(5);              // 105
const curriedAdd = curryN(1, obj.add);
curriedAdd(5);                     // NaN or a TypeError - `this` is lost
// the shapes that are worth it, and why each one is not just a curry

// 1. a configured pipeline stage — the chain IS the explanation
const validator = (schema) => (value) => validate(schema, value);
const req = validator({ name: "string", age: "number" });
req({ name: "Ada", age: 36 });     // true
// here the one-argument shape is genuinely useful, because a validator always
// takes exactly one value

// 2. an event handler — the framework's callback signature, as a function
const on = (event) => (handler) => {
  el.addEventListener(event, handler);
  return () => el.removeEventListener(event, handler);   // an unsubscribe
};
const off = on("click");
const handler = off(doThing);
handler();                         // detaches - which a bare curry cannot give

// 3. configuration as a reader
const request = (base) => (path) => (init = {}) =>
  fetch(base + path, init);
const api = request("/api");
api("/users");                    // GET /api/users
api("/users", { method: "POST", body });

// 4. and the case against, which is a whole codebase's worth of examples
const isPositive = (n) => (m) => n > 0 && m > 0;
isPositive(1)(2);                 // is this clearer than isPositive(1, 2)?
// no. The signature was documentation and it has been thrown away for a
// chain that has to be read left to right and evaluated left to right, with
// nothing gained in exchange

Currying earns its place where every step genuinely takes one argument — a validator, a pipeline stage, an event binding — because there the shape is the interface. It does not earn it where a function takes two or three arguments, because there the signature was the documentation and the chain replaces it with something you must unfold to read.

Currying reduces arity by exactly one at every step and takes one argument per call; partial application fixes any subset and leaves arity n − k. They are different techniques with the same name. Build a curry on fn.length only if every parameter is plain — a default or a rest parameter changes the reported length and the curry fires early. bindArgs for a leftmost binding is usually the clearer of the two.

11.5 Memoization

Memoisation caches a function's results by its arguments. It is the clearest payoff of purity — you can only reuse a result if the function would have produced the same one — and it is also the easiest place to introduce a bug that returns stale data forever.

The implementation, and the three questions

function memoize(fn, keyOf){
  const cache = new Map();
  const key = keyOf || ((...args) => JSON.stringify(args));
  function memoized(...args){
    const k = key(...args);
    if (cache.has(k)) return cache.get(k);
    const out = fn.apply(this, args);     // note: `this` is forwarded
    cache.set(k, out);
    return out;
  }
  memoized.cache = cache;                 // so a caller can seed or clear it
  return memoized;
}
const parseConfig = memoize((s) => JSON.parse(s));
parseConfig('{"a":1}');
parseConfig.cache.clear();

// question 1: can the key be derived from the arguments at all?
const a = { v: 1 }, b = { v: 1 };
parseConfig === parseConfig;         // yes, and note each closure has its own
memoize(fn) === memoize(fn);         // cache - two calls are two caches

// question 2: is the function pure? If not, this is a correctness bug
let n = 0;
const bad = memoize(() => ++n);
bad(); bad(); bad();                 // 1, 1, 1 - and n is still 1, ever
// a cached impure function is not fast, it is wrong

// question 3: what about the arguments that are NOT in the key?
const readFile = memoize((path, encoding) => fs.readFileSync(path, encoding));
// fine, because the file cannot change under you mid-process - usually.
// a live file, a clock, a random source: the cache must not outlive it
// the key derivation traps, in order of how expensive they are to find

// 1. JSON.stringify is lossy in four specific ways
JSON.stringify([1, undefined, () => {}]);   // "[1,null,null]"
JSON.stringify([NaN, Infinity]);              // "[null,null]"
JSON.stringify({ a: undefined });             // "{}"
JSON.stringify(new Date(0));                  // '"1970-01-01T00:00:00.000Z"'
JSON.stringify({ toString(){ return "x"; } });// '"x"'
JSON.stringify({ a: 1 }, ["a"]);             // replacer and space change it
// so two different argument lists can produce the same key, and the second
// call silently returns the first result. A memoised fetch keyed this way
// will serve one user's response to another.

// 2. objects and arrays are stringified by content, so two structurally
//    equal objects COLLIDE - which is sometimes what you want and sometimes
//    catastrophic
const m1 = memoize((o) => expensive(o), (o) => JSON.stringify(o));

// 3. argument order changes the key even when the call is equivalent
m1({ a: 1, b: 2 });     // key '{"a":1,"b":2}'
m1({ b: 2, a: 1 });     // key '{"b":2,"a":1}' - a cache miss for the same thing
// which is why a canonical key is worth writing when the argument is an object

// 4. symbol keys are dropped by JSON.stringify entirely
const sk = Symbol("k");
JSON.stringify({ [sk]: 1, a: 2 });      // '{"a":2}' - and so is `a`, if you
                                        // replacer on it at all

// the keys that do work, and when each is right
const k1 = (...args) => args.join("|");                 // primitives only
const k2 = (a, b) => `${a.id}:${a.v}:${b}`;        // explicit subset
const k3 = (o) => canonicalJson(o);                    // sorted-key JSON
const k4 = (node) => node;                             // identity - a WeakMap
// and a good canonicalJson, which is what most memoised stores actually want
function canonicalJson(v){
  if (v === null || typeof v !== "object") return JSON.stringify(v);
  if (Array.isArray(v)) return "[" + v.map(canonicalJson).join(",") + "]";
  const keys = Object.keys(v).sort();
  return "{" + keys.map((k) => JSON.stringify(k) + ":" + canonicalJson(v[k])).join(",") + "}";
}
canonicalJson({ b: 1, a: 2 }) === canonicalJson({ a: 2, b: 1 });   // true
// the leak, and the two fixes
function memoizeLRU(fn, max = 100){
  const cache = new Map();
  const memoized = (...args) => {
    const k = keyOf(args);
    if (cache.has(k)){
      const v = cache.get(k);
      cache.delete(k); cache.set(k, v);    // re-insert: Map is LRU by reorder
      return v;
    }
    const out = fn(...args);
    cache.set(k, out);
    if (cache.size > max) cache.delete(cache.keys().next().value);  // oldest
    return out;
  };
  return memoized;
}
// which is ~10 lines and is the difference between a cache and a memory
// leak: an unbounded cache on a function whose arguments vary is unbounded

// the version that cannot leak, for object arguments
function memoizeWeak(fn){
  const byArg = new WeakMap();      // arg0 -> Map of the rest -> result
  return function (first, ...rest){
    let inner = byArg.get(first);
    if (!inner){ inner = new Map(); byArg.set(first, inner); }
    const k = JSON.stringify(rest);
    if (!inner.has(k)) inner.set(k, fn.call(this, first, ...rest));
    return inner.get(k);
  };
}
memoizeWeak(parseNode);
// and this is the right choice when the first argument is a document node
// or an entity: the entry disappears when the object does, so the cache
// cannot outlive its keys

// and the honest alternative to all of this
const cache = new Map();
async function load(id){
  if (cache.has(id)) return cache.get(id);
  const p = fetchData(id);          // cache the PROMISE, not the result
  cache.set(id, p);                 // so a concurrent second call joins
  return p;
}
// caching the promise is the standard pattern for async work: it collapses
// duplicate in-flight requests as well as duplicate completed ones

Memoisation is only sound for a pure function, and it is worth remembering that "pure" includes the clock, the random source, the database and anything else reachable from outside. A memoised query against a live table is a stale-read bug with a performance feature wrapped around it — which is why caching a promise and choosing the invalidation yourself is usually safer than caching a result and hoping.

When not to memoise

// 1. it is already fast. Below roughly 50 microseconds the cache costs
//    as much as the call: JSON.stringify of the arguments is not free, and
//    for a two-number argument it can be slower than the function
const sum = (a, b) => a + b;
memoize(sum)(1, 2);      // slower than sum(1, 2)

// and this is measurable, not theoretical
function bench(fn, args, n = 100000){
  const t0 = performance.now();
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) fn(...args);
  return (performance.now() - t0) / n;      // ms per call
}
bench(sum, [1, 2]);                 // 0.00002
bench(memoize(sum), [1, 2]);       // 0.00031 - fifteen times slower

// 2. the arguments are large. The key is derived from them, so a 1 MB payload
//    is stringified on every call to save a computation worth microseconds.
memoize((bigObject) => analyse(bigObject));   // a bad trade
// memoize((id) => analyse(load(id)), (id) => id);   // a good one

// 3. the function is not pure. Covered, and repeated because it is the one
//    that produces a WRONG ANSWER rather than a slow one
let seq = 0;
const next = () => ++seq;
const cached = memoize(next);
cached(); cached(); cached();      // 1, 1, 1 - and seq is 1 for ever
// a cache on an impure function converts "occasionally wrong" into
// "wrong from the second call onwards"

// 4. the data changes and you cannot invalidate. This is the one that bites
const getRow = memoize((id) => db.queryOne("select * from t where id = ?", id));
getRow.cache.clear();              // the whole table, every time
// so a per-entity cache, not a per-function one:
const rowCache = new Map();
const getRow2 = (id) => {
  if (!rowCache.has(id)) rowCache.set(id, db.queryOne(sql, id));
  return rowCache.get(id);
};
const update = (id, patch) => {
  db.update(sql, { id, ...patch });
  rowCache.delete(id);             // one entry, invalidated at the write
};

// 5. the result is huge. Caching a 40 MB query result to avoid a query that
//    takes 20 ms is trading a bounded 40 MB for nothing
//    if the call is rare, the cache is pure overhead

// the decision, in one line
// memoise when:  the function is pure, the key is cheap to derive,
//                the body costs more than the key,
//                and the result is smaller than the cache entry
//              and you can name the event that invalidates it
// the framework version, which every mature codebase has
const cache = new Map();
const inflight = new Map();

async function query(sql, params){
  const key = sql + "|" + JSON.stringify(params);
  if (cache.has(key)) return cache.get(key);
  if (inflight.has(key)) return inflight.get(key);   // collapse duplicates

  const p = db.query(sql, params)
    .then((rows) => { cache.set(key, rows); inflight.delete(key); return rows; })
    .catch((err) => { inflight.delete(key); throw err; });   // note: removed
                                                     // on failure too, or a
                                                     // transient error is
                                                     // cached for ever
  inflight.set(key, p);
  return p;
}

// and the stale-while-revalidate pattern, which is the real answer for a
// cache whose data changes
async function get(key){
  const hit = cache.get(key);
  if (hit){
    if (hit.fresh) return hit.value;
    // stale: return it immediately, refresh in the background
    revalidate(key);
    return hit.value;
  }
  return await load(key);
}
function revalidate(key){
  if (inflight.has(key)) return;
  inflight.set(key, load(key).then((v) => { cache.set(key, v); inflight.delete(key); }));
}
// which is why a cache is not one thing: it is a fresh/stale state machine,
// and the interesting code is the transition between the two

Memoisation is a bet with four conditions and the fourth is the one that ages badly: you must be able to name the event that invalidates the entry. "The data changed" is not an event; "this specific row was written" is. Where the invalidation cannot be named, the honest answer is a cache with a short lifetime rather than a permanent one, and the decision to memoise at all is worth writing next to the call rather than leaving to whoever reads the diff last.

Memoisation needs three things: a pure function, a key derivable from the arguments, and a bound on the cache. JSON.stringify is lossy — it drops undefined, functions and symbols, nulls NaN, and distinguishes key order — so it silently collides. Cache promises rather than results for async work, use a WeakMap keyed on an object when the key can die, and cap the cache with an LRU or it is an unbounded leak.

12 · Error Handling, Debugging & Performance

12.1 try/catch/finally and throw12.2 Custom errors and error properties12.3 Console methods and breakpoints12.4 Common pitfalls12.5 Debounce and throttle

Handles failures well, finds bugs fast, and keeps the UI smooth. You will write custom errors, use the console and breakpoints, and tame the worst pitfalls. The goal is fewer surprises and faster diagnosis when something does go wrong.

Bugs are part of the job. This chapter gives you the error-handling patterns, the debugging workflow, and the throttle and debounce techniques that keep interfaces fast.

12.1 try/catch/finally and throw

A try block catches a throw, not a failure. Almost every mistake here is a consequence of that: code that throws where nothing is listening, code that catches more than it meant to, and code that swallows the reason on the way past.

The shape, and what each keyword actually does

try {
  risky();
} catch (err) {
  recover(err);
} finally {
  cleanup();          // runs whether the try succeeded, threw, or returned
}

// three things that surprise people

// 1. `let`/`const` in a try is block-scoped to the try
try { const x = 1; } catch (e){ }
x;                      // ReferenceError - not in scope here

// 2. a RETURN in finally overrides the try AND the catch
function f(){
  try { return "try"; }
  finally { return "finally"; }     // returns "finally" - the try's return
}                                   // is discarded, with no warning
function g(){
  try { throw new Error("x"); }
  catch { return "caught"; }
  finally { return "finally"; }    // and the throw is swallowed entirely
}
// so a finally that returns is almost always a bug, and the fix is to make
// finally not return: assign, or throw

// 3. an exception in finally replaces the original one
function h(){
  try { throw new Error("the real problem"); }
  finally { cleanup(); }            // if cleanup throws, THAT is what
}                                   // propagates, and "the real problem"
                                    // is lost unless cleanup used `cause`

// and the optional catch binding, for when you do not need the error
try { JSON.parse(raw); } catch { showFallback(); }
Throwing inside a finally discards whatever was in flight. The most expensive version of this is return in finally, which discards the original exception with no diagnostic at all — the caller sees a normal return and cannot know an error happened.
// what to throw, and what NOT to throw
throw new Error("Could not load user 42");          // yes: a message
throw new ValidationError("email is required", { field: "email" });
throw Object.assign(new Error("Payment declined"), { code: "DECLINED", status: 402 });

// no: a string. There is no stack, and `err.message` is undefined
throw "something went wrong";        // a TypeError: Cannot read properties of
                                    // undefined (reading 'message')
// no: an object literal. Same problem, plus it is not an Error, so
// Sentry groups it under nothing and log scraping misses it
throw { code: 500, msg: "oops" };

// no: undefined, accidentally
let err;
throw err;                          // throws undefined - and a `catch (e)`
                                    // guard of `if (e)` is then false

// throwing a non-Error is legal and occasionally unavoidable across a
// boundary that hands you a rejected value; wrap it at the boundary
function toError(e){
  if (e instanceof Error) return e;
  if (typeof e === "string") return new Error(e, { cause: e });
  return new Error("Non-error thrown", { cause: e });
}

// and `cause`, which is the feature that makes wrapping lossless
class HttpError extends Error {
  constructor(status, url, { cause } = {}){
    super(`${status} from ${url}`, { cause });   // ES2022
    this.name = "HttpError";
    this.status = status;
  }
}
try { await fetchJson(url); }
catch (err) { throw new HttpError(err.status ?? 0, url, { cause: err }); }
// the original survives in err.cause, so a log can print both the chain and
// the stack that actually failed

Where the boundary belongs

// catch as close to the failure as you can do something about it
function parse(input){                       // no try: nothing to recover,
  return JSON.parse(input);                  // and the caller wants the throw
}
function loadUser(id){
  try {
    return JSON.parse(fs.readFileSync(`${DIR}/${id}.json`));
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.code === "ENOENT") return null;  // a MISSING user is not an error
    throw err;                                // anything else is not ours to swallow
  }
}

// catch broadly at the edge, and do something visible with it
async function route(req, res){
  try {
    await handle(req);
  } catch (err) {
    report(err);                     // to a log, with the stack
    res.status(err.status ?? 500).json({ error: "Internal error" });
    // never res.status(500).json({ error: err.message }) - a stack trace and
    // a SQL fragment in a JSON body is a disclosure
  }
}

// three ways a catch goes wrong
try { doThing(); } catch (e) {}                       // 1. silent: nothing happened
try { doThing(); } catch (e) { console.log(e); }      // 2. logged and abandoned:
                                                      //    the caller cannot tell
try { risky(); } catch (e) { return DEFAULT; }        // 3. a fallback that hides a
                                                      //    systematic failure behind
                                                      //    a plausible-looking value

// and the rethrow that preserves the type while adding context
async function getOrder(id){
  let raw;
  try {
    raw = await db.query("select * from orders where id = ?", [id]);
  } catch (err) {
    throw new Error(`could not load order ${id}`, { cause: err });
  }
  if (!raw.length) throw new NotFoundError(`order ${id}`);
  return mapRow(raw[0]);
}
// async: the four mistakes, all of which look correct
async function wrong1(){ try { await fetchJson(u); } catch (e) { log(e); } }  // ok

async function wrong2(){
  try { doAsync(); }        // NOT awaited: the try block finishes immediately
  catch (e) { log(e); }     // so this catch can never see anything
}
async function wrong3(){
  doAsync().catch(log);     // correct, and different in shape
}
async function wrong4(){
  try { doAsync(); }        // an unhandled rejection, not a handled one
  finally { cleanup(); }
}
async function wrong5(){
  try { return doAsync(); } // returning a promise from inside try: the
  finally { cleanupNow(); } // finally runs BEFORE the promise settles, so
}                           // cleanupNow() runs too early — and a finally
                            // that throws rejects the returned promise

// the rule: await INSIDE the try, or use .catch(). There is no third option
// and no way to make an un-awaited promise throw synchronously.

// and the async equivalent of catch/finally
async function withRetry(fn, n = 3){
  for (let i = 0; ; i++){
    try { return await fn(); }
    catch (err) {
      if (i === n - 1) throw err;
      await sleep(2 ** i * 100);         // 100, 200, 400 — and the delay is
    }                                    // awaitED, so the rejection is
  }                                      // attributable to this call
}

A catch is a decision, not a net. Every one that swallows without doing something with the error has converted a loud failure into a wrong answer, and the wrong answer will be found later by someone who has no idea a catch was involved. Either handle it, re-throw it with context, or let it through — but decide which.

What belongs in finally, and what does not

StatementIn finallyBecause
Closing a stream, a lock, a transactionYesMust happen on every path
Hiding a spinner, restoring focusYesUI state must not depend on the outcome
Logging the errorNoIt fires for the success path too
RetryingNoA retry in finally hides the first failure
Re-throwing the original errorNoOnly catch knows whether there was one
return or breakNeverOverrides everything, silently
throw a different errorOnly with causeOtherwise the original is lost
// the shape that is right: finally does one job and cannot throw
async function transfer(from, to, amount){
  const fromAcct = await accounts.lock(from);
  const toAcct   = await accounts.lock(to);
  try {
    await fromAcct.debit(amount);
    await toAcct.credit(amount);
    await audit.record({ from, to, amount });
  } finally {
    await fromAcct.unlock();     // no return, no throw - so it cannot
    await toAcct.unlock();       // override the caller's result
  }
}
// note that the COMMIT is not in the finally. A commit in a finally runs
// even when the debit failed, which is how money gets created.

// and the finally that swallows, which is the version to look for in review
function load(){
  try { return fetchData(); }
  catch (err) { report(err); }
  finally { track("load"); }        // fine
}
function load2(){
  try { return fetchData(); }
  catch (err) { report(err); }
  finally { track("load"); console.log("done"); }
  // and the version to grep for:
  //   finally { cleanup(); }  where cleanup() can throw
  // which loses the original error entirely. Wrap it:
  finally {
    try { cleanup(); }
    catch (cleanupErr) { report(cleanupErr, { whileHandling: original }); }
  }
}

// one more: a return inside a CATCH is a legitimate way to convert a throw
// into a value, and it is the only place in the language where that is a
// feature rather than a bug
function parseOr(raw, fallback){
  try { return JSON.parse(raw); }
  catch { return fallback; }        // explicit, at the boundary, on purpose
}

A try catches a throw, not a failure. A return or a throw inside finally overrides whatever was in flight and discards it silently. Throw Error objects, wrap the original in { cause } rather than losing it, and put await inside the try — a try around an un-awaited promise catches nothing at all.

12.2 Custom errors and error properties

A custom error class exists for one reason: so a caller can tell which failure happened without matching on a message string. Everything else about it — the name, the stack, the cause chain — is inherited machinery that is easy to break.

The four lines that matter

class ValidationError extends Error {
  constructor(field, message, { cause } = {}){
    super(message, { cause });          // 1. pass the message up
    this.name = "ValidationError";       // 2. set the name explicitly
    this.field = field;                  // 3. add the structured data
  }
}

try {
  validate(input);
} catch (err) {
  if (err instanceof ValidationError){
    form.setError(err.field, err.message);
  } else {
    throw err;                            // not ours - do not disguise it
  }
}

// the four things that break when you forget step 2
class Bad extends Error { constructor(m){ super(m); } }
const b = new Bad("x");
b.name;                    // "Error"   - the name comes from the Error
                           // prototype, and Error.prototype.name is "Error",
                           // and a subclass does not get its own automatically
b.constructor.name;        // "Bad"
String(b);                 // "Error: x" - so a log shows the wrong type
b instanceof Error;        // true - inheritance works, so this is NOT the test
b.stack;                   // starts at the constructor, NOT at the throw site

// step 4: this is the one nobody writes and everybody needs
class HttpError extends Error {
  constructor(status, url, opts){
    super(`HTTP ${status} for ${url}`);
    this.name = "HttpError";
    this.status = status;
    this.url = url;
    Error.captureStackTrace?.(this, HttpError);
  }
}
// captureStackTrace(this, HttpError) removes every frame up to and including
// the HttpError constructor, so the stack starts at the code that CAUSED the
// error rather than at `new HttpError(...)`. Without it every stack has three
// identical internal frames on it. It is V8-only; on other engines it is a
// no-op and the extra frames are harmless.
Subclassing Error works for instanceof but not for name or stack, because both come from the prototype the base constructor set. Always assign this.name, and call Error.captureStackTrace(this, YourClass) where it exists — otherwise every one of your errors logs as "Error" and every stack carries the same three internal frames.
// codes versus messages, and why the message is not the interface
class ApiError extends Error {
  constructor(code, message, { status = 0, cause, details } = {}){
    super(message, { cause });
    this.name = "ApiError";
    this.code = code;            // a stable, machine-readable token
    this.status = status;
    this.details = details;
  }
}
const E = {
  RATE_LIMITED: "E_RATE_LIMITED",
  NOT_FOUND:    "E_NOT_FOUND",
};
function apiError(code, message, extra){ return new ApiError(code, message, extra); }

if (err.code === E.NOT_FOUND) return null;
switch (err.code){
  case E.RATE_LIMITED: return retryAfter(err.details.retryAfter);
  default: throw err;
}

// a matcher on message text is a bug waiting for a copy edit
if (err.message.includes("not found")) { }   // breaks when someone rewords it
if (err.message === "User not found by id"){ }  // breaks the moment the id moves
// which is the whole argument for a code: the message is for a human and will
// change; the code is for a machine and must not

// one error type with a code, rather than one type per failure
// - many types: instanceof chains that get long, and a new file per case
// - one type + code: a switch, and the cases are greppable strings
// the second is usually better once there is more than about five cases

// and the cross-realm problem, which instanceof cannot solve
class MyError extends Error {}
try {
  const iframe = document.createElement("iframe");
  document.body.append(iframe);
  const foreign = new iframe.contentWindow.MyError("x");
  foreign instanceof MyError;         // FALSE - a different Error.prototype
} catch (e) { /* same-origin iframe in modern browsers */ }
// which is why a branded check is safer where a realm boundary is involved:
Object.prototype.toString.call(e);      // "[object Error]" - not a brand check
function isMyError(e){ return e?.name === "MyError"; }   // string, so it works
// ...and `e.name` is only reliable if step 2 above was done
// a taxonomy that survives, and the two relationships that matter

// by WHO can act on it
class AppError extends Error {}                 // the application is wrong
class ValidationError extends AppError {}       // the caller is wrong
class NotFoundError extends AppError {}          // the caller asked for nothing
class PermissionError extends AppError {}        // authentication/authorisation
class TransientError extends AppError {}         // retry: network, 503, timeout
class FatalError extends AppError {}             // do not retry: a bug

// by WHERE it came from
class HttpError extends AppError {
  constructor(status, url, opts = {}){
    super(`${status} ${url}`, { cause: opts.cause });
    this.name = "HttpError";
    this.status = status;
    this.retryable = status === 429 || status >= 500;
  }
}
class DbError extends AppError {
  constructor(op, { cause } = {}){
    super(`database ${op} failed`, { cause });
    this.name = "DbError";
    this.retryable = /deadlock|timeout/i.test(cause?.message ?? "");
  }
}

// and the ONE decision that consumes both: is this retryable?
// a property beats an instanceof chain, because the chain has to be
// maintained and the property is set where the error is constructed
async function call(fn, { attempts = 3 } = {}){
  for (let i = 0; ; i++){
    try { return await fn(); }
    catch (err) {
      const canRetry = err instanceof AppError && err.retryable;
      if (!canRetry || i === attempts - 1) throw err;
      await sleep(2 ** i * 250);
    }
  }
}
// and note what this does NOT do: it does not retry a ValidationError, a
// 400, or a 404, because those are answers rather than failures

Give every error class a retryable property and one retry loop at the edge serves the whole application — because that decision depends on what the error is, and it can only be made where the error was constructed. Everything else the caller needs — a field name, a status, a retry-after — belongs in a property too, for the same reason: the message is for a human and will be reworded.

Getting the error to the log in one piece

An error class is only as good as what reaches the log, and the default stack plus a cause is not enough once a request crosses three layers.

// the shape that survives: every layer adds context, none destroys
class AppError extends Error {
  constructor(message, { cause, code, status = 500, retryable = false, meta = {} } = {}){
    super(message, { cause });
    this.name = new.target.name;          // the subclass's own name, no matter
                                          // how deep the hierarchy gets
    this.code = code;
    this.status = status;
    this.retryable = retryable;
    this.meta = meta;
    if (Error.captureStackTrace) Error.captureStackTrace(this, new.target);
  }
}
class NotFoundError extends AppError {
  constructor(what, id){
    super(`${what} ${id} not found`, {
      code: "E_NOT_FOUND", status: 404, meta: { what, id },
    });
  }
}

// one writer, used everywhere, so the shape is decided once
function serialise(err){
  const parts = [];
  for (let e = err; e && !parts.some((p) => p.stack === e.stack); e = e.cause){
    parts.push({
      type: e.name,
      message: e.message,
      code: e.code,
      status: e.status,
      // the FIRST stack is the throw site of the outermost error; the cause
      // chain carries the sites of the inner ones. Both are needed: the outer
      // one says where the request failed, the inner says why.
      stack: (e.stack || "").split("\n").slice(0, 8),
      meta: e.meta,
    });
  }
  return parts;
}
report({ level: "error", err: serialise(err), requestId, userId });

// and what this buys: the log line for a wrapped error contains
//   E_NOT_FOUND: order 8812 not found
//     at loadOrder (orders.js:44:11)            <- the cause, from the DB layer
//   Error: could not load order 8812
//     at GET /orders/:id (routes.js:88:5)       <- where the request failed
// ...which is the difference between a five-minute and a five-hour bug.

// and the two things to keep OUT of an error payload
// err.request / err.response - they hold credentials and are not
//   JSON-serialisable, so they are silently dropped by JSON.stringify and
//   retained by anything that does not
// a stack built from new Error() in a hot path - captureStackTrace already
//   gives you the site, and constructing twice costs the frame twice

The cause chain is the whole reason to wrap rather than re-throw a new message, and it is the part that goes missing first: a wrapper that does not pass { cause } produces a log with a correct outer message and no explanation. Walking the chain once, at the edge, and writing it as one record is the shape that makes a wrapped error as diagnosable as an unwrapped one.

Subclassing Error gives you instanceof but not name or stack — both come from the base prototype, so set this.name and call Error.captureStackTrace(this, YourClass). Carry a stable code rather than branching on message text, and a retryable flag so one retry loop at the edge covers every case. instanceof also fails across an iframe or worker realm.

12.3 Console methods and breakpoints

The console is a debugger with a worse UI, and the debugger is a console with a worse API. Knowing which tool answers which question — and what each one costs — is most of what separates a fast bug hunt from a slow one.

The console methods, and what each is for

MethodUse it forDo not use it for
console.logGeneral output; %s %d %o %O %c formatsObjects you will log again — they are live references
console.tableArrays of same-shaped objectsNested data — it flattens one level only
console.group / groupEndNested output; collapses in the UIGroups left unclosed, which swallow everything after
console.dir(el)DOM nodes and their properties—
console.count()Counting executions, per labelCounting in production — see below
console.time / timeEndOne measurement, with the label in the outputPrecise profiling — the resolution is poor and JIT-warm
console.trace()A call stack from a lineAsync stacks, which it will not show
console.assertInvariants; logs only on failureControl flow — it does not throw
// the log that lies, which costs hours
const state = { items: [] };
console.log("before", state);
state.items.push("a");
console.log("after", state);        // both print items: ["a"]

// because the console keeps a REFERENCE, not a snapshot. Three fixes:
console.log("before", structuredClone(state));   // expensive, always right
console.log("before", JSON.stringify(state));    // loses undefined and Dates
console.log("before", %o, state);                // DEVTOOLS: eager expansion,
                                                 // evaluated at log time
// or the one that costs nothing: `debugger` and look at the object then

// the table, which is the fastest way to read a list
const rows = users.map((u) => ({ id: u.id, name: u.name, role: u.role }));
console.table(rows);
console.table(rows, ["id", "role"]);      // two columns, 80 of them visible

// and the three that survive a stack trace
console.group("fetch");
console.log("url:", url);
console.log("init:", init);
console.groupEnd();

// the log levels a logger should have, because console has none
const LEVELS = { error: 0, warn: 1, info: 2, debug: 3 };
let threshold = LEVELS.info;
const log = {
  error: (...a) => threshold <= 0 && console.error(...a),
  warn:  (...a) => threshold <= 1 && console.warn(...a),
  info:  (...a) => threshold <= 2 && console.log(...a),
  debug: (...a) => threshold <= 3 && console.log("[debug]", ...a),
};
// which is what every logging library is: a threshold, a prefix, and a
// structured payload
// the measurement, and what it does not tell you
console.time("parse");
const out = parse(huge);
console.timeEnd("parse");      // "parse: 412.7ms"

// it does NOT break the function down, it is not reliable to two decimal
// places, and the first run includes JIT warm-up
console.time("parse"); parse(huge); console.timeEnd("parse");   // warm it first

// the real measurement API, for "why is this slow"
performance.mark("parse:start");
const out = parse(huge);
performance.mark("parse:end");
performance.measure("parse", "parse:start", "parse:end");
performance.getEntriesByName("parse").at(-1).duration;
// or just let the browser do it: the Performance panel, and
console.profile("render"); /* work */ console.profileEnd();

// long tasks, which is the one that explains a janky page
new PerformanceObserver((list) => {
  for (const e of list.getEntries()) if (e.duration > 50) warn(e);
}).observe({ type: "longtask", buffered: true });
// a task over 50 ms is what a dropped frame feels like, and the observer
// names the attribution that caused it

Breakpoints, and the four that replace most logging

// 1. a LOGPOINT — a console.log with no code change and no rebuild
//    DevTools: right-click a line number -> Add logpoint
//    enter:   console.log("here", item)
//    and it survives a reload and a refresh of the bundle, because it is not
//    in the source at all

// 2. a CONDITIONAL breakpoint — no logging at all, it just stops
//    DevTools: right-click the gutter -> Add conditional breakpoint
//    enter:   item.id === 42
//    or:      !item.valid
//    or:      window.__watch === item
//    which is strictly better than a log for "what is item at this point",
//    because you can then walk the call stack and inspect anything

// 3. the `debugger` STATEMENT, for a file you can edit
function suspicious(v){
  debugger;              // stops if devtools is open, does nothing otherwise
  return v;
}
// it is inert in production in practice: the statement parses, and the only
// cost is that the word cannot be a property name — which is why it must not
// appear in shipped code, and why it is worth a lint rule rather than a habit

// 4. a WATCH EXPRESSION, which evaluates continuously
//    DevTools -> Watch:  items.filter(i => !i.valid).length
//    and it updates as you step, so you see the number move

// plus the two that are not breakpoints at all:
//  - "Pause on exceptions" (the red circle in Sources) with "pause on caught"
//    OFF — the setting that catches a swallowed error nobody logged
//  - the Network panel's "preserve log", which is what makes a failure that
//    only happens on the fifth request reproducible
// source maps, which decide whether any of this is usable
// throw new Error("boom");                    // at line 1 of app.js
// throw new Error("boom");                    // at line 4123 of app.9f2c.js
//                                                    ^ and this one costs hours

// the rules
// - generate them, and ship them: source-map JS files do not expose your source
//   to a user; they expose it to anyone who asks DevTools
// - set them in dev only: setting "source-map" in a Content-Security-Policy
//   blocks eval-based devtools, which includes the console and breakpoints
Content-Security-Policy: script-src 'self'         // no source-map -> no eval
// so a strict CSP and a usable debugger are mutually exclusive, and the
// trade is usually taken deliberately rather than discovered
// - "hidden-source-map" hides the map from the network tab but not from the
//   file system, and it is the honest choice for a published library
// - minify without mangling names if you expect anyone to read a stack trace
//   from production: a frame called "a" tells nobody anything

The order to try things in is worth fixing, because it is almost always right: reproduce it, then reach for a logpoint or a conditional breakpoint rather than editing the file, then step if the data is the question, then profile if the timing is. Adding a console.log and reloading the bundle is the slowest of these by an order of magnitude, and it is what most people do first.

Reading a stack trace, and the four shapes

TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'items')
    at render (app.js:412:19)
    at update (app.js:388:7)
    at dispatch (vendor.js:1204:31)
    at HTMLButtonElement.onClick (app.js:31:5)

// read it bottom-up: the outermost frame is where the code that had a
// handle on it ran, and the innermost is where it actually broke
//   onClick    -> the event handler
//   dispatch   -> a framework's dispatch loop  (not your bug)
//   update     -> YOUR bug: it called render with nothing
//   render     -> the throw site: something.items where something is undefined
//
// so the line to open is app.js:388, not app.js:412. 412 is where the
// symptom appeared.

// the four shapes you will actually see
// 1. "x is not a function"          -> a missing import, or a typo
// 2. "Cannot read properties of undefined"  -> a destructure of a nullish
//      value; the VARIABLE in the message is the first thing to check
// 3. "x is not defined"             -> the opposite: a typo or a missing
//      global, and NOT a nullish value
// 4. "Maximum call stack size exceeded"    -> unbounded recursion, almost
//      always a reducer or a JSON cycle

// async stacks are where reading goes wrong
//   at async validate (form.js:22:5)
//   at async submit (form.js:60:9)
// with NO frame between them for the await chain, so the gap is where the
// answer is. Chrome prints the chain if --enable-async-stack-traces is on, and
// DevTools shows it in a normal pause.

// and the one that wastes the most time
Error: 500
    at parseResponse (api.js:31:11)
// which says nothing: it has been thrown far from the cause, the status has
// lost its statusText, and the response body is gone. A thrown object with a
// number in it is worse than no error at all.
// the debugging instruments that are not the debugger

// 1. breakpoints you cannot set in the editor
//    DevTools -> Sources -> right-click the line number
//      "logpoint"           a console.log that is not in the source
//      "conditional"        stop only when an expression is true
//      "dom breakpoint"     stop when a node's subtree changes
//      "event listener"     stop when click/keydown is dispatched anywhere
//    The event-listener one is the only practical way to find who handles a
//    click on a third-party component.

// 2. the console as an expression evaluator, which is under-used
//    copy($0)                 the selected DOM node
//    $$, document.querySelectorAll  two $ shortcuts
//    monitor(obj.foo)          logs every change to that property
//    monitorEvents(el, "click")
//    queryObjects(Constructor) every instance of a class
//    getEventListeners(el)     every listener on a node
//    copy(JSON.stringify(x))  as JSON
//    -  "$0" is the currently selected element in the Elements panel, and it
//    is the fastest way to answer "what is this thing"

// 3. local overrides, for CSS you cannot edit yet
//    DevTools -> Styles -> the .cls rule -> "New Style Rule"
//    or the Overrides panel: right-click a file -> "Save for overrides",
//    edit it, refresh. Survives reloads, applies to every matching node.
//    This is the sanctioned way to fix a CSS bug before you can push.

// 4. the Performance panel for "it is slow", and the Memory panel for "it
//    grows". Neither is a console command, and both answer questions
//    console.time cannot.

The order that works is: reproduce, then logpoint or conditional breakpoint, then step, then profile. Editing the file to add a console.log is the slowest of those and the one most people reach for first, because it is the only one that requires no knowledge of DevTools. It also requires a rebuild, so on a minified bundle it tells you nothing at all.

console.log holds a reference, so logging an object and mutating it shows the mutated value — pass a cloned or eagerly-expanded object. Use logpoints and conditional breakpoints instead of editing source: they need no rebuild and survive a reload. Generate source maps and ship them; a stack trace through a minified bundle without one is unreadable. A strict CSP without source-map disables the console and breakpoints entirely.

12.4 Common pitfalls

These are the mistakes that survive code review, because each one is a line that looks correct and is not. They are grouped by what they cost: silently wrong answers first, then errors that point at the wrong place.

Silently wrong

Looks likeIs actually
==Coercion. "" == 0 and [] == false are both true
if (list) for an empty array[] is truthy. Every array is, including []
arr.sort((a,b) => a > b)A comparator returning a boolean, which is 0 or 1. The sort does nothing stable
arr.map(parseInt)parseInt("10", 0) — the index becomes the radix
typeof x === "object"true for null, and for arrays
0.1 + 0.2 === 0.3false. IEEE 754 binary floating point
new Date("2026-01-01")UTC in modern engines, local time in older ones
for (const k in obj)Includes inherited enumerable keys
delete obj.propReturns false for a non-configurable property, and does not throw outside strict mode
obj.hasOwnProperty("x")Fails if the object has a null prototype, or a property of that name
NaN === NaNfalse. Use Object.is or Number.isNaN
[1,2,3].sort()Lexicographic: [10, 9, 1], because everything became a string
// the ones that cost the most, with the fix
[1,2,3].sort();                        // [1, 2, 3] - by luck
[10, 9, 1].sort();                    // [1, 10, 9] - WRONG
[10, 9, 1].sort((a, b) => a - b);   // [1, 9, 10]

["1","10","2"].sort();                        // WRONG
["1","10","2"].sort((a,b) => a - b);        // works, by coercion
["1","10","2"].sort((a,b) => Number(a) - Number(b));  // explicit

["10", "1", "2"].map(parseInt);        // [10, NaN, 2]
["10","1","2"].map((s) => parseInt(s, 10));   // [10, 1, 2]

typeof null;                          // "object" - a 1995 bug kept for compat
Array.isArray([]);                    // the reliable test
if (arr.length) { }                   // never `if (arr)`

// equality, and the four that behave differently
[0] == false;      // true
"" == 0;           // true
null == undefined; // true
null == 0;         // false - and `null >= 0` is true
NaN == NaN;        // false
0 === -0;          // true
Object.is(0, -0);  // false
[NaN].includes(NaN);   // true  - SameValueZero
[NaN].indexOf(NaN);    // -1    - strict equality
// which is why `Object.is` and `includes` disagree about NaN and -0, and
// why a Set dedupes NaN where indexOf cannot find it

// money, which is not a float
0.1 + 0.2;                       // 0.30000000000000004
Math.round((0.1 + 0.2) * 100) / 100;   // 0.3 - and the rounding trap below
(1.005).toFixed(2);              // "1.00" - 1.005 is stored as 1.00499...
Math.round(1.005 * 100) / 100;   // 1 - the same problem
// integers of cents, or a decimal type, or a rounding library. Never a float.
// the two that look like bugs in someone else's code
hasOwnProperty.call(obj, key);      // always safe
Object.hasOwn(obj, key);            // ES2022, and the clearest
"key" in obj;                       // includes the prototype chain

// the null-prototype object, which has no hasOwnProperty at all
const dict = Object.create(null);
dict.a = 1;
dict.hasOwnProperty("a");           // TypeError: not a function
Object.hasOwn(dict, "a");           // true

// and the property that shadows it
const evil = { hasOwnProperty: () => false, a: 1 };
evil.hasOwnProperty("a");           // false - and nothing is wrong

// dates, where the difference is invisible and enormous
new Date("2026-01-01").getTimezoneOffset();   // depends on the machine
new Date("2026-01-01").toISOString();         // "2025-12-31T23:00:00.000Z" on
                                             // a machine west of Greenwich
new Date(2026, 0, 1);                   // LOCAL 1 January - month is 0-indexed
new Date("2026-01-01T00:00:00Z");        // UTC, unambiguous - prefer this
// and "2026-01-01" is specified as UTC by the standard, while a bare
// "2026-01-01 00:00" is specified as LOCAL, and one engine shipped both

// the loop that skips elements
const live = document.getElementsByTagName("li");
for (const el of live){ if (bad(el)) el.remove(); }        // skips the next
for (const el of [...live]){ if (bad(el)) el.remove(); }   // correct
// getElementsBy* returns a LIVE list; querySelectorAll does not
// the three that are not about the language at all
// 1. relying on property order
const o = {};
for (const k of ["b","a","1","2"]) o[k] = 1;
Object.keys(o);       // ["1","2","b","a"] - integer keys first, ascending
// so a config object keyed by untrusted strings does not iterate in the order
// it was built, and a render that depends on it will look fine and be wrong

// 2. mutating while iterating a Map with a forEach
const m = new Map([[1,"a"],[2,"b"],[3,"c"]]);
m.forEach((v, k) => { if (k === 1) m.set(4, "d"); m.delete(k); });
// entries added during forEach ARE visited, so this can loop for ever if the
// added entry also deletes something

// 3. the off-by-one in a reduce
[1,2,3].reduce((acc, n) => acc + n);            // 6  - the 0 seed is implicit
[[1],[2]].reduce((acc, n) => acc.concat(n));    // [1,2] - and here it is WRONG,
                                                 // because the seed is 1
[[1],[2]].reduce((acc, n) => acc.concat(n), []);  // [1,2] - always pass it
[].reduce((a,b) => a+b);                       // TypeError
[].reduce((a,b) => a+b, 0);                    // 0 - the empty case is why
// and the trap inside the trap: `[]` as a seed works until you forget it once
// and then the failure is a number where an array was expected, two lines away

The pattern in the worst of these is that the wrong behaviour is not an error — an empty array is truthy, a boolean comparator is a valid number, a float that is nearly right still prints. That is what makes them expensive: they survive review and they survive testing, because a test asserts on values that happen to come out right for the inputs anyone tried.

The review checklist

SeeAsk
== or !=Is the coercion intended, or is it load-bearing?
arr.sort() on numbersWhere is the comparator?
.map(fn) where fn takes 2 argsIs the index being passed?
== nullIntentional? It catches both null and undefined
for…inOver an array or a plain object with a prototype?
reduce without a seedWhat happens for an empty array?
new Date("2026-01-01")Is the UTC/local ambiguity intended?
A float used for moneyIs it cents, or a decimal type?
getElementsBy* in a loopIs the list being mutated inside it?
hasOwnProperty(Could the object shadow it, or have a null prototype?
An empty array in a conditionWas length meant?
JSON.parse of a responseIs it validated, or cast?
A bare catch (e) {}Where does it go now?
setTimeout with no cleanupCan the component unmount first?
addEventListener in a componentIs there a matching removal?
Array.isArray vs instanceofCould it cross a realm?
A regular expression without an escapeIs a . intended, or a wildcard?
replace("x", "y")First occurrence only — was replaceAll meant?
// the three that are missed by every checklist, because they are not
// about a single line

// 1. two features that each read one property and should read the other
function onSort(a, b){ return a.order - b.order; }
function onName(a, b){ return a.name.localeCompare(b.name); }
table.sort(onSort);
table.filter(x => x.name.includes(term));
// fine until a filtered row's `order` is undefined, and then onSort returns
// NaN and the sort's behaviour is unspecified. The features interact.

// 2. a function that is correct and called from the wrong place
function sendEmail(to){ transporter.send({ to }); }   // no return, no await
async function onSignup(user){
  await createUser(user);
  sendEmail(user.email);     // not awaited: the request may finish after
}                            // the response, and a rejection is unhandled
// the fix is `await sendEmail(...)`, and the checklist item is really
// "every promise-returning call is either awaited or explicitly detached"

// 3. a default that is not a default
const PAGE_SIZE = 20;
function list({ page = 1, size = PAGE_SIZE } = {}){
  return db.query("limit ? offset ?", [size, (page - 1) * size]);
}
list();                       // fine
list({ size: 0 });            // size 0 is NOT falsy-defaulted, so a limit of
                              // zero rows - which is either correct or a bug
list({ page: 0 });            // offset -20 - a negative offset, and most
                              // databases reject it
// destructuring defaults fire on undefined and on nothing else, so a 0 from a
// form input or a query string goes straight through

The checklist catches the single-line mistakes; the three below it do not, because each one involves two lines that are individually correct and jointly wrong. A sort and a filter that each read a different field, an un-awaited call in an otherwise correct handler, and a destructuring default that does not apply to a legitimate 0 — all three survive review, and all three are found the hard way.

Compare numeric arrays with (a, b) => a - b, never the default sort() and never a comparator returning a boolean. Use Object.hasOwn rather than hasOwnProperty, which a property can shadow and a null-prototype object does not have. An empty array is truthy; typeof null is "object"; [].reduce without a seed throws. Money is not a float, and dates want an explicit Z.

12.5 Debounce and throttle

Debounce waits for the noise to stop; throttle allows a fixed rate. They solve different problems, they are not interchangeable, and both are three lines of code whose subtle part is the lifecycle — the trailing call, the cancellation, and the timer that holds a closure.

Debounce

function debounce(fn, ms = 300, { leading = false } = {}){
  let timer = null;
  let lastArgs = null;

  function debounced(...args){
    lastArgs = args;
    const callNow = leading && timer === null;
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => {
      timer = null;
      if (!leading) fn.apply(this, lastArgs);   // the trailing call
    }, ms);
    if (callNow) return fn.apply(this, args);   // the leading call
  }

  debounced.cancel = () => { clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; };
  debounced.flush   = () => {
    if (timer === null) return;
    clearTimeout(timer); timer = null;
    fn.apply(this, lastArgs);
  };
  debounced.pending = () => timer !== null;
  return debounced;
}

const onSearch = debounce((term) => fetchResults(term), 250);
input.addEventListener("input", (e) => onSearch(e.target.value));

onSearch.cancel();   // on unmount - without it, a pending timer fires into a
                     // component that no longer exists, and calls setState on it
onSearch.flush();    // "run it now" - a Save button that must not wait 300ms
// throttle, which is a different problem entirely
function throttle(fn, ms = 100){
  let last = 0;
  let timer = null;
  let lastArgs = null;

  function throttled(...args){
    const now = Date.now();
    lastArgs = args;
    const remaining = ms - (now - last);
    if (remaining <= 0){
      last = now;
      if (timer){ clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; }
      fn.apply(this, args);
    } else if (!timer){
      // queue exactly one trailing call, and never a queue of them
      timer = setTimeout(() => {
        timer = null;
        last = Date.now();
        fn.apply(throttled, lastArgs);
      }, remaining);
    }
  }
  throttled.cancel = () => { if (timer){ clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; } last = 0; };
  return throttled;
}

const onScroll = throttle(() => updateProgress(), 100);
window.addEventListener("scroll", onScroll, { passive: true });
// note the `passive` flag, which is unrelated to throttling and matters more:
// it promises not to call preventDefault, so the browser can scroll without
// waiting for the listener

// rAF throttle, which is the right tool for anything visual
let queued = false;
function onResize(){
  if (queued) return;
  queued = true;
  requestAnimationFrame(() => { queued = false; layout(); });
}
window.addEventListener("resize", onResize);
// it aligns to the display's refresh and cannot run more than once per frame,
// which is exactly the constraint a layout recalculation has. A time-based
// throttle at 16ms is the same idea implemented worse.

// and the one that needs neither, because the browser already coalesces it
input.addEventListener("input", handler);
// typing fires per keystroke by definition, so THIS is where debounce belongs
// the leak, which is the part everyone forgets
function component(){
  const search = debounce(fetchResults, 300);
  input.addEventListener("input", search);
  return () => {
    search.cancel();                  // 1. clear the pending timer
    input.removeEventListener("input", search);   // 2. remove the listener
  };                                 // both, or the handler outlives the DOM
}
// without 1, the timer holds the closure, the closure holds the component's
// state, and the state is retained for 300ms after unmount — which is a leak
// in anything that mounts and unmounts in a loop
// without 2, every mount adds another listener and they all fire

// and the version that cannot leak: an AbortController per call
function debouncedFetch(url, { ms = 250, signal } = {}){
  const ac = new AbortController();
  const timer = setTimeout(() => { ac.abort(); }, ms);
  return fetch(url, { signal: ac.signal })
    .then((r) => r.json())
    .finally(() => clearTimeout(timer));
}
// which handles the network layer rather than the caller, and composes with
// the sequence-number guard from 8.4 for the last-response-wins case

// the three tests worth writing for either one
// 1. calls once after a burst
const d = debounce(() => count++, 100);
d(); d(); d();
await sleep(150);
assert(count === 1);
// 2. cancel suppresses the trailing call
const d2 = debounce(() => count++, 100);
d2(); d2.cancel();
await sleep(150);
assert(count === 0);
// 3. this and arguments survive
const obj = { v: 42, run: debounce(function () { got = this.v; args = [...arguments]; }, 10) };
obj.run(1, 2);
// 4. and that a flush produces the same call the timer would have
const d3 = debounce(() => count++, 100);
d3(); d3.flush();
assert(count === 1);
await sleep(150);
assert(count === 1);   // the timer was cancelled, so it does not double-fire

Debounce is for an event whose final value matters — a search box, a resize, a validation field — because only the last one is worth acting on. Throttle is for an event that must be acted on continuously — a scroll handler, a drag, a progress bar — because dropping the middle of the stream loses information. Using debounce on a scroll handler makes the bar freeze and then jump; using throttle on a search box issues a request per keystroke with a delay the reader notices.

Choosing, in one table

EventUseBecause
Search inputDebounce 250–400 msOnly the final term is worth a request
Form validation on blurNeitherBlur already happens once per field
Form validation on inputDebounce 300 msOr checkValidity natively, which is free
Window resizeDebounce 150 ms, or rAFDo not lay out on every frame of a drag
Scroll positionrAF, or throttle 100 msMark it passive
Infinite scroll fetchNeitherGuard on a flag and a distance, not a timer
AutosaveDebounce 800 ms + beforeunloadIdle time is the signal, not elapsed time
Drag and droprAFIt is already frame-aligned
Button double-clickNeitherDisable the button — clearer for the reader
Mousemove for a tooltiprAF, or throttle 50 msAnything slower than 50 ms reads as lag
// the one that needs neither, because the guard is the point
let fetching = false;
async function onNearBottom(){
  if (fetching) return;                 // one request in flight at a time
  const el = document.querySelector("#list");
  if (el.scrollTop + el.clientHeight < el.scrollHeight - 300) return;
  fetching = true;
  try { await appendPage(nextCursor()); }
  finally { fetching = false; }
}
el.addEventListener("scroll", onNearBottom, { passive: true });

// the pattern for "the reader must not be able to act twice", which is
// different and is better solved with the disabled attribute than with a
// timer at all
async function submit(e){
  const btn = e.target.querySelector("button[type=submit]");
  btn.disabled = true;                  // the honest fix: the control says so
  try { await save(new FormData(e.target)); }
  catch (err) { showError(err); }
  finally {
    btn.disabled = false;
    if (savedSuccessfully) btn.remove();  // and remove it, so it cannot be
  }                                     // re-enabled for a form that is gone
}
// a debounced double-submit guard leaves the button enabled for 300 ms, which
// is exactly long enough for an impatient second click to land before the
// timer fires. Disabling it is both simpler and honest about the state.

// and the general answer to "how often should this run", in order:
//   1. can the browser do it?   <input> autocomplete, CSS scroll-behavior,
//                               native validation, passive listeners
//   2. is it already frame-aligned?  rAF
//   3. does only the last one matter?  debounce
//   4. does each one matter but not this often?  throttle
//   5. is it a state change rather than an event?  disable the control
// ...and a timer is the answer to at most a third of them.

Debounce and throttle are answers to a question most of these events are not asking. A double-clicked submit button is not a rate problem, it is a state problem, and the honest fix is to disable the control — which tells the reader what happened, where a 300 ms window where the button still looks clickable tells them nothing.

Debounce waits for the silence; throttle allows a fixed rate. Both need three things most snippets omit: a leading/trailing choice, a cancel() that is called on unmount — otherwise the pending timer retains the closure — and a flush path for "run it now". For anything visual, prefer a requestAnimationFrame coalescer over a time-based throttle, and mark scroll listeners { passive: true }.

Appendix · Quick Reference & Glossary

A.1 Operators quick referenceA.2 Array methods quick referenceA.3 String methods quick referenceA.4 Promise methods quick referenceA.5 Glossary

Bundles quick-reference tables and a glossary into one place. Skim it whenever you forget an operator, method, or term. The tables group related facts for fast lookup, and the glossary explains each term in a sentence or two.

A.1 Operators quick reference

Lists the operators this guide uses, grouped by purpose, with one-line examples each. Operators appear in groups such as arithmetic, comparison, logical, and assignment, so you can find the one you need. Keep this page open while you write code.

OperatorMeaningExample
=== / !==Strict equality checksa === b
??Nullish coalescing, defaults on null/undefinedx ?? 0
?.Optional chaininguser?.name
&&= / ||= / ??=Logical assignmenta ??= 0
...Spread and rest[...arr], ...args
=>Arrow function shorthand(a, b) => a + b

A.2 Array methods quick reference

Maps the array methods used across the guide to what they return. The return value decides which method fits, whether that is a new array, a single value, a boolean, or an index. Use this reference to choose the right tool for each transformation.

MethodReturnsMutates?
mapNew array of transformed elementsNo
filterNew array of kept elementsNo
reduceSingle accumulated valueNo
findFirst matching element, else undefinedNo
some / everyBooleanNo
sortThe same array, reorderedYes
includesBooleanNo

A.3 String methods quick reference

Lists the most useful string methods and what each returns. Strings are immutable, so these all produce new strings rather than altering the original. Methods like slice, includes, split, replace, and trim cover the most common text-processing needs.

MethodReturns
toUpperCase / toLowerCaseTransformed copy
trimCopy without surrounding whitespace
includes / startsWith / endsWithBoolean
splitArray of pieces
sliceSubstring
replace / replaceAllCopy with replacements

A.4 Promise methods quick reference

Summarizes the promise combinators and timeline methods. Combinators like all, allSettled, race, and any coordinate several promises at once, while then, catch, and finally attach handlers to a single promise. Each method solves a different waiting problem.

MethodBehavior
then / catch / finallyConsume, handle failure, always-run cleanup
Promise.allAll settle; rejects on the first failure
Promise.allSettledNever rejects; reports every outcome
Promise.raceFirst to settle wins
Promise.anyFirst to succeed wins

A.5 Glossary

Defines the core terms of the guide in one or two sentences each, such as closure, coercion, and prototype. Look here when a term resurfaces in a later chapter. Each entry gives a working definition and points your memory at the fuller chapter.

  • Primitive — an immutable single value: number, string, boolean, null, undefined, symbol, or bigint.
  • Coercion — JavaScript converting one type to another automatically, often with surprising results.
  • Closure — a function that keeps access to the variables of the scope where it was created.
  • Hoisting — declarations being available before the line that defines them.
  • Prototype — the hidden object that supplies fallback properties to another object.
  • Destructuring — unpacking objects or arrays into named variables in one expression.
  • Spread — copying the values of an iterable into a new array or object.
  • Event bubbling — an event travelling from its target up through the ancestors.
  • Microtask — a queued callback, such as a promise callback, that runs before the next macrotask.
  • Promise — a placeholder for a value that will be available later.
  • Generator — a function that yields values lazily and can pause and resume.
  • Memoization — caching function results keyed by their input.
